
**OCI Identity and Access Management (IAM):**

**Overview:**

- OCI IAM is a cloud-native service that provides identity and access management features

- Two major components: **Identity** (authentication) and **Access Management** (authorization)

- Controls who has access to what cloud resources using policies as the control mechanism

- When a user attempts an action on a resource, OCI IAM checks associated policies to allow or deny it

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              OCI IAM — Two Core Components                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IDENTITY (Authentication)       │   │  ACCESS MANAGEMENT (Authorization)│
  │                                  │   │                                   │
  │  • Complete identity lifecycle   │   │  • Fine-grained access control    │
  │    management                    │   │  • Role-based access control      │
  │  • Inbound / Outbound / SSO      │   │    (RBAC)                         │
  │  • Integrates with external      │   │  • Permissions defined using      │
  │    identity providers and apps   │   │    IAM policies                   │
  │  • Secure, easy access for       │   │  • Controls what actions users,   │
  │    end users                     │   │    groups, or services can        │
  │                                  │   │    perform on OCI resources       │
  └──────────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Authentication (AuthN):**

- Security mechanism that ensures only authenticated users/entities are granted access to cloud resources

- Confirms the identity of the entity requesting access before allowing any action

**Authentication Methods in OCI IAM:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              OCI IAM Authentication Methods                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Username & Password / Passwordless
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Standard credential-based authentication for console      │
     │  and user access                                           │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. API Keys (Public/Private Key Pair)
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Used when making API calls via SDK or CLI                 │
     │  Public/private key pair signs and authenticates calls     │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. OAuth 2.0 Tokens
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Tokens obtained via OAuth 2.0 for both authentication     │
     │  and authorization; allow applications to access           │
     │  resources on a user's behalf                              │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  4. Instance Principal
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Compute instances securely access OCI resources           │
     │  WITHOUT needing username/password credentials             │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  5. Federated Identity
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Users authenticate via external identity providers        │
     │  that support SAML 2.0 protocol                            │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  6. Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Additional layer of security added on top of any          │
     │  of the above methods                                      │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Authorization (AuthZ):**

- Done using IAM policies — ensures only authorized entities can access and manipulate resources

- Policies define what actions are permitted based on predefined permissions

**Authentication + Authorization Flow:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              IAM AuthN → AuthZ Flow                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

User/Entity attempts action on OCI resource
         │
         ▼
  Step 1: AUTHENTICATION
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Confirm identity (credentials, API key,   │
  │  token, instance principal, federation)    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │  Identity confirmed
         ▼
  Step 2: AUTHORIZATION
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Check IAM policies for permissions        │
  │  Does this user/group have permission      │
  │  to perform this action on this resource?  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │                    │
    Allowed ✓            Denied ✗
         │                    │
         ▼                    ▼
  Action executed       Action denied

Example:
  User-A → stop compute instance  → Allowed (has permission)
  User-A → delete storage bucket  → Denied (no permission)
  User-B → read storage bucket    → Allowed (has permission)
  User-C → manage/modify database → Allowed (full permission)
```

**OCI IAM Key Components:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              OCI IAM Components Overview                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Identity Domain
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Logical container for users, groups, and applications   │
  │  Example: separate domain for production team vs dev team│
  │  Manages authentication and identity lifecycle           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Compartment
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Logical container to isolate and secure OCI resources   │
  │  within a tenancy                                        │
  │  Policies are scoped to tenancy or compartment           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Users
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Individual identities that access OCI resources         │
  │  Created within an identity domain                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Groups
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Collection of users; policies are applied to groups     │
  │  rather than individual users                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Policies
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Define permissions granted to users, groups, or other   │
  │  entities; written using a simple OCI policy syntax      │
  │  Scoped to tenancy or compartment                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Dynamic Groups
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Groups whose membership is determined by matching rules │
  │  (e.g., compute instances in a specific compartment)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Principals
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Any entity that can interact with OCI resources:        │
  │  users, groups, instance principals, dynamic groups      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**RBAC Setup Workflow:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Role-Based Access Control Setup Steps                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Create Identity Domain
         → Logical container for users and groups

Step 2: Create Users within the Identity Domain
         → Individual identities

Step 3: Create Groups and assign Users to Groups
         → Logical grouping of users by role/function

Step 4: Write Policies scoped to tenancy or compartment
         → Define what permissions the group has on resources

Step 5: Resources reside in Compartments
         → Policy specifies permissions for resources in compartment/tenancy
```

**Key Points:**

- OCI IAM has two core functions: **Authentication** (identity verification) and **Authorization** (permissions via policies)

- Authentication methods include: username/password, API keys, OAuth 2.0 tokens, instance principals, federated identity (SAML 2.0), and MFA

- Authorization is managed entirely through IAM policies that define allowed actions per user/group/entity

- Identity Domains are logical containers for users, groups, and applications

- Compartments are logical containers for isolating and securing OCI resources

- Policies are scoped to a tenancy or compartment and written in simple OCI policy syntax

- The typical RBAC setup: create identity domain → create users → create groups → write policies → apply to compartment/tenancy

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Identity Domain:**

**Overview:**

- An identity domain represents a user population in OCI along with their associated configurations and security settings

- It is a self-contained Identity and Access Management service — a container for managing users, roles, federation, and provisioning

- Provides secure application integration through SSO (Single Sign-On) using SAML and OAuth protocols

- Identity domain is a resource in OCI — like any other resource, you can write IAM policies for it and scope it to a compartment

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Identity Domain — What It Contains                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        Identity Domain                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Users       │  │  Groups      │  │  Dynamic Groups        │  │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  OAuth /     │  │  MFA &       │  │  App Gateway /         │  │
  │  │  Federation  │  │  Adaptive    │  │  PAM / RADIUS Proxy    │  │
  │  │  (SAML)      │  │  Security    │  │  Applications          │  │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  User Provisioning — AD Bridge / Provisioning Bridge     │     │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Identity Domain Use Cases:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Example: Three Separate Identity Domains               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Domain 1: Employee Access
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Manage employee access across cloud and on-premises     │
  │  applications; simplify authentication and authorization │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Domain 2: Business Partners
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Provide access to supply chain and ordering systems     │
  │  for external partners; isolated user population with    │
  │  its own authentication and access policies              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Domain 3: Consumer Identities
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Manage consumer users accessing public-facing apps;     │
  │  separate domain with custom authentication settings     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Four Core Capabilities of OCI IAM with Identity Domain:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Four Core Capabilities                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Inbound Authentication & SSO
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  How users are authenticated INTO the service              │
     │  • Social logon and federated logons                       │
     │  • AD integration (without moving AD credentials to cloud) │
     │  • Delegated authentication between AD and IAM (AD Bridge) │
     │  • MFA and adaptive security on top                        │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. Identity Store & Lifecycle Management
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  How users are onboarded and managed in OCI IAM            │
     │  • AD Bridge and Provisioning Bridge                       │
     │  • Console, CLI, or API onboarding                         │
     │  • Application Catalog (400+ preconfigured templates)      │
     │  • Manage: user roles, group membership, permissions       │
     │  • Provisioning/de-provisioning, account synchronization   │
     │  • Security policy configuration                           │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. Outbound Authentication & SSO
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  After identity confirmed → enable SSO to target apps      │
     │  Target applications can be:                               │
     │  • SaaS applications                                       │
     │  • Enterprise applications (on-premises or cloud-hosted)   │
     │  • Linux hosts                                             │
     │  • VPN clients                                             │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  4. OCI Authorization using Access Policies
     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Manage access to OCI resources via IAM policies           │
     │  • Controls access to compute, storage, networking, etc.   │
     │  • Policies scoped to tenancy or compartment               │
     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Directory Integration & User Onboarding:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              User Onboarding Methods                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AD Bridge        → Sync users/groups from Microsoft AD     │
  │                     Supports delegated authentication        │
  │  Prov. Bridge     → Sync from any other external directory   │
  │  Console/CLI/API  → Manual or automated onboarding          │
  │  App Catalog      → 400+ preconfigured app templates         │
  │  SAML / OIDC      → Standard protocol integrations          │
  │  Proxy Gateways   → Extend to SaaS, PaaS, on-premises apps  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**MFA Methods Supported:**

- Email passcodes

- Mobile application passcodes (authenticator app)

- Security questions

- Passwordless authentication

**Key Points:**

- An identity domain is a self-contained IAM container representing a user population with its own configurations and security settings

- Supports inbound federation (SAML 2.0) and application SSO (OAuth, SAML, OpenID Connect)

- Four core capabilities: Inbound Authentication & SSO, Identity Store & Lifecycle Management, Outbound Authentication & SSO, OCI Authorization via Access Policies

- Users can be onboarded via AD Bridge, Provisioning Bridge, console/CLI/API, or Application Catalog (400+ templates)

- Identity domains are OCI resources — IAM policies can be written for them and scoped to a compartment

- Multiple identity domains can co-exist for different populations (employees, partners, consumers) each with independent configurations

- Supports MFA and adaptive security for stronger identity verification

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Identity Domain Types:**

**Overview:**

- When you create an OCI account, a default identity domain is included automatically (Free domain type)

- Different domain types exist to support different use cases — from basic OCI access to enterprise workforce and consumer identity management

- Refer to official OCI documentation for the latest feature lists and limits per domain type

**Five Identity Domain Types:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Identity Domain Types Comparison                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. FREE (Default)
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Included automatically when OCI account is created        │
   │  • Called the "default identity domain"                      │
   │  • Used to manage access to all OCI resources                │
   │  • Supports various authentication methods                   │
   │  • Limit: 2,000 users                                        │
   │  • NOT suitable for enterprise-level workforce access mgmt   │
   │  • Good for: basic OCI resource access, no app integrations  │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. ORACLE APPS
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Tied to specific Oracle service purchases                 │
   │  • Automatically included when purchasing:                   │
   │    - Oracle SaaS applications                                │
   │    - Oracle PaaS applications                                │
   │    - GBU (Global Business Unit) applications                 │
   │    - VBCs (Visual Builder Cloud Service)                     │
   │  • Customer CANNOT manually select or create this type       │
   │  • Managed by Oracle service teams                           │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. ORACLE APPS PREMIUM
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Can be selected manually from the console                 │
   │  • Supports hybrid IAM scenarios:                            │
   │    - Proxies, Gateways, Bridges                              │
   │    - Extends IAM to on-premises or OCI-hosted Oracle apps    │
   │  • Compatible Oracle apps: E-Business Suite, PeopleSoft,     │
   │    Oracle Database, and others                               │
   │  • Can add non-Oracle applications                           │
   │  • Limit: Maximum 6 non-Oracle applications                  │
   │  • For: Managing Oracle app identities across hybrid env.    │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. PREMIUM
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Fully loaded — ALL features, NO restrictions              │
   │  • High limits on users, groups, and applications            │
   │    (designed for enterprise scale)                           │
   │  • Supports hybrid scenarios (on-premises fully supported)   │
   │  • Use when: Oracle is your full IAM provider                │
   │  • Suitable for enterprise-level workforce access management │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5. EXTERNAL USER
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Designed for non-employees and consumer use cases         │
   │  • Use cases:                                                │
   │    - Contractors needing access                              │
   │    - Consumer-facing applications at large scale             │
   │  • Features: Social logon, self-service registration,        │
   │    password management                                       │
   │  • Supports hybrid features                                  │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Domain Type Selection Guide:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Scenario                              │  Recommended Domain Type    │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Basic OCI resource access only        │  Free (default)             │
│  Oracle SaaS/PaaS purchase included    │  Oracle Apps (auto)         │
│  Oracle on-prem apps + hybrid IAM      │  Oracle Apps Premium        │
│  Full enterprise IAM, all features     │  Premium                    │
│  Contractors / consumer app users      │  External User              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Upgrade Path:**

```
  Default (Free)
       │
       ├──▶ Premium          (if you need full enterprise features)
       │
       └──▶ External User    (if you need consumer/contractor access)
```

**Key Points:**

- Every OCI account starts with a Free default identity domain (limit: 2,000 users)

- Oracle Apps domain type is automatically assigned with Oracle SaaS/PaaS/GBU purchases — cannot be manually created

- Oracle Apps Premium can be selected manually and supports hybrid IAM with proxies, gateways, and bridges (max 6 non-Oracle apps)

- Premium domain type provides all features with high limits — suitable for full enterprise workforce IAM

- External User domain type is for non-employees and consumer-facing applications; supports social logon and self-service

- Always refer to official OCI documentation for the most current feature lists and limits per domain type

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Default Domain:**

**Overview:**

- When you create an OCI tenancy, the first identity domain — called the **default domain** — is created automatically

- It is a container for users, groups, roles, application integrations, and federation (SSO, SAML, OAuth)

- Created in the **root compartment** of the tenancy

- Can be used for any users, groups, or applications you want to create or integrate in the root compartment

**Default Domain — Key Characteristics:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Default Domain Properties                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✗  Cannot be deactivated or deleted                         │
  │     (created as part of tenancy setup)                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✗  Cannot hide the default domain option from Sign In page  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✓  Always replicated to ALL regions the tenancy subscribes  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✓  Located in the root compartment                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Pre-Created Resources in Default Domain:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Auto-Created in Default Domain                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Users:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1 Default Admin User                                        │
  │  • First IAM user for the tenancy                            │
  │  • Responsible for initial setup of additional admins        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Groups:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. All Domain Users group                                   │
  │     • All users in the domain automatically belong here      │
  │                                                              │
  │  2. Administrators group                                     │
  │     • Admin/super user is a member of this group             │
  │     • CANNOT be deleted                                      │
  │     • Must always contain at least one user                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Policy:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Auto-created policy grants Administrators group full access │
  │  to ALL OCI cloud resources                                  │
  │  • CANNOT be updated or deleted                              │
  │  • Any user added to Administrators group gets full access   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Default Admin User — Responsibilities:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Admin User Responsibilities                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✓ Assign roles to manage users, groups, applications, system
    configuration, and security settings

  ✓ Perform delegated administration — assign users to different
    administrative roles and domains:
    • Domain Administrator
    • Security Administrator

  ✓ Enable Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA) and configure
    authentication factors

  ✓ Create self-registration profiles for different sets of users

  ✓ Manage approvals for policies and applications
```

**Do's and Don'ts for Admin Account:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Admin Account Best Practices                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DO:                                  DON'T:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐       ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✓ Create separate IAM     │       │  ✗ Add more users to the   │
  │    users with appropriate  │       │    Administrators group     │
  │    permissions             │       │    unnecessarily            │
  │                            │       │                            │
  │  ✓ Create specific roles   │       │  ✗ Use admin/super user    │
  │    and responsibilities    │       │    account for day-to-day  │
  │    for each user           │       │    routine tasks            │
  │                            │       │                            │
  │  ✓ Use multiple identity   │       │  ✗ Store all users and     │
  │    domains for isolation   │       │    groups only in root      │
  │    of users and admin      │       │    compartment / default   │
  │    control                 │       │    domain                   │
  └────────────────────────────┘       └────────────────────────────┘
```

**Default Domain Structure:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Default Domain in Root Compartment                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  OCI Tenancy (Root Compartment)
  └── Default Identity Domain
        ├── Users
        │     └── Default Admin User (first IAM user)
        ├── Groups
        │     ├── All Domain Users
        │     └── Administrators (cannot delete; min 1 user)
        └── Policy
              └── Admins → Full access to all OCI resources
                           (cannot update or delete)
```

**Key Points:**

- The default domain is automatically created in the root compartment when a tenancy is provisioned

- It cannot be deactivated, deleted, or hidden from the Sign In page

- It replicates to all regions subscribed by the tenancy

- Pre-created resources: 1 admin user, 2 groups (All Domain Users, Administrators), and 1 undeletable policy

- The Administrators group cannot be deleted and must always have at least one user

- Any user added to Administrators group automatically gets full access to all OCI services

- The admin user should be used for initial setup only — not for day-to-day tasks

- Use multiple identity domains to maintain isolation of users and administrative control across teams

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Creating Identity Domains:**

**Why Create Multiple Identity Domains:**

- Even though a default domain exists, multiple domains provide isolation, security, and organizational structure for complex environments

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Reasons to Create Multiple Identity Domains            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Isolation of Administrative Control
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Multiple domains act as containers separating teams   │
     │    and departments                                       │
     │  • Prevents unauthorized access or interference between  │
     │    different groups                                      │
     │  • Example: Production domain vs Development domain      │
     │    → separate users + separate applications per domain   │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. Different Security Policies per Domain
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Apply separate security policies, access controls,    │
     │    and compliance rules to each domain                   │
     │  • Example: Production domain users have stricter        │
     │    security settings than development domain users       │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. Simplified Identity Management
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Structured way to organize and manage identities at   │
     │    large scale                                           │
     │  • Think of it like folders on a computer desktop —      │
     │    keeps everything organized and easy to find           │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  4. Granular Administrative Control
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Each domain can have its own set of administrators    │
     │  • Admins manage users, applications, and policies       │
     │    within their specific domain only                     │
     │  • Essential for large organizations                     │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Example: Production vs Development Domains:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Multiple Domain Example                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  OCI Tenancy
  ├── Default Domain (root)
  │
  ├── Production Domain
  │     ├── Users (prod team)
  │     ├── Groups
  │     ├── Applications (prod-integrated apps)
  │     └── Security Policies (strict settings)
  │
  └── Development Domain
        ├── Users (dev team)
        ├── Groups
        ├── Applications (dev-integrated apps)
        └── Security Policies (relaxed settings)
```

**Auto-Created Resources When Creating a New Identity Domain:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Auto-Created with Each New Domain                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1 Default Administrator Group                               │
  │  1 Domain Admin User (domain administrator)                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Domain Admin can:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✓ Manage users, groups, and applications within the domain  │
  │  ✓ Assign administrative roles to other users:               │
  │      - Security Admin                                        │
  │      - Application Admin (handles app integrations)          │
  │  ✓ Configure MFA settings                                    │
  │  ✓ Configure password and sign-in policies                   │
  │  ✓ Create self-registration profiles for consumer users      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Home Region and Replication:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Home Region Rules for Identity Domains                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Home Region
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • The selected region in the OCI console at the time of     │
  │    domain creation becomes the domain's HOME REGION          │
  │                                                              │
  │  Example:                                                    │
  │  Console region = US East (Ashburn)                          │
  │  → Create identity domain                                    │
  │  → Ashburn becomes the home region for that domain           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Replication (IMPORTANT difference from default domain)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Default Domain:                                             │
  │  ✓ Automatically replicated to ALL subscribed regions        │
  │                                                              │
  │  Additional (non-default) Domains:                           │
  │  ✗ NOT automatically replicated to other regions             │
  │  → Must MANUALLY enable replication for each region          │
  │  → Required if users in the domain need to access OCI        │
  │    resources in other regions                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Multiple identity domains provide isolation of administrative control, different security policies, simplified management, and granular admin control

- Creating a new identity domain automatically creates one administrator group and one domain admin user

- The domain admin manages users, groups, and applications within their domain; can assign sub-roles (Security Admin, App Admin)

- The region selected in the OCI console when creating the domain becomes that domain's home region

- Unlike the default domain, additional identity domains are NOT automatically replicated to all regions — replication must be manually enabled per region

- Enable region replication for a domain when users need to interact with OCI resources in other regions

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Groups:**

**Overview:**

- Groups are a collection of users who share common roles, permissions, and access policies

- Permissions are granted to groups (not individual users), simplifying access management at scale

- Groups make it easy to track and audit who has access to specific resources — essential for security and compliance

**Why Use Groups:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Benefits of Using Groups                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Simplified Access Management
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Grant permissions to a group instead of each individual │
     │  user — one policy change affects all members at once    │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. Organized by Role/Function
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Example groups:                                         │
     │  • Network Admin Group — manages network resources       │
     │  • Instance Admin Group — manages compute instances      │
     │  • Certificate Managers — manages certificates           │
     │  • Engineering, Marketing, Finance groups                │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. Security & Compliance Auditing
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Easily see which group has access to which resources    │
     │  Identify potential security risks quickly               │
     │  Supports compliance reporting and access reviews        │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**How Groups Work with Domains:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Group Setup Workflow                                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Domain Admin creates groups aligned to organization structure
         Examples: Engineering, Marketing, Finance, Network Admins

Step 2: Domain Admin adds users to groups based on their roles
         Users with similar responsibilities are grouped together

Step 3: Policies are set to define what each group can do
         (Policies and Users covered in upcoming lessons)
```

**Two Default Groups in Every Identity Domain:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Default Groups (Auto-Created by IAM)                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Domain Administrators
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Created during domain creation                        │
     │  • Admin user (if created with domain) is added here     │
     │  • CANNOT be deleted                                     │
     │  • Must always have at least one user                    │
     │  ⚠️ Any user added to this group gets FULL CONTROL over  │
     │     all capabilities of that identity domain             │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. All Domain Users
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Every newly created user is automatically added here  │
     │  • Does NOT appear in the Groups tab                     │
     │  • Created by IAM — cannot be deleted by administrator   │
     │  • If you assign an application to this group, ALL       │
     │    users in the domain can access that application        │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Groups Summary Table:**

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Group                │  Auto-Added Users    │  Deletable │  Visible   │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Domain Administrators│  Domain admin user   │  No        │  Yes       │
│  All Domain Users     │  Every new user      │  No        │  No (hidden│
│                       │                      │            │  in Groups │
│                       │                      │            │  tab)      │
│  Custom Groups        │  Manually by admin   │  Yes       │  Yes       │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Groups are collections of users sharing common roles, permissions, and access policies

- Permissions are assigned to groups, not individual users — simplifies management and auditing

- Two default groups exist in every identity domain: **Domain Administrators** and **All Domain Users**

- Domain Administrators group cannot be deleted and must always contain at least one user — any member has full domain control

- All Domain Users group is auto-assigned to every new user, is hidden from the Groups tab, and cannot be deleted

- Assigning an application to the All Domain Users group gives all users in the domain access to that application

- Additional custom groups can be created beyond the two defaults as needed

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Managing Users:**

**Overview:**

- Users are the individual identities that access OCI resources and applications

- Understanding the user lifecycle, who can manage users, and how to onboard them is essential for IAM administration

**User Lifecycle Stages:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              User Lifecycle                                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌───────────────┐
  │ Non-Existent  │  User profile not yet created
  └───────┬───────┘
          │ (create user account)
          ▼
  ┌───────────────┐
  │   Activated   │  Active access to OCI resources and applications
  │               │  based on assigned permissions
  │               │  Can: modify permissions, change groups,
  │               │        assign/remove admin role privileges
  └───────┬───────┘
          │                          │
          │ (sabbatical / temp       │ (user retires
          │  restrict access)        │  or leaves)
          ▼                          ▼
  ┌───────────────┐          ┌───────────────┐
  │  Deactivated  │          │    Deleted    │
  │               │          │               │
  │  Cannot sign  │          │  All groups,  │
  │  in or use    │          │  applications,│
  │  OCI resources│          │  and admin    │
  │               │          │  roles revoked│
  └───────┬───────┘          └───────────────┘
          │ (reactivate)
          ▼
  ┌───────────────┐
  │   Activated   │  Access restored
  └───────────────┘

Note: Can also create a user directly in Deactivated state
      (e.g., when joining date is in the future)
```

**Who Can Manage Users:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              User Management Permissions                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Tenancy Admin
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Permission to manage ALL resources in the tenancy     │
     │  • Can manage users, groups, and all identity components │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. Domain Admin
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Member of the Domain Administrators group             │
     │  • Can manage all capabilities within that domain,       │
     │    including users and groups                            │
     │  • Can assign admin roles to other users                 │
     │    (e.g., User Manager Admin role)                       │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. User Manager (via Admin Role Assignment)
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Domain Admin assigns User Manager Admin role          │
     │  • That user can then manage users and groups within     │
     │    the domain                                            │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  4. Any User with Policy Permission
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • IAM policy grants user/group permission to manage     │
     │    users                                                 │
     │  • Example policy syntax:                                │
     │    Allow group <GroupName> to manage users in tenancy    │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**User Onboarding Methods:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Methods to Onboard Users to OCI IAM                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. OCI Console or API
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Quick creation of individual users                    │
     │  • Best for small number of users                        │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. CSV Import (Console)
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Bulk import of large number of users and groups       │
     │  • Efficient for batch onboarding                        │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. IAM Self-Registration
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Self-service capability for consumer users            │
     │  • Users register themselves via a self-registration     │
     │    profile                                               │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  4. App Catalog (External Source Automation)
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Bring users from external applications automatically  │
     │  • Supported sources:                                    │
     │    - Oracle HCM (Human Capital Management)               │
     │    - Oracle Unified Directory                            │
     │    - Oracle Internet Directory                           │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  5. SCIM APIs (Cross-Domain Identity Management)
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Use SCIM APIs for automated user lifecycle management │
     │  • Supports cross-domain identity management             │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  6. AD Bridge / Provisioning Bridge (On-Premises)
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Software components installed locally on-premises     │
     │  • AD Bridge: Sync users from Microsoft Active Directory │
     │  • Provisioning Bridge: Sync from other directories      │
     │  • Both sync and provision users into OCI IAM            │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  7. Just-In-Time (JIT) Provisioning
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Used for federated identity scenarios                 │
     │  • User account auto-created in IAM on first login       │
     │    via federation                                        │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Onboarding Method Selection Guide:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Scenario                              │  Recommended Method         │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Few users, quick setup                │  Console / API              │
│  Many users at once                    │  CSV Import                 │
│  Consumer self-service                 │  IAM Self-Registration      │
│  From Oracle HCM / Directory           │  App Catalog                │
│  Automated lifecycle management        │  SCIM APIs                  │
│  From on-premises Active Directory     │  AD Bridge                  │
│  From other on-prem directories        │  Provisioning Bridge        │
│  Federation-based onboarding           │  JIT Provisioning           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- User lifecycle stages: Non-Existent → Activated → Deactivated → Deleted (can also be created directly as deactivated)

- Deactivated users cannot sign in or access OCI resources; accounts can be reactivated

- Deleted users have all group memberships, application access, and admin roles revoked automatically

- Four types of entities can manage users: Tenancy Admin, Domain Admin, User Manager (via role), users with IAM policy permission

- Seven onboarding methods: Console/API, CSV import, self-registration, App Catalog, SCIM APIs, AD Bridge, JIT provisioning

- AD Bridge and Provisioning Bridge are installed on-premises to sync users from AD or other directories into OCI IAM

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Administrator Roles:**

**Overview:**

- When an identity domain is created, a domain admin (superuser) is also created with full privileges over the domain

- Domain admin can delegate specific responsibilities to other users via predefined administrator roles

- Administrator roles come with a broad set of predefined permissions — no need to write complex policy statements

- Roles apply within the identity domain only — they do NOT extend to compartments (that is handled by IAM policies)

**Domain Admin Capabilities (Superuser):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Domain Admin — Full Superuser Privileges               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✓ Manage users, groups, and group memberships
  ✓ Manage applications and integrations
  ✓ Manage system configuration and security settings
  ✓ Manage audit and notifications
  ✓ Delegate admin roles to other users within the domain
```

**Predefined Administrator Roles:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Administrator Roles and Permissions                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Identity Domain Administrator
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Superuser with access to ALL domain capabilities      │
     │  • Responsible for delegating roles to other users       │
     │  • Equivalent to the domain admin role                   │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. Security Administrator
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Manage system settings and security configurations    │
     │  • Manage password policies and sign-in policies         │
     │  • Configure Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)           │
     │  • Manage identity providers in the domain               │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. Application Administrator
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Create, update, and manage applications               │
     │  • Grant or revoke application access for users/groups   │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  4. User Administrator
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Full user and group management within the domain      │
     │  • Create and delete users and groups                    │
     │  • Manage group memberships and all related capabilities │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  5. User Manager
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Limited permissions compared to User Administrator    │
     │  • Can be assigned to manage ALL users or users from     │
     │    a specific group only                                 │
     │  • Handles: account updates, activations, deactivations, │
     │    password resets, and more                             │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  6. Help Desk Administrator
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Manage and unlock user accounts                       │
     │  • Handle password resets                                │
     │  • Manage authentication factors and bypass codes        │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  7. Audit Administrator
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  • Generate and run reports for the identity domain      │
     │  • Track and monitor various activities within the domain│
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Administrator Roles Summary Table:**

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Role                    │  Key Permissions                                │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Identity Domain Admin   │  All capabilities + role delegation             │
│  Security Admin          │  Security config, MFA, password/sign-in policy  │
│  Application Admin       │  App management, grant/revoke app access        │
│  User Administrator      │  Full user/group management                     │
│  User Manager            │  Limited user management (scoped by group)      │
│  Help Desk Administrator │  Unlock accounts, password reset, bypass codes  │
│  Audit Administrator     │  Reports and activity tracking                  │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Differences: Admin Roles vs IAM Policies:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Administrator Roles          │  IAM Policies                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Predefined, no policy syntax │  Custom policy syntax required       │
│  Scoped to identity domain    │  Scoped to tenancy/compartment       │
│  Manage identity components   │  Manage OCI cloud resources          │
│  Do NOT extend to compartments│  Extend to OCI resource plane        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Administrator roles allow domain admins to delegate specific responsibilities without writing complex policies

- Seven predefined roles: Identity Domain Admin, Security Admin, Application Admin, User Administrator, User Manager, Help Desk Admin, Audit Admin

- User Administrator has full user/group management; User Manager has limited/scoped permissions

- Administrator roles are scoped to the identity domain only — they do NOT extend to OCI compartments

- Using predefined roles aligns with cloud security best practices and simplifies IAM governance

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Policies:**

**Overview:**

- Policies provide authorization — granular permissions for users to access OCI resources and services

- Authorization controls: what permissions a user has, what actions they can perform, and on which resources

- Used to implement Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) in OCI

- **Default behavior: everything is DENIED** — policies only allow; there is no "deny" policy in OCI

- Always follow the principle of **least privilege** — grant only the minimum permissions needed

**Policy Syntax:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              OCI Policy Syntax                                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Allow <subject> to <verb> <resource-type> in <location> [where <condition>]

  Four key components:
  ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Subject     │  WHO is being allowed (group, dynamic group)   │
  │  Action      │  WHAT they can do (verb + resource type)       │
  │  Location    │  WHERE the permission applies (scope)          │
  │  Condition   │  Optional — for more granular control          │
  └──────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Subject Clause:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Subject Clause Options                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Group (recommended — always prefix domain name):
  Allow group <domain-name>/<group-name> to ...

  Example (production domain, network admin group):
  Allow group production/network-admins to manage virtual-network-family in tenancy

  Dynamic Group:
  Allow dynamic-group <domain-name>/<dynamic-group-name> to ...

  Using OCID instead of name:
  Allow group <ocid> to ...
  Allow dynamic-group <ocid> to ...

  Any user in tenancy:
  Allow any-user to ...

  Multiple groups (chaining):
  Allow group domain/A-admin, domain/B-admin to ...
  (Single policy applies to multiple groups)

  ⚠️ Important: When no domain is prefixed, the DEFAULT domain is implied.
     Always prefix the domain name (even "default") for clarity:
     Allow group default/network-admins to ...
```

**Action Clause — Verbs:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Verb Hierarchy (access level increases downward)       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  inspect   │  Observe and monitor only; no data access       │
  │            │  Example: inspect objects → see object exists,  │
  │            │  count, metadata — NOT the content              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  read      │  Basic access to resource content               │
  │            │  Example: read objects → read the object content│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  use       │  Modify/use existing resources                  │
  │            │  Example: use objects → re-encrypt an object    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  manage    │  Full control — create, delete, modify anything │
  │            │  Example: manage objects → create/delete objects│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Verbs are consistent across ALL resource types in OCI
```

**Action Clause — Resource Types:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Resource Type Hierarchy                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Level 1: All Resources (broadest)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  all-resources                                               │
  │  Covers EVERY resource in OCI — including new services added │
  │  Example: manage all-resources in tenancy                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Level 2: Aggregate Resource Type (family level)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  virtual-network-family  (all networking components)         │
  │  instance-family         (all compute components)            │
  │  object-family           (all object storage components)     │
  │  volume-family           (all block volume components)       │
  │  database-family         (all database components)           │
  │  Example: manage virtual-network-family in compartment X     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Level 3: Individual Resource Type (most granular)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  vcns, subnets, instances, db-nodes, objects, buckets, etc.  │
  │  Example: manage vcns in compartment X                       │
  │           use subnets in compartment X                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ IMPORTANT: Identity resources have NO aggregate resource type.
     Must always write policies at individual resource type level:
     manage users, manage groups, manage compartments, etc.
```

**Location (Placement) Clause:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Location Options                                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Entire tenancy:
  ... in tenancy

  Specific compartment (by name):
  ... in compartment <compartment-name>

  Specific compartment (by OCID):
  ... in compartment id <compartment-ocid>

  Example:
  Allow group production/network-admins to manage virtual-network-family
  in compartment sandbox
```

**Complete Policy Examples:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Policy Examples                                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Network admins manage all networking in a compartment:
     Allow group default/network-admins to manage virtual-network-family
     in compartment sandbox

  2. Storage readers can read objects in tenancy:
     Allow group default/storage-readers to read objects in tenancy

  3. Compute instance calls Object Storage (dynamic group):
     Allow dynamic-group default/my-instances to manage objects
     in compartment data-compartment

  4. Multiple groups — same permission:
     Allow group default/A-admin, default/B-admin to manage users
     in tenancy

  5. Any user can inspect instances in tenancy:
     Allow any-user to inspect instances in tenancy
```

**Resource Type Examples per Verb:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Verb     │  Object Storage Example   │  What It Means               │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  inspect  │  inspect objects          │  See metadata, count, exists  │
│  read     │  read objects             │  Read the content             │
│  use      │  use objects              │  Re-encrypt with different key│
│  manage   │  manage objects           │  Create, delete, modify       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Default is DENY — policies only grant permissions; there is no explicit deny policy in OCI

- Always follow the principle of least privilege — grant only what is needed

- Policy syntax: `Allow <subject> to <verb> <resource-type> in <location> [where <condition>]`

- Subject can be: group, dynamic group, OCID, any-user, or chained multiple groups

- Always prefix domain name in group references — omitting it implies the default domain

- Four verb levels (inspect → read → use → manage) — consistent across all OCI resource types

- Three resource type levels: `all-resources` → aggregate family → individual resource type

- **Identity resources have NO aggregate type** — must use individual resource types (users, groups, compartments, etc.)

- Location scopes permission to the entire tenancy or a specific compartment (by name or OCID)

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Compartments:**

**Overview:**

- Compartments are logical containers used to organize, isolate, and implement access control for OCI resources

- When you create an OCI account you get a **tenancy** — also called the **root compartment** (the top-level container)

- You can create sub-compartments within the root compartment to organize resources by function, project, team, or region

- Best practice: always create dedicated compartments rather than placing everything in the root compartment

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Compartment Structure (Analogy)                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Computer Folders          OCI Compartments
  ┌─────────────┐           ┌─────────────────────┐
  │  Main Folder│           │  Tenancy (Root)      │
  │  (root)     │    ≈      │  Compartment         │
  │  ├── docs/  │           │  ├── Network Compartment
  │  ├── imgs/  │           │  │     VCN, Load Balancer
  │  └── vids/  │           │  └── Storage Compartment
  └─────────────┘           │        Block Storage, File Storage
                            └─────────────────────┘
```

**Compartment Features:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              1. Single Compartment per Resource                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • Each resource belongs to exactly ONE compartment at a time
  • A resource CANNOT exist in multiple compartments simultaneously
  • Example: A VM placed in Compartment A cannot also be in Compartment B

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              2. Access Control via Policies                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • Policies scope user permissions to a specific compartment
  • Example:
    Allow group default/storage-admins to manage block-storage-family
    in compartment storage-compartment
  • Users can only act on resources within the compartment specified
    in the policy

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              3. Cross-Compartment Resource Interaction              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • Resources in DIFFERENT compartments can still interact
  • Example: VM in Compute Compartment can use VCN in Network Compartment
  • No requirement for resources to be in the same compartment
    to work together

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              4. Resource Movement Between Compartments              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • Resources can be moved from one compartment to another
  • Example: Move a VM from Compartment A → Compartment B as needed

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              5. Compartments are GLOBAL                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • A compartment created in the home region (e.g., Phoenix) is
    automatically available in ALL subscribed regions
  • Resources from multiple regions can be placed in the same compartment
  • Example:
    Instance in Phoenix + Instance in Lisbon → both in Compartment A

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              6. Nested Compartments (up to 6 levels deep)           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • Compartments can be nested to create hierarchical structures
  • Maximum depth: 6 levels
  • Enables mapping logical hierarchy to organizational/project structure

  Example:
  Tenancy (Root)
  └── Project Compartment
        ├── Team A Sub-Compartment
        │     ├── Dev Sub-Sub-Compartment
        │     └── Test Sub-Sub-Compartment
        └── Team B Sub-Compartment
              └── Production Sub-Sub-Compartment
```

**Compartment Organization Examples:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Common Compartment Strategies                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  By Function:                  By Team/Project:
  ┌─────────────────┐           ┌─────────────────┐
  │  Root           │           │  Root           │
  │  ├── Network    │           │  ├── Production │
  │  ├── Compute    │           │  └── Development│
  │  ├── Storage    │           └─────────────────┘
  │  └── Database   │
  └─────────────────┘
```

**Access Control with Compartments:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              How Policies Use Compartments                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  User → Group → Policy → Compartment → Resources

  Example:
  Allow group default/network-admins to manage virtual-network-family
  in compartment network-compartment
  → Network admins can ONLY access networking resources in that compartment
  → They CANNOT access resources in storage-compartment or compute-compartment
```

**Key Points:**

- The tenancy itself is the root compartment — the top-level container for all OCI resources

- Compartments are logical containers; best practice is to create dedicated compartments rather than using only root

- Each resource belongs to exactly one compartment and cannot exist in multiple simultaneously

- Resources in different compartments can still interact with each other (no co-location required)

- Resources can be moved between compartments

- Compartments are global — available across all subscribed regions; resources from multiple regions can share a compartment

- Compartments support nesting up to 6 levels deep for complex organizational hierarchies

- Policies scoped to a compartment restrict user actions to resources within that compartment only

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Compartment Quotas:**

**Overview:**

- Compartment quotas allow administrators to control and limit resource consumption within compartments

- Useful for ensuring fair distribution of high-value cloud resources across teams/departments

- Quota limits are set using **quota policies** (separate from IAM authorization policies)

- Spending budgets can also be set on compartments to control cloud costs

**Quota Policy Syntax:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Quota Policy Syntax                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  <action> <service-family> quota <quota-name> <count>
  in <tenancy | compartment <compartment-name>>

  Three Action Keywords:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  set    → Set a specific quota limit (numerical value)       │
  │  unset  → Reset quota back to the default service limit      │
  │  zero   → Set quota to 0 (removes access to the resource)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Quota Policy Examples:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Quota Policy Examples                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Limit VCNs to 4 in production compartment:
     set vcn quota vcn-count to 4 in compartment production

  2. Limit Exadata to 1 across the entire tenancy:
     set database quota /* to 1 in tenancy

  3. Limit VM Standard 2 / BM Standard 2 to 10 cores in tenancy:
     set compute-core quota standard2-core-count to 10 in tenancy

  4. Wildcard — apply quota to ALL resources in a service family:
     zero database quota * in tenancy
     (sets ALL database resources to 0 across tenancy)

  5. Exception pattern — block all, allow for specific compartment:
     zero database quota * in tenancy
     unset database quota * in compartment production
     → Exadata blocked for all; production compartment gets full default limit
```

**Three Quota Action Keywords:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Quota Action Keyword Details                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  set
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Sets a specific numerical quota limit for a resource        │
  │  Example: set vcn quota vcn-count to 4 in compartment prod   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  unset
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Resets quota back to the DEFAULT service limit of tenancy   │
  │  Effectively allocates full default capacity to compartment  │
  │  Example: unset database quota * in compartment production   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  zero
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Sets quota to 0 — removes all access to the resource        │
  │  in the specified scope                                      │
  │  Example: zero database quota * in tenancy                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Block All + Exception Pattern:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Use Case: Block resource tenancy-wide, allow in prod   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Scenario: Only production compartment should be allowed to use Exadata

  Step 1: Block all Exadata across entire tenancy
          zero database quota * in tenancy

  Step 2: Create exception — allow full default limit in production
          unset database quota * in compartment production

  Result:
  • All departments: zero Exadata quota
  • Production compartment: default service limit for Exadata restored
```

**Budgets (Spending Limits):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Compartment Budgets                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  • Set a monthly spending limit (budget) on any compartment
  • Define a threshold percentage (e.g., 80%)
  • Track actual spending vs. budget
  • Receive alerts when spending reaches the defined threshold

  Example:
  Budget: $2,000/month for production compartment
  Threshold: 80% ($1,600)
  → Alert triggered when spending reaches $1,600
```

**Key Points:**

- Compartment quotas use quota policies (not IAM authorization policies) to limit resource consumption per compartment

- Three quota actions: `set` (specific limit), `unset` (reset to default service limit), `zero` (remove all access)

- Use wildcard `*` in quota name to apply the action to ALL resources within a service family

- Common pattern: `zero` quota tenancy-wide + `unset` for specific compartment = block all except one department

- Quota names and service family names are resource-specific — refer to OCI documentation for correct values

- Budgets allow spending limits on compartments with threshold alerts to control cloud costs

================================================================================

**OCI IAM Policy Attachment and Policy Inheritance:**

**Overview:**

- **Policy Inheritance:** Compartments inherit policies from their parent compartment — permissions flow downstream
- **Policy Attachment:** Where a policy is attached determines who can manage (modify/delete) that policy

**Policy Inheritance:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Policy Inheritance — Permissions Flow Downstream       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Root Compartment (Tenancy)
  │  Policy: Allow group Administrators to manage all-resources in tenancy
  │  (standard admin policy — cannot be edited or deleted)
  │  (no domain prefix = default domain implied)
  │
  └── Compartment A
        │  Admin group can manage resources here (inherited from root)
        │
        └── Compartment B
              │  Admin group can manage resources here (inherited from A)
              │
              └── Compartment C
                    Admin group can manage resources here (inherited from B)

  Rule: A policy written for a parent compartment applies to ALL
        nested child compartments below it (inheritance flows downstream)
```

**Inheritance Example:**

```
  Policy written for Compartment A:
  Allow group Domain1/NetworkAdmin to manage virtual-network-family
  in compartment A

  Effect:
  ✓ NetworkAdmin can manage VCNs in Compartment A
  ✓ NetworkAdmin can manage VCNs in Compartment B (child of A)
  ✓ NetworkAdmin can manage VCNs in Compartment C (child of B)
  → Permissions flow downstream automatically
```

**Policy Attachment:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Where You Attach a Policy = Who Can Manage It          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Root Compartment
  │  Policy 1 (attached here)
  │  → Anyone with "manage policies" in tenancy can update/delete it
  │
  └── Parent Compartment
        │  Policy 2 (attached here)
        │  → Anyone with "manage policies" in parent can update/delete it
        │
        └── Child Compartment
              Policy 3 (attached here)
              → Anyone with "manage policies" in child can update/delete it

  Key Rule: A user with policy management permission in a child compartment
  CANNOT modify or delete policies attached to a parent compartment
```

**Where to Attach a Policy for a Given Target Compartment:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Scenario: Allow NetworkAdmin to manage VCNs in C       │
│              (Root → Compartment A → Compartment B → Compartment C) │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Option 1: Attach to Compartment C (best practice — clean, easy)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Allow group Domain1/NetworkAdmin to manage virtual-network- │
  │  family in compartment C                                     │
  │  Who controls: users with "manage policies" in C             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Option 2: Attach to Compartment B (parent of C)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Allow group Domain1/NetworkAdmin to manage virtual-network- │
  │  family in compartment C                                     │
  │  (inheritance makes it apply to C)                           │
  │  Who controls: users with "manage policies" in B             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Option 3: Attach to Compartment A (grandparent of C)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Allow group Domain1/NetworkAdmin to manage virtual-network- │
  │  family in compartment B:C                                   │
  │  (must provide path: B:C because A only sees B directly)     │
  │  Who controls: users with "manage policies" in A             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Option 4: Attach to Root Compartment (tenancy)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Allow group Domain1/NetworkAdmin to manage virtual-network- │
  │  family in compartment A:B:C                                 │
  │  (must provide full path: A:B:C)                             │
  │  Who controls: users with "manage policies" in tenancy only  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Compartment Path Syntax (for Non-Direct Parents):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              When Attaching Policy to an Ancestor Compartment       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  When writing a policy in a grandparent or higher compartment,
  you must provide the full path to the target compartment:

  Attached in A → target C:   in compartment B:C
```
