└──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Typical Route Table Setup (Public vs Private Subnet):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Public Subnet Route Table                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Destination       Target
  0.0.0.0/0    →   Internet Gateway

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Private Subnet Route Table                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Destination                    Target
  0.0.0.0/0                 →   NAT Gateway
  <Oracle Services CIDR>    →   Service Gateway
```

**Key Points:**

- Route tables define the **path for outbound traffic**; intra-VCN traffic uses an implicit local route (not visible in console)

- Default route table is auto-created with every VCN; shows 0 rules but always has the implicit local route

- **One subnet → one route table** only; one route table can serve multiple subnets

- **Most specific rule wins** when destination CIDRs overlap (e.g., a specific Oracle Services CIDR beats 0.0.0.0/0)

- If no matching route rule exists, traffic is **dropped**

- Six supported target types: Internet Gateway, NAT Gateway, Service Gateway, Local Peering Gateway, Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG), Private IP

- NAT Gateway is **unidirectional** — allows outbound traffic from private subnets only, not inbound

================================================================================

**VCN Internet Gateway:**

**Overview:**

- An Internet Gateway enables **direct, bidirectional connectivity** between a VCN and the public internet

- Supports both **ingress** (internet → VCN) and **egress** (VCN → internet) traffic

- Managed service — Oracle handles scaling, high availability, and fault tolerance

- Only **one Internet Gateway per VCN** is needed (and only one is allowed)

**Traffic Direction:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Internet Gateway — Traffic Flow                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Egress:   VCN ──────────────────▶ Internet   (outbound)
  Ingress:  VCN ◀────────────────── Internet   (inbound)

  Internet Gateway supports BOTH directions (bidirectional) ↔
```

**Three Conditions to Use Internet Gateway:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  An instance can communicate via Internet Gateway ONLY IF:          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Condition 1: Instance must be in a PUBLIC subnet
               (private subnets cannot use Internet Gateway)

  Condition 2: Instance must have a PUBLIC IP address
               assigned to its vNIC

  Condition 3: The subnet's route table must have a route rule:
               Destination: 0.0.0.0/0
               Target:      Internet Gateway

  ⚠️  Even with all 3 conditions met, security list / NSG rules
      must also OPEN the required ports for communication to succeed
```

**Architecture Diagram:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Internet Gateway Setup                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VCN                                                          │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────┐                           │
  │  │  Public Subnet                │                           │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐  │                           │
  │  │  │  VM (has public IP)     │  │                           │
  │  │  └────────────┬────────────┘  │                           │
  │  └───────────────┼───────────────┘                           │
  │                  │  Route Table: 0.0.0.0/0 → Internet GW     │
  │                  ▼                                            │
  │         [Internet Gateway]                                    │
  └──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                     ↕  (bidirectional)
                  Internet
```

**Route Table Entry:**

```
  Destination    Target
  0.0.0.0/0  →  Internet Gateway
```

**Key Points:**

- Internet Gateway provides **bidirectional** internet connectivity (ingress and egress) for a VCN

- Only **one Internet Gateway per VCN** — you cannot create multiple

- Three conditions required: instance in **public subnet** + instance has **public IP** + subnet route table has rule pointing to Internet Gateway

- Security list / NSG port rules must also be configured to allow the specific traffic (routing alone is not sufficient)

- Managed service — no need to manage availability, scaling, or failover

================================================================================

**VCN NAT Gateway:**

**Overview:**

- NAT = **Network Address Translation**

- Gives instances in a **private subnet** outbound access to the internet without exposing them to inbound connections

- Unlike Internet Gateway, NAT Gateway is **unidirectional** — outbound only

- Managed, highly available service (replaces the older NAT instance approach)

**Traffic Rules:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              NAT Gateway — Allowed vs Denied Traffic                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✓ ALLOWED:  Private instance initiates connection to internet
              (e.g., downloading patches, calling external APIs)

  ✓ ALLOWED:  Private instance receives RESPONSE to its own request

  ✗ DENIED:   Inbound connection INITIATED from the internet
              (internet cannot reach private instances via NAT GW)

  Direction:  Private Subnet ──────────▶ Internet   (outbound only)
              Private Subnet ◀── ─ ─ ─── Internet   (responses OK)
              Private Subnet   ✗◀──────── Internet   (new inbound denied)
```

**How NAT Works — Source IP:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Source IP in Outbound Requests                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Private instance has NO public IP → cannot communicate directly

  NAT Gateway translates the source IP:
    Private instance IP  ──▶  NAT Gateway's PUBLIC IP  ──▶  Internet

  → Internet sees the NAT Gateway's public IP as the source
  → Private instance identity is hidden from the internet
```

**NAT Gateway Public IP Options:**

```
┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  IP Type             │  Description                                 │
├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Ephemeral           │  Oracle assigns a public IP automatically    │
│                      │  Not permanent — may change                  │
├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Reserved            │  You assign a pre-reserved public IP         │
│                      │  Permanent — survives gateway recreation     │
└──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Supported Traffic Types:**

- TCP
- UDP
- ICMP (ping)

**Concurrent Connections:**

- Supports up to **20,000 concurrent connections**

**Blocking Traffic:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              NAT Gateway Block Feature                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  After creating a NAT Gateway, you can BLOCK all traffic through it

  → Overrides ALL route rules and security rules in the VCN
  → All traffic through NAT Gateway is dropped regardless of rules
  → Useful for emergency access control or maintenance
```

**Scope — VCN-Only Resource:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  NAT Gateway can ONLY be used by resources in its OWN VCN           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✗ Resources in a PEERED VCN cannot use another VCN's NAT Gateway
  ✗ On-premises resources connected via DRG cannot use the NAT Gateway

  → NAT Gateway serves only the VCN it is attached to
```

**Architecture Diagram:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              NAT Gateway Architecture                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VCN                                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐                      │
  │  │  Private Subnet                    │                      │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐        │                      │
  │  │  │  DB/App  │  │  DB/App  │        │                      │
  │  │  │  Server  │  │  Server  │        │                      │
  │  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘        │                      │
  │  └───────┼─────────────┼──────────────┘                      │
  │          └──────┬───────┘                                     │
  │     Route: 0.0.0.0/0 → NAT Gateway                           │
  │                 ▼                                             │
  │         [NAT Gateway]  (has public IP)                        │
  └─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────── ┘
                    │  outbound only ──▶
                 Internet
                    │  ✗ inbound connections blocked
```

**Route Table Entry:**

```
  Destination    Target
  0.0.0.0/0  →  NAT Gateway
```

**Key Points:**

- NAT Gateway provides **outbound-only** internet access for private subnet instances — inbound connections initiated from the internet are denied

- Private instances have no public IP; NAT Gateway's public IP is used as the source IP for outbound requests

- Public IP options: **ephemeral** (Oracle-assigned, not permanent) or **reserved** (pre-allocated, permanent)

- Supports **TCP, UDP, and ICMP** traffic; up to **20,000 concurrent connections**

- NAT Gateway has a **block feature** — when enabled, all traffic through it is dropped regardless of route/security rules

- NAT Gateway is **VCN-scoped** — cannot be shared with peered VCNs or on-premises networks

- Managed, highly available service — no manual scaling or failover needed

================================================================================

**VCN Service Gateway:**

**Overview:**

- A Service Gateway enables **private access to Oracle Services Network (OSN)** — traffic never traverses the public internet

- Traffic travels over **Oracle's internal network fabric**, keeping data private and secure

- Hosts in the VCN can access Oracle services using only **private IP addresses** — no public IP, no Internet Gateway, no NAT Gateway required

**Oracle Services Network (OSN):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Oracle Services Network (OSN)                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  • Reserved for Oracle-managed cloud services
    (e.g., Object Storage, Autonomous Database, etc.)

  • These services have PUBLIC IP addresses

  • Without Service Gateway: traffic to OSN goes over the internet
    (not best practice — exposes data in transit)

  • With Service Gateway: traffic stays on Oracle network fabric
    (private, secure, never hits the internet)
```

**Services CIDR Label:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Services CIDR Label                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Problem with manual IP ranges:
    Oracle services in OSN have public IP address ranges
    If you hardcode those ranges in route tables / security rules,
    you must manually update them whenever Oracle changes the IPs

  Solution — Services CIDR Label:
    A predefined string that represents ALL regional public IP ranges
    for a specific Oracle service — automatically stays current

  Example format:
    oci-phx-objectstorage
    (OCI Phoenix region — Object Storage service)

  → Use the Services CIDR label as the route destination instead
    of manually entering IP address ranges
```

**Architecture Diagram:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Service Gateway Architecture                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VCN                                                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐                      │
  │  │  Private Subnet                    │                      │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐        │                      │
  │  │  │  DB/App  │  │  DB/App  │        │                      │
  │  │  │  Server  │  │  Server  │        │                      │
  │  │  │(private  │  │(private  │        │                      │
  │  │  │  IP only)│  │  IP only)│        │                      │
  │  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘        │                      │
  │  └───────┼─────────────┼──────────────┘                      │
  │          └──────┬───────┘                                     │
  │    Route: oci-phx-objectstorage → Service Gateway             │
  │                 ▼                                             │
  │         [Service Gateway]                                     │
  └─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                    │  Oracle Network Fabric (private, no internet)
                    ▼
          Oracle Services Network
          (e.g., Object Storage)
```

**Route Table Entry (Private Subnet):**

```
  Destination                  Target
  0.0.0.0/0               →   NAT Gateway         (general internet)
  oci-phx-objectstorage   →   Service Gateway     (Oracle services, private)

  → Most specific rule wins: Object Storage traffic goes via Service
    Gateway even though it also falls under 0.0.0.0/0
```

**Comparison — Gateway Types for Private Subnets:**

```
┌────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Gateway               │  Use Case for Private Subnet              │
├────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  NAT Gateway           │  Outbound access to general internet      │
│                        │  (e.g., patch downloads, external APIs)   │
├────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Service Gateway       │  Private access to Oracle Services        │
│                        │  Network (e.g., Object Storage)           │
│                        │  without touching the internet            │
└────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Service Gateway provides **private access to Oracle Services Network** — traffic travels over Oracle's network fabric, never the internet

- Hosts need **only private IP addresses** — no public IP, Internet Gateway, or NAT Gateway required

- Oracle services in OSN have public IPs but are accessed privately via Service Gateway

- Use **Services CIDR labels** (e.g., `oci-phx-objectstorage`) as route destinations instead of hardcoded IP ranges — automatically stays current if Oracle changes IPs

- Service Gateway is **regional** — it enables access to Oracle services in the **same region** only

- Combine with NAT Gateway in the same route table: `0.0.0.0/0 → NAT GW` for general internet, `<OSN label> → Service GW` for Oracle services (most specific rule wins)

================================================================================

**VCN Public Subnet — Configuration:**

**Overview:**

- A public subnet allows instances within it to be assigned **public IP addresses** on their vNIC

- Selecting "Public Subnet" at creation time enables this capability; it is the **default choice** in OCI console

- The public/private designation **cannot be changed after creation**

**Full Setup Checklist for Internet Connectivity:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Public Subnet — Steps for Internet Access              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Step 1: Create subnet as PUBLIC
          → Enables public IP assignment on vNICs

  Step 2: Deploy VM instance in the public subnet
          → Enable public IP address on the instance vNIC during creation

  Step 3: Associate the subnet with a route table containing:
          Destination: 0.0.0.0/0
          Target:      Internet Gateway

  Step 4: Configure security rules to open required ports:
          → Security List (subnet-level) and/or
          → Network Security Group (vNIC-level)

  ⚠️  Routing + public IP alone is NOT sufficient —
      security rules must also allow the traffic
```

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Public Subnet Architecture                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VCN                                                         │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────┐                       │
  │  │  Public Subnet                    │                       │
  │  │  ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐  │                       │
  │  │  │ VM 1        │ │ VM 2        │  │                       │
  │  │  │ public IP ✓ │ │ public IP ✓ │  │                       │
  │  │  └──────┬──────┘ └──────┬──────┘  │                       │
  │  └─────────┼───────────────┼─────────┘                       │
  │            └───────┬───────┘                                  │
  │    Route: 0.0.0.0/0 → Internet Gateway                        │
  │    Security: ports opened in Security List / NSG               │
  │                    ▼                                           │
  │           [Internet Gateway]                                   │
  └────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────── ┘
                       ↕  (bidirectional)
                    Internet
```

**Key Points:**

- Public subnet = instances **can** have public IP addresses assigned to their vNIC; private subnet = instances **cannot**

- Public IP is allocated to the **instance vNIC**, not the subnet itself

- Four requirements for full internet connectivity: public subnet + public IP on vNIC + route table entry `0.0.0.0/0 → Internet Gateway` + security rules opening required ports

- Subnet access type (public/private) is set at creation and **cannot be changed later**

================================================================================

**VCN Private Subnet — Configuration:**

**Overview:**

- A private subnet **prohibits public IP addresses** for instances deployed within it

- Instances have **only private IP addresses** on their vNIC

- Provides isolation from direct internet access — suitable for backend workloads (databases, app servers)

**Connectivity Options from Private Subnet:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Private Subnet — Connectivity Options                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Destination             │  Gateway to Use                       │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Internet                │  NAT Gateway (outbound only)          │
  │  (e.g., patch downloads) │                                       │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Oracle Services Network │  Service Gateway (private path)       │
  │  (e.g., Object Storage)  │                                       │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  On-premises environment │  Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG)        │
  │  (customer data center)  │                                       │
  └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Private Subnet Architecture                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VCN                                                         │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────┐                       │
  │  │  Private Subnet                   │                       │
  │  │  ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐  │                       │
  │  │  │ DB Server 1 │ │ DB Server 2 │  │                       │
  │  │  │ private IP  │ │ private IP  │  │                       │
  │  │  │ only        │ │ only        │  │                       │
  │  │  └──────┬──────┘ └──────┬──────┘  │                       │
  │  └─────────┼───────────────┼─────────┘                       │
  │            └───────┬───────┘                                  │
  │                    │                                           │
  │     ┌──────────────┴───────────────┐                          │
  │     ▼                              ▼                           │
  │ [NAT Gateway]              [Dynamic Routing GW]               │
  └──────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┘
         │ outbound only                │
         ▼                              ▼
      Internet                  On-Premises Network
```

**Typical Private Subnet Route Table:**

```
  Destination                  Target
  0.0.0.0/0               →   NAT Gateway          (internet, outbound)
  <Oracle Services CIDR>  →   Service Gateway      (OSN, private)
  <On-premises CIDR>      →   Dynamic Routing GW   (on-prem connectivity)
```

**Key Points:**

- Private subnet **prohibits public IP addresses** — instances have private IPs only

- Use **NAT Gateway** for outbound internet access (e.g., patch downloads) — inbound from internet is blocked

- Use **Service Gateway** for private access to Oracle Services Network (e.g., Object Storage)

- Use **Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG)** for communication with on-premises environments

- Subnet access type (public/private) **cannot be changed after creation**

================================================================================

**VCN Security — Overview:**

**Security Strategies for VCN:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              VCN Security Approaches                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Public / Private Subnets
     → Designate subnets as private to prevent public IP assignment

  2. Security Rules (two constructs)
     → Security List    — subnet-level virtual firewall
     → Network Security Group (NSG) — vNIC-level virtual firewall

  3. Instance-Level Firewall Rules
     → Configure OS firewall rules directly on the VM instance

  4. IAM Policies
     → Control who can create/manage VCNs, subnets, security lists,
       and NSGs

  5. Route Tables and Gateways
     → Control traffic paths and limit access points (e.g., no
       Internet Gateway in private-only architectures)

  6. Security Zones
     → Compartment-level enforcement of Oracle security best practices
     → Does NOT permit public IP addresses — private subnets only
     → Ensures networking resources comply with Oracle security principles
```

**Two VCN Security Constructs — Analogy:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Housing Society Analogy                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Housing Society  =  Subnet
  Individual House =  VM Instance
  Main Gate Guard  =  Security List  (controls entry to the subnet)
  House Guard      =  Network Security Group  (controls entry to the VM)

  Person (traffic) must:
    1. Pass the main gate guard (Security List)  →  enter the subnet
    2. Pass the house guard (NSG)                →  reach the VM instance

  → You can use Security List only, NSG only, or BOTH
```

**Security List vs Network Security Group (NSG):**

```
┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Security List             │  Network Security Group (NSG)        │
├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Associated with a SUBNET  │  Associated with a VNIC              │
│  Applies to ALL VMs in     │  Applies to specific VMs/resources   │
│  the subnet                │  (more granular)                     │
├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Up to 5 security lists    │  Up to 5 NSGs per VNIC               │
│  per subnet                │                                      │
├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
│  A DEFAULT security list   │  No default NSG — created manually   │
│  is created with every VCN │                                      │
└────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Limits:**

- Max **5 security lists** per subnet
- Max **5 NSGs** per vNIC
- A **default security list** is auto-created when a VCN is created (similar to the default route table)

**Key Points:**

- VCN security is enforced through multiple layers: subnet type, security rules, IAM policies, instance firewalls, route tables, gateways, and security zones

- **Security List** = subnet-level virtual firewall (all VMs in the subnet are affected)

- **Network Security Group (NSG)** = vNIC-level virtual firewall (more granular, applies per resource)

- Both Security List and NSG define **ingress** (inbound) and **egress** (outbound) traffic rules

- You can use Security List only, NSG only, or both together

- **Security Zones** enforce Oracle security best practices at the compartment level — they **do not allow public IP addresses** (private subnets only)

- A **default security list** is automatically created with every new VCN

================================================================================

**VCN Network Security Group (NSG):**

**Overview:**

- NSG is a **virtual firewall** that defines ingress (inbound) and egress (outbound) security rules at the **vNIC level**

- Associated with the vNIC of supported resources — more granular than Security List (which is subnet-level)

- Supports resources: **Compute Instances, Load Balancers, Mount Targets, API Gateways**, and others

- When a VCN is created, **no default NSG is created** — you must create NSGs manually

- All resources in an NSG must be in the **same VCN**

**NSG Components:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              NSG Contains Two Things                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Set of VNICs (or resources) to associate the NSG with
  2. Set of security rules (ingress + egress)
```

**Ingress Rule Fields:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Ingress Rule — Traffic INTO the instance               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Field              Options / Notes
  ─────────────────  ────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Source Type        CIDR Block | Service (e.g., Object Storage) | NSG
  Source             CIDR / Service name / NSG reference
  IP Protocol        TCP, UDP, ICMP, RDP, SSH, etc.
  Source Port Range  Optional
  Dest Port Range    Optional (auto-populated based on protocol)
  Rule Type          Stateful or Stateless
```

**Egress Rule Fields:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Egress Rule — Traffic OUT FROM the instance            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Field              Options / Notes
  ─────────────────  ────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Destination Type   CIDR Block | Service | NSG
  Destination        CIDR / Service name / NSG reference
  IP Protocol        TCP, UDP, ICMP, etc.
  Source Port Range  Optional
  Dest Port Range    Optional
  Rule Type          Stateful or Stateless
```

**Stateful vs Stateless Rules:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Stateful vs Stateless Security Rules                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STATEFUL (uses connection tracking):
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  • Response traffic is AUTOMATICALLY tracked and allowed
  • No need to define a matching egress rule for ingress traffic

  Example:
    Ingress rule: TCP, source 0.0.0.0/0, dest port 80  (HTTP)
    → Response traffic from instance automatically allowed ✓
    → No explicit egress rule needed

  Use case: General-purpose traffic, most typical deployments

  STATELESS (no connection tracking):
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  • Response traffic is NOT automatically allowed
  • Must explicitly define BOTH ingress AND egress rules

  Example:
    Ingress rule: TCP, source 0.0.0.0/0, dest port 80
    Egress rule REQUIRED: TCP, dest 0.0.0.0/0, source port 80,
                          dest port all  ← must add explicitly

  Use case: High-volume, internet-facing websites (HTTP/HTTPS)
            where connection tracking overhead should be avoided

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                  │  Stateful           │  Stateless             │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Connection track │  Yes                │  No                    │
  │ Response auto-OK │  Yes                │  No — must add rule    │
  │ Best for         │  General workloads  │  High-volume HTTP/HTTPS│
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘
```

**NSG as Source/Destination (Inter-Tier Communication):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Using NSG as Source/Destination Type                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Use case: Controlling traffic between application tiers

  Web Tier (3 instances) → NSG-Web
  App Tier (2 instances) → NSG-App

  To allow web tier → app tier traffic:
    NSG-App ingress rule:
      Source Type: NSG
      Source:      NSG-Web  ← only traffic from web tier is allowed

  → Condition: both NSGs must be in the same VCN
  → More flexible and maintainable than CIDR-based rules
    (no need to update rules when instance IPs change)
```

**Architecture Example:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              NSG Architecture Example                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  VCN
  ├── Subnet A
  │   ├── Instance 1 → NSG-A  (rule: ingress TCP 80 from 0.0.0.0/0)
  │   └── Instance 2 → NSG-A
  └── Subnet B
      └── Instance 3 → NSG-B  (rule: ingress TCP 22 from 0.0.0.0/0)

  → Instance 1 and 2 share the same NSG → same security rules
  → Instance 3 has a different NSG → SSH (port 22) allowed from internet
  → NSG rules are applied at the vNIC level, not the subnet level
```

**Key Points:**

- NSG is a **vNIC-level virtual firewall** — more granular than Security List (subnet-level)

- Contains two things: **set of VNICs** to associate with, and **set of ingress/egress rules**

- Source/Destination types for rules: **CIDR**, **Service**, or **NSG** (another NSG in the same VCN)

- **Stateful** rules auto-allow response traffic (uses connection tracking); **Stateless** rules require explicit ingress AND egress rules but avoid connection tracking overhead

- Use **NSG as source/destination** to control traffic between tiers (web → app) without hardcoding IPs

- **No default NSG** is created when a VCN is created — must be created manually

- Up to **5 NSGs per vNIC**; all resources in an NSG must be in the **same VCN**

- Recommended for high-volume HTTP/HTTPS use stateless rules; use stateful for general workloads

================================================================================

**VCN Security List:**

**Overview:**

- Security List is a **subnet-level virtual firewall** — a common set of ingress and egress rules applied to **all instances** within a subnet

- Defined at the subnet level but **enforced at the vNIC level**

- A **default security list** is automatically created when a VCN is created

- Up to **5 security lists** can be associated with a single subnet

**Security List vs NSG — Key Differences:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Security List               │  Network Security Group (NSG)      │
├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Associated with SUBNET      │  Associated with VNIC              │
│  Applies to ALL VMs in subnet│  Applies to specific VNICs         │
├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Source/Dest types:          │  Source/Dest types:                │
│  CIDR, Service               │  CIDR, Service, NSG                │
│  ✗ No Security List ref      │  ✓ Can reference another NSG       │
├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Default created with VCN    │  No default — must create manually │
└──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Use Case — Enforcing Rules Across All Instances in a VCN:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Security List Applied to Multiple Subnets              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  VCN
  ├── Subnet A  ─┐
  ├── Subnet B  ─┼── All associated with Security List 1
  └── Subnet C  ─┘      (same rules apply to all VMs in all subnets)

  → One Security List can enforce uniform rules across the entire VCN
  → Ideal when the same base rules should apply to everything
```

**When Multiple Security Lists and NSGs Are Applied:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Combined Security Rules — Union Principle              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Example:
  Subnet → Security List 1, Security List 2
  VNIC   → NSG-A, NSG-B

  Result: UNION of all rules from SL1 + SL2 + NSG-A + NSG-B

  Guiding Principle:
  → Traffic is ALLOWED only if there is an ALLOW rule in ANY
    of the associated Security Lists OR NSGs
  → If no allow rule exists in any construct → traffic is DENIED

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │  SL1 allows  │  SL2 allows  │  NSG allows  │  Result          │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  ✓           │  ✗           │  ✗           │  ALLOWED         │
  │  ✗           │  ✗           │  ✓           │  ALLOWED         │
  │  ✗           │  ✗           │  ✗           │  DENIED          │
  └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────┘
```

**Stateful and Stateless Rules:**

- Security List supports both **stateful** and **stateless** rules — same behavior as NSG rules

- Stateful: response traffic is automatically allowed (connection tracking)

- Stateless: must explicitly define both ingress and egress rules

**Architecture Diagram:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Security List Architecture                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VCN                                                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Subnet                                                  │  │
  │  │  Associated with: Security List 1, Security List 2       │  │
  │  │                                                          │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │  │
  │  │  │  VM Instance vNIC                                │    │  │
  │  │  │  Associated with: NSG-A, NSG-B                   │    │  │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Effective rules = SL1 ∪ SL2 ∪ NSG-A ∪ NSG-B  (union of all)
```

**Key Points:**

- Security List is a **subnet-level** firewall; enforced at the vNIC level for all instances in the subnet

- A **default security list** is auto-created with every VCN; up to **5 security lists** per subnet

- Security List source/destination types: **CIDR** and **Service** only — **cannot reference another Security List** (unlike NSG which can reference another NSG)

- When Security Lists and NSGs are combined, the **union** of all rules applies — traffic is allowed if **any** construct has a matching allow rule

- Use Security List to enforce **uniform baseline rules** across all subnets in a VCN; use NSG for resource-specific granular control

================================================================================

**VCN IP Management — Overview:**

**Two Types of IP Addresses:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Private IP vs Public IP                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Private IP                  │  Public IP                       │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Internal communication      │  Reachable from the internet     │
  │  within VCN and connected    │  Assigned to a private IP object │
  │  networks                    │  on the resource                 │
  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Private IP — Use Cases:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Private IP Use Cases                                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Intra-VCN communication
     → Instance communicates with other instances in the same VCN
       (same or different subnets)

  2. VCN Peering (same region or cross-region)
     → Instances in peered VCNs communicate using private IPs

  3. On-premises connectivity
     → Via Site-to-Site VPN or FastConnect — uses private IPs
```

**VNIC — Private IP Address Limits:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Private IP Addresses per VNIC                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Each VNIC:
    Primary private IP address:    1    (always present)
    Secondary private IP addresses: up to 32
    ─────────────────────────────────────
    Total private IPs per VNIC:    33  (max)

  Instance with Primary VNIC + Secondary VNIC:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Instance                                                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────┐   ┌───────────────────┐             │
  │  │  Primary VNIC     │   │  Secondary VNIC   │             │
  │  │  1 primary IP     │   │  1 primary IP     │             │
  │  │  + up to 32 sec   │   │  + up to 32 sec   │             │
  │  └───────────────────┘   └───────────────────┘             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  → Each private IP (primary or secondary) can optionally have a
    public IP address assigned to it
```

**Public IP Address Types:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Ephemeral vs Reserved Public IP                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ephemeral               │  Reserved                          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Temporary               │  Persistent                        │
  │  Exists only for the     │  Exists beyond instance lifetime   │
  │  LIFETIME of the instance│  Can be reassigned to another      │
  │  Deleted when instance   │  resource after instance is        │
  │  is terminated           │  terminated                        │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Can ONLY be assigned to │  Can be assigned to PRIMARY or     │
  │  PRIMARY private IP      │  SECONDARY private IP              │
  │  on a VNIC               │  on a VNIC                         │
  └──────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  Pricing: ✓ No charge for public IP addresses in OCI
             Even unassigned reserved public IPs are free
```

**Conditions for Public IP to Be Reachable from Internet:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Three Conditions for Internet Reachability                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Internet Gateway must exist in the VCN

  2. Public subnet route table must have:
     Destination: 0.0.0.0/0  →  Target: Internet Gateway

  3. Security List / NSG must allow the required traffic (ports open)
```

**Resources with Public IPs:**

- Any instance in a **public subnet** with public IP enabled on vNIC
- **NAT Gateway** — assigned a public IP (ephemeral or reserved) for outbound translation
- Other OCI resources designed for direct internet reachability

**Key Points:**

- Every instance has at least one **primary private IP** address; each VNIC supports up to **33 private IPs** (1 primary + 32 secondary)

- Each private IP (primary or secondary) can optionally have a **public IP** assigned to it

- **Ephemeral** public IP = tied to instance lifetime, can only be assigned to the primary private IP

- **Reserved** public IP = persists after instance termination, can be assigned to primary OR secondary private IP, can be reassigned to any resource

- **No charge** for public IP addresses in OCI, including unassigned reserved IPs

- Private IPs are used for: intra-VCN communication, VCN peering (same or cross-region), and on-premises connectivity (VPN / FastConnect)

================================================================================

**VCN Reserved Public IP Addresses:**

**Overview:**

- A Reserved Public IP is a **persistent** public IP address that exists beyond the lifetime of any individual instance

- Can be unassigned and reassigned to any resource at any time

- Reduces security risk by allowing controlled, predictable IP address assignment

**Key Properties:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Reserved Public IP — Key Properties                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Creation:       One at a time (not bulk)
  Limit:          50 reserved public IPs per region
  Scope:          REGIONAL — can be assigned to a private IP in
                  ANY Availability Domain in the region
  Persistence:    Never automatically deleted — exists until you
                  explicitly delete it
  IP Source:      Oracle-assigned (from Oracle's public IP pool)
```

**Lifecycle — Assign, Unassign, Reassign:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Reserved Public IP Lifecycle                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Create Reserved Public IP
         │
         ▼
  Assign to a private IP (instance VNIC)
         │
         ▼
  Unassign (at any time)
         │
         ▼
  IP returns to tenancy's pool of reserved public IPs
         │
         ▼
  Reassign to any other resource when needed

  → Instance terminated? IP still exists in tenancy pool ✓
  → No automatic deletion — must be manually deleted
```

**Behavior When Private IP Moves Between VNICs:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Reserved Public IP follows the Private IP it is assigned to        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Instance
  ├── VNIC 1
  │   ├── Primary Private IP 1
  │   └── Secondary Private IP 2  ← Reserved Public IP X assigned here
  └── VNIC 2
      └── ...

  If Secondary Private IP 2 is MOVED from VNIC 1 → VNIC 2:
    → Reserved Public IP X also moves to VNIC 2 automatically
```

**Comparison — Ephemeral vs Reserved:**

```
┌──────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Ephemeral               │  Reserved                              │
├──────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Temporary               │  Persistent                            │
│  Deleted with instance   │  Survives instance termination         │
│  Primary IP only         │  Primary OR secondary private IP       │
│  Not reassignable        │  Unassign and reassign freely          │
│  No regional limit       │  Limit: 50 per region                  │
└──────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Reserved Public IP is **persistent** — survives instance termination and never auto-deleted

- Limit: **50 reserved public IPs per region**; created one at a time

- **Regional scope** — can be assigned to a private IP in any AD in the region

- When unassigned, IP returns to the tenancy's reserved IP pool (not released back to Oracle)

- Reserved public IP **follows the private IP** it is assigned to if that private IP is moved between VNICs

- Can be assigned to **primary or secondary** private IP (unlike ephemeral which is primary only)

- Provides flexibility to assign public IPs to resources on demand, reducing security risk from always-on public exposure

================================================================================

**VCN Bring Your Own IP (BYOIP):**

**Overview:**

- BYOIP allows you to import your own **public IPv4 CIDR blocks or IPv6 prefixes** from on-premises into OCI

- Enables smooth cloud migration when IP addresses are hard-coded in applications or devices

- Imported addresses are validated against the **Regional Internet Registry (RIR)** to confirm ownership

**Why Use BYOIP:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              BYOIP Benefits                                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Solution Continuity
     → Hard-coded IP addresses in applications or devices work
       without modification after migrating to OCI

  2. IP Reputation
     → Some internet services rely on contiguous IP address space
     → Bring your known/trusted IPs to maintain reputation

  3. IP Pools for Resource Deployment
     → Summarize groups of IPv4 addresses into pools
     → Use those pools for resources like load balancers
```

**Limits:**

```
┌────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Address Type      │  Allowed Range                              │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  IPv4 CIDR block   │  Minimum /24  |  Maximum /8                 │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  IPv6 prefix       │  Must be /48 or larger                      │
└────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**BYOIP Workflow:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              BYOIP Import Process (Step by Step)                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Step 1: Request to import
          └── Submit request to import your public IPv4 CIDR
              or IPv6 prefix that you own into OCI

  Step 2: Oracle issues a Verification Token
          └── Oracle generates a token to prove you own the IPs

  Step 3: Add Verification Token to RIR record
          └── Add the token to your IP address info at the
              Regional Internet Registry (RIR)
          └── ⏱️ This step can take up to 1 day

  Step 4: Create Route Origin Authorization (ROA)
          └── Create ROA with your RIR
          └── Include Oracle's BGP ASN for the commercial cloud
          └── ROA authorizes Oracle to ADVERTISE the BYOIP CIDR block

  Step 5: Request Oracle to complete the import
          └── Oracle communicates with the RIR and verifies ownership
          └── ⏱️ Total workflow can take up to 10 business days

  Step 6: Oracle provisions the BYOIP addresses
          └── CIDR block or IPv6 prefix provisioned to your compartment
          └── Now yours to manage in OCI

  Step 7: (Optional) Advertise to the internet
          └── You can advertise the IP block/prefix to the internet

  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Key terms:
    RIR  = Regional Internet Registry
           (validates ownership of public IP address space)
    ROA  = Route Origin Authorization
           (authorizes Oracle to advertise your CIDR on the internet)
    BGP ASN = Oracle's Border Gateway Protocol Autonomous System Number
```

**After Successful Import:**

- IPv4: Manage addresses through **IP Pools** in your compartment
- IPv6: Managed as an IPv6 prefix in your compartment
- Can be used with resources such as compute instances and load balancers
- Can be advertised to the internet if needed

**Key Points:**

- BYOIP lets you bring your own public IPv4 CIDRs (/24–/8) or IPv6 prefixes (/48+) into OCI

- Oracle validates ownership through the **Regional Internet Registry (RIR)** — addresses must be registered there

- A **Route Origin Authorization (ROA)** must be created with the RIR, providing Oracle's BGP ASN so Oracle can advertise the CIDR

- The full import workflow takes up to **10 business days**; adding the verification token to RIR takes up to **1 day**

- Primary use case: **smooth migration** when applications have hard-coded IP addresses

- After import, IPv4 addresses are managed through **IP Pools** in your OCI compartment

================================================================================

**VCN Public IP Pools:**

**Overview:**

- A **Public IP Pool** is a set of IPv4 CIDR blocks allocated exclusively to your tenancy

- Built from **Bring Your Own IP (BYOIP)** CIDR blocks — either the entire CIDR block or a portion of it

- Available only for your tenancy — not shared with other OCI customers

- **IPv6 addresses do NOT use the IP Pools functionality** — pools are IPv4 only

**How It Works:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Public IP Pool Flow                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Step 1: Bring Your Own IP (BYOIP)
          └── Import your public IPv4 CIDR block into OCI

  Step 2: Create a Public IP Pool
          └── Assign the entire BYOIP CIDR block OR a portion of it
              to the public IP pool

  Step 3: Use the Pool (two options)
          ├── Option A: Create a Reserved IP from the pool
          │            → Attach to a specific resource
          │              (NAT Gateway, Load Balancer, Compute Instance)
          │
          └── Option B: Direct Launch from Pool
                       → Launch a resource and the IP is automatically
                         allocated directly from the pool
                       → No need to pre-create a reserved IP
```

**Two Usage Options:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Option A: Reserved IP       │  Option B: Direct Launch from Pool │
├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Create a reserved public IP │  Launch resource directly          │
│  from the pool               │  IP allocated from pool at launch  │
│  Then attach to a resource   │  No pre-reservation needed         │
│  (NAT GW, LB, Compute)       │                                    │
└──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Limits and Quotas:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Public IP Pool Limits                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  IP CIDR ranges per pool:  0 or more
  Minimum CIDR range size:  /28
  Maximum CIDR range size:  /24
  Address type:             IPv4 only
```

**Key Points:**

- Public IP Pools hold IPv4 CIDR blocks sourced from BYOIP — exclusively allocated to your tenancy

- Must first complete the BYOIP import process before creating a public IP pool

- A pool can contain the full BYOIP CIDR or a subset of it

- Two ways to use: **create a reserved IP** from the pool and attach to a resource, or **direct launch** from the pool (IP assigned automatically at resource creation)

- Supported resources: NAT Gateway, Load Balancer, Compute Instances

- **IPv6 does not use IP Pools** — pools are IPv4 only

- CIDR range per pool: minimum **/28**, maximum **/24**

================================================================================

**VCN Connectivity Options — Overview:**

**VCN-to-VCN Connectivity:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              VCN Peering Options                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Scenario                │  Method                            │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  VCN ↔ VCN               │  Option 1: Local Peering Gateway   │
  │  (SAME region)           │           (LPG — one per VCN pair) │
  │  = Local Peering         │  Option 2: Dynamic Routing Gateway │
  │                          │           (DRG — both VCNs attach  │
  │                          │            to one DRG)             │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  VCN ↔ VCN               │  Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG)     │
  │  (DIFFERENT regions)     │  with Remote Peering Connection    │
  │  = Remote Peering        │                                    │
  └──────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️  After configuring any peering, route rules must be added to
      the route tables in both VCNs to enable traffic flow
```

**VCN to On-Premises Connectivity:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Three Options: VCN ↔ On-Premises                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Option 1: Public Internet                                       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Gateway:    Internet Gateway / NAT Gateway                      │
  │  Encryption: Not guaranteed (may apply app-level encryption)     │
  │  Throughput: No guarantee                                        │
  │  Cost:       Free                                                │
  │  Use Case:   POC / proof of concept; SaaS applications           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Option 2: Site-to-Site VPN (IPsec VPN)                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Gateway:    Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG)                       │
  │  Encryption: Yes — encrypted tunnel over internet                │
  │  Throughput: No guarantee (traffic traverses internet)           │
  │  Managed by: Oracle Cloud Infrastructure                         │
  │  Cost:       Free service                                        │
  │  Use Case:   Secure connectivity for non-critical workloads;     │
  │              recommended for POC with security requirement       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Option 3: FastConnect                                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Connection: DEDICATED private connection (not internet)         │
  │  Bandwidth:  Up to 100 Gbps                                      │
  │  Latency:    Low and consistent                                  │
  │  Encryption: Secure                                              │
  │  Managed by: Oracle Cloud Infrastructure                         │
  │  Cost:       Port charges (hourly); no egress data transfer fee  │
  │  Use Case:   Business-critical and latency-sensitive apps;       │
  │              compliance and security requirements                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Comparison Table — On-Premises Connectivity:**

```
┌─────────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────┐
│  Attribute      │  Public       │  Site-to-Site    │  FastConnect │
│                 │  Internet     │  VPN             │              │
├─────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────┤
│  Connection     │  Internet     │  Internet        │  Dedicated   │
├─────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────┤
│  Encryption     │  No           │  Yes (IPsec)     │  Yes         │
├─────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────┤
│  Throughput     │  No guarantee │  No guarantee    │  High / SLA  │
│  Guarantee      │               │                  │  up to 100G  │
├─────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────┤
│  Latency        │  Variable     │  Variable        │  Low, stable │
├─────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────┤
│  Cost           │  Free         │  Free            │  Port fee    │
│                 │               │                  │  (no egress) │
├─────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────┤
│  Best For       │  POC, SaaS    │  POC + security  │  Business    │
│                 │               │                  │  critical    │
└─────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────┘
```

**Architecture Overview:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              VCN Connectivity Summary                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

                      ┌────────────────────────┐
                      │  OCI Region 1          │
                      │  ┌───────┐  ┌───────┐  │
  Local Peering  ────▶│  │ VCN A │──│ VCN B │  │  (LPG or DRG)
                      │  └───────┘  └───────┘  │
                      └───────────┬────────────┘
                                  │ DRG
                                  │
                      ┌───────────▼────────────┐
                      │  OCI Region 2          │
  Remote Peering ────▶│  ┌───────┐             │  (DRG + RPC)
                      │  │ VCN C │             │
                      │  └───────┘             │
                      └───────────┬────────────┘
                                  │
            ┌─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐
            ▼                     ▼                       ▼
      Public Internet      Site-to-Site VPN         FastConnect
      (IGW / NAT GW)       (IPsec, encrypted)    (Dedicated, 100G)
            │                     │                       │
            └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘
                                  │
                          On-Premises Network
```

**Key Points:**

- **Local Peering** (same region): use Local Peering Gateway (LPG) or Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG)

- **Remote Peering** (different regions): use DRG with Remote Peering Connection

- **After peering**: route rules must be added to both VCN route tables

- Three on-premises connectivity options: **Public Internet** (free, no encryption), **Site-to-Site VPN** (encrypted over internet, free), **FastConnect** (dedicated, low latency, up to 100 Gbps, port fee only)

- FastConnect is preferred for business-critical, latency-sensitive, and compliance-regulated workloads

- Site-to-Site VPN is managed by OCI and is a free service; suitable for POC with security requirements

================================================================================

**VCN Local Peering:**

**Overview:**

- **Local VCN Peering** = private connectivity between two VCNs in the **same OCI region**

- Resources in peered VCNs communicate using **private IP addresses** (no public IPs needed)

- Alternative to using public internet (which requires public IPs and Internet Gateway)

**Prerequisites:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Local Peering Prerequisites                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Both VCNs must be in the SAME region

  2. The VCNs must have NON-OVERLAPPING CIDR blocks
     Example:
       VCN 1: 10.0.0.0/16     ✓ non-overlapping
       VCN 2: 192.168.0.0/16

  3. Route rules must be configured in both VCNs after setup

  4. Security rules (Security List / NSG) must allow the required traffic
```

**Two Methods for Local VCN Peering:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Method 1: Local Peering Gateway (LPG)                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Setup:
    VCN 1  ──[LPG 1]──── connection ────[LPG 2]──  VCN 2

  Steps:
  1. Create a Local Peering Gateway on EACH VCN (total: 2 LPGs)
  2. Create a peering CONNECTION between the two LPGs
  3. Add route rules in each VCN pointing to the other's CIDR
  4. Configure security rules to allow traffic

  Route table example (VCN 1 subnet):
    Destination: 192.168.0.0/16  →  Target: LPG 1

  Route table example (VCN 2 subnet):
    Destination: 10.0.0.0/16     →  Target: LPG 2

  Benefit: Extremely high bandwidth and low latency
  Limit:   Each VCN can have up to 10 LPGs

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Method 2: Upgraded Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG v2)               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Setup:
    VCN 1 ──┐
             ├── [DRG] ──  connects both VCNs
    VCN 2 ──┘

  Steps:
  1. Create ONE Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG)
  2. Attach BOTH VCN 1 and VCN 2 to the single DRG
  3. Add route rules in each VCN pointing to the other's CIDR
  4. Configure security rules to allow traffic

  Route table example (VCN 1 subnet):
    Destination: 192.168.0.0/16  →  Target: DRG

  Route table example (VCN 2 subnet):
    Destination: 10.0.0.0/16     →  Target: DRG

  Benefit: Flexibility in routing — one DRG can attach up to 300 VCNs
  Limit:   One VCN can be attached to one DRG
```

**Method Comparison:**

```
┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
│  Attribute             │  LPG Method          │  DRG Method         │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
│  Gateways required     │  2 LPGs (one per VCN)│  1 DRG              │
│                        │  + 1 connection      │                     │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
│  Scale                 │  Up to 10 LPGs/VCN   │  Up to 300 VCN      │
│                        │                      │  attachments/DRG    │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
│  Benefit               │  High bandwidth,     │  Routing flexibility │
│                        │  low latency         │  at scale            │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
│  DRG version           │  N/A                 │  DRG v2 (upgraded)  │
└────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘
```

**Important Routing Rules for Peered VCNs:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Internet Gateway Sharing — NOT Allowed                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Scenario:
    VCN 1 (has Internet Gateway) ←─── peered ───→ VCN 2 (no IGW)

  Question: Can VCN 2 instances use VCN 1's Internet Gateway?

  Answer:
    ✗ VCN 2 instances CANNOT send traffic to internet via VCN 1's IGW
    ✓ VCN 2 instances CAN receive traffic that arrived via VCN 1's IGW

  Rule: Gateways (Internet GW, NAT GW, etc.) in a peered VCN are
        NOT accessible for outbound traffic from the other VCN
```

**Key Points:**

- Local VCN Peering connects two VCNs in the **same region** via private IP addresses

- Two methods: **Local Peering Gateway (LPG)** — needs 2 LPGs + 1 connection; **DRG v2** — needs 1 DRG attached to both VCNs

- VCNs must have **non-overlapping CIDRs** to peer

- After peering, **route rules** must be added to subnet route tables in both VCNs pointing to the peer CIDR

- **Security rules** must also be configured to allow the specific traffic types

- DRG v2 supports up to **300 VCN attachments** — preferred for hub-and-spoke architectures

- Peered VCNs **cannot share gateways** for outbound traffic — each VCN's gateway is local to that VCN only

================================================================================

**VCN Remote Peering:**

**Overview:**

- **Remote VCN Peering** = private connectivity between two VCNs in **different OCI regions**

- Resources in peered VCNs communicate using **private IP addresses** — traffic never traverses the internet

- VCNs can be in the **same tenancy** or **different tenancies**

**Prerequisites:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Remote Peering Prerequisites                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  1. Two VCNs in DIFFERENT regions with NON-OVERLAPPING CIDRs

  2. A Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG) created and attached to each VCN
     (total: 2 DRGs — one per region/VCN)

  3. A Remote Peering Connection (RPC) created on each DRG
     (total: 2 RPCs — one per DRG)

  4. A CONNECTION established between the two RPCs

  5. Route rules configured in both VCNs after setup

  6. Security rules (Security List / NSG) configured to allow traffic
```

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Remote Peering Architecture                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │  OCI Region 1                │   │  OCI Region 2                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐  │   │  ┌────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  VCN 1                 │  │   │  │  VCN 2                 │  │
  │  │  (10.0.0.0/16)         │  │   │  │  (192.168.0.0/16)      │  │
  │  └──────────┬─────────────┘  │   │  └────────────┬───────────┘  │
  │             │                │   │               │              │
  │         [DRG 1]              │   │           [DRG 2]            │
  │             │                │   │               │              │
  │         [RPC 1] ─────────────────────────── [RPC 2]            │
  │                   connection │   │                              │
  └──────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────┘

  Components:
    DRG = Dynamic Routing Gateway (one per VCN)
    RPC = Remote Peering Connection (one per DRG, acts as connection point)
```

**Tenancy Support — Legacy vs Upgraded DRG:**

```
┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
│  DRG Type                │  Cross-Tenancy Support               │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Legacy DRG (v1)         │  Same tenancy only                   │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Upgraded DRG (v2)       │  Same tenancy AND different tenancies │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  Note: RPC can technically also be used between VCNs in the same region,
        but it is primarily intended for cross-region connectivity
```

**Local vs Remote Peering Comparison:**

```
┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
│  Attribute             │  Local Peering         │  Remote Peering    │
├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
│  VCN location          │  Same region           │  Different regions │
├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
│  CIDR requirement      │  Non-overlapping       │  Non-overlapping   │
├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
│  Gateway required      │  LPG (x2) OR DRG (x1) │  DRG (x2) + RPC   │
├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
│  Cross-tenancy (v2)    │  Yes                   │  Yes (DRG v2 only) │
├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
│  Traffic path          │  Oracle network        │  Oracle network    │
│                        │  (no internet)         │  (no internet)     │
└────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Remote peering connects two VCNs in **different OCI regions** using private IP addresses — traffic stays on Oracle's network

- Requires **2 DRGs** (one per VCN) and **2 RPCs** (one per DRG), plus a connection between the two RPCs

- VCNs must have **non-overlapping CIDRs** (same requirement as local peering)

- Route rules and security rules must be configured after setup

- **Legacy DRG** supports same-tenancy remote peering only; **upgraded DRG (v2)** supports cross-tenancy peering

- VCNs can be in the same or different tenancies

================================================================================

**Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) — Basics:**

**Overview:**

- **BGP (Border Gateway Protocol)** is the routing protocol of the internet — it determines the **best network routes** for data transmission between networks

- Analogy: like a maps application that calculates the optimized route for your journey — BGP selects the optimal path for your traffic

**Autonomous Systems:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Autonomous System (AS) Concepts                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Autonomous System (AS):
  - A collection of connected IP networks under the control of a
    SINGLE administrative entity
  - Each AS is assigned a unique Autonomous System Number (ASN)

  BGP Peer:
  - A device located at the edge/boundary of an autonomous system
  - BGP peers exchange routing information with neighboring BGP peers

    ┌───────────┐          routing info          ┌───────────┐
    │   AS 1    │ ◄──────────────────────────── │   AS 2    │
    │ [BGP Peer]│ ────────────────────────────► │ [BGP Peer]│
    └───────────┘          exchange             └───────────┘
```

**How BGP Works — Route Selection:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              BGP Route Discovery & Path Selection                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  BGP tasks:
    1. Route Discovery — learns all possible paths between networks
    2. Path Selection  — selects the best route based on attributes
                         (e.g., fewest hops, policy preferences)

  Example — Packet from AS 1 to AS 3:

    Option 1 (2 hops):   AS 1 → AS 2 → AS 3               ✓ SELECTED
    Option 2 (4 hops):   AS 1 → AS 6 → AS 5 → AS 4 → AS 3

    BGP selects Option 1 — fewer hops = preferred path

                 [AS 2]
                /       \
  [AS 1] ─────            ───── [AS 3]
                \                /
                 [AS 6]─[AS 5]─[AS 4]
```

**BGP in OCI — Why It Matters:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              OCI On-Premises Connectivity Options                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Two ways to connect OCI to on-premises:

  1. Site-to-Site VPN
     - Can use: BGP routing
                Static routing
                Combination of both

  2. FastConnect
     - ALWAYS uses BGP for route advertisements (mandatory)

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Connection Type     │  Routing Protocol                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Site-to-Site VPN    │  BGP, Static, or both                │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  FastConnect         │  BGP only (always required)          │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- BGP is the internet's routing protocol — selects the best path for data traveling through multiple networks based on attributes like hop count

- An **Autonomous System (AS)** is a collection of IP networks under a single administrative entity, each with a unique AS Number (ASN)

- **BGP peers** sit at the boundary of an AS and exchange routing information with neighboring peers

- BGP performs **route discovery** (learns available paths) and **path selection** (chooses the best route)

- In OCI, Site-to-Site VPN supports BGP, static routing, or both; **FastConnect always uses BGP** — understanding BGP is critical for configuring either

================================================================================

**Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG):**

**Overview:**

- A **Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG)** is a **virtual router** that facilitates communication between on-premises networks and Virtual Cloud Networks (VCNs)

- A single DRG can connect multiple VCNs (up to **300 VCNs**) in the same or different tenancies

**DRG Attachment Types:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              DRG Attachment Types                                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

                        ┌──────────────┐
    VCN attachment ────►│              │◄──── IPsec tunnel attachment
                        │     DRG      │      (Site-to-Site VPN)
    RPC attachment ────►│  (virtual    │◄──── Virtual circuit attachment
                        │   router)   │      (FastConnect)
    Loopback ──────────►│              │◄──── Cross-tenancy attachment
                        └──────────────┘

  Loopback attachment: used to forward traffic from a virtual circuit
  attachment to an IPsec tunnel attachment within the SAME DRG
```

**DRG Route Tables:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Default Route Tables (auto-created on DRG creation)    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Route Table 1: Autogenerated DRG route table for VCN attachments
  Route Table 2: Autogenerated DRG route table for RPC, Virtual Circuit,
                 and IPsec attachments

  Notes:
  - You can create ADDITIONAL route tables and assign them per attachment
  - You can assign the SAME route table to multiple attachments
  - Each attachment always has one associated route table
```

**Route Types inside DRG Route Tables:**

```
┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Route Type      │  Description                                     │
├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Static Route    │  Created manually by administrator               │
├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Dynamic Route   │  Automatically imported via Import Route         │
│                  │  Distribution statements                         │
└──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Static routes always have HIGHER preference than dynamic routes
```

**DRG Route Distributions:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Route Distributions (auto-created on DRG creation)     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Import Route Distributions (2 created automatically):
    1. Import route distribution for ALL routes
       → assigned to Route Table for VCN attachments
       → VCN route table learns routes from ALL attachment types

    2. Import route distribution for VCN routes only
       → assigned to Route Table for RPC/VC/IPsec attachments
       → these tables learn ONLY VCN routes by default

  Export Route Distribution (1 created automatically):
    1. Default export route distribution
       → specifies how routes are advertised FROM route tables TO attachments

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Default Behavior Summary (after attaching VCN + RPC)           │
  ├───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Route Table              │  Dynamic Routes Populated           │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  For VCN attachments      │  VCN routes + RPC routes (all)      │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  For RPC/VC/IPsec         │  VCN routes only                    │
  └───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Import Route Distribution — How It Works:**

```
  1. Write statements with match criteria (e.g., "all traffic" or
     match on specific attachment type)

  2. If a statement matches, routes from that attachment are
     dynamically imported into the DRG route table assigned to
     that distribution

  3. Statements are prioritized by number — LOWEST number = HIGHEST priority

  Flow:
  Attachment (VCN/RPC/IPsec/VC)
        │ route advertisement
        ▼
  Import Route Distribution (match criteria)
        │ if match
        ▼
  DRG Route Table (dynamic route entry added)
```

**Export Route Distribution — How It Works:**

```
  - Reverse of import — specifies how routes are advertised FROM
    the assigned route tables TO the attachments
