  Restriction:
  - Routes imported from an IPsec tunnel or virtual circuit attachment
    will NEVER be exported to other IPsec tunnel or virtual circuit
    attachments (by design)
```

**Route Conflict Resolution (Priority Order):**

```
  When route conflicts exist, priority is:

    1. Static routes           (highest priority — always wins)
    2. VCN routes
    3. Virtual circuit routes  (FastConnect)
    4. IPsec tunnel routes     (Site-to-Site VPN)
    5. Remote Peering Connection routes  (lowest priority)

  Notes:
  - VCN and static routes have empty AS paths
  - IPsec tunnel and virtual circuit routes have populated AS paths
  - Dynamic route conflicts resolved by AS path length
```

**ECMP — Equal Cost Multi-Path Routing:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              ECMP (Equal Cost Multi-Path Routing)                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  - Default: OFF
  - Enabled per DRG route table
  - Enables load balancing of network traffic across:
      • Multiple FastConnect virtual circuits
      • Multiple IPsec tunnels
  - Uses Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) for load balancing
```

**VCN Route Table vs DRG Route Table:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Two Route Tables for a VCN Attachment                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────┐   attachment   ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │    VCN       │◄──────────────►│            DRG                  │
  └──────────────┘                └─────────────────────────────────┘
        │                                        │
  VCN Route Table                       DRG Route Table
  (routes packets                       (routes packets entering DRG
   entering VCN via                      — determines next hop
   the attachment)                        inside DRG)
```

**DRG Routing Engine — Packet Flow:**

```
  Step 1: Packet arrives into DRG via an attachment
                    │
  Step 2: Route lookup performed on the route table
          associated with the INGRESS attachment
                    │
  Step 3: DRG routing engine forwards packet to next hop
          based on matching route table entry
```

**Use Cases:**

```
┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Use Case                  │  Description                           │
├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Simplified configuration  │  Single DRG replaces multiple LPGs    │
│                            │  for VCN-to-VCN peering               │
├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  High availability         │  Use FastConnect in one region to      │
│                            │  communicate with VCN in another region│
├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Increased scale           │  Up to 300 VCNs attached to one DRG   │
├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Transit / hub-and-spoke   │  Route traffic through a hub VCN       │
│                            │  (e.g., NVA inspection of traffic)    │
├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Complex routing           │  Unique route table per attachment     │
└────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- DRG is a **virtual router** connecting VCNs to on-premises networks (via VPN or FastConnect) and to other VCNs (local or remote)

- Supported attachment types: **VCN, RPC, IPsec tunnel, FastConnect virtual circuit, loopback, cross-tenancy**

- Two DRG route tables are **auto-created**: one for VCN attachments (learns all routes), one for RPC/VC/IPsec attachments (learns VCN routes only)

- Routes inside DRG can be **static** (manual) or **dynamic** (imported via import route distributions) — static always wins conflicts

- **Import route distribution** controls which routes are pulled INTO the DRG route table; **export route distribution** controls which routes are advertised OUT to attachments

- Statement priority in distributions: **lowest number = highest priority**

- Routes from an IPsec/VC attachment are **never exported** to other IPsec/VC attachments

- **ECMP** (off by default, enabled per route table) provides load balancing across multiple FastConnect circuits or IPsec tunnels using BGP

- Route conflict priority: Static > VCN > Virtual Circuit > IPsec > RPC

- A single DRG supports up to **300 VCNs** and enables **hub-and-spoke** transit routing architectures

================================================================================

**Site-to-Site VPN:**

**Overview:**

- **Site-to-Site VPN** (also known as **VPN Connect** or **IPsec VPN**) provides an **IPsec-encrypted connection** between an on-premises environment and an OCI Virtual Cloud Network

- Traffic traverses the **public internet**, but is **encrypted end-to-end** using the IPsec protocol

- IPsec encrypts packets before transfer and decrypts them on arrival

**IPsec Modes:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              IPsec Packet Modes                                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Packet structure:
  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │    Header     │           Payload            │
  │ (origin IP,   │  (actual data / content)     │
  │  dest IP...)  │                              │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  Transport Mode:
  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │    Header     │    [ENCRYPTED payload]        │
  │  (unchanged)  │                              │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘
  → Encrypts payload only; header stays intact

  Tunnel Mode:   [ENCRYPTS & AUTHENTICATES entire packet]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [ENCRYPTED: Header + Payload]               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  → Encrypts and authenticates BOTH header and payload

  ✓  Oracle supports TUNNEL MODE only
```

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Site-to-Site VPN Architecture                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  On-Premises                 Internet               OCI Region
  ┌──────────────┐                              ┌──────────────────┐
  │  Customer    │  ══════════════════════════► │  VCN             │
  │  Premises    │  Tunnel 1 (encrypted)        │  ┌────────────┐  │
  │  Equipment   │  ══════════════════════════► │  │    DRG     │  │
  │  (CPE)       │  Tunnel 2 (encrypted)        │  └────────────┘  │
  └──────────────┘                              └──────────────────┘
         │
  (hardware or software VPN router)

  Key: Each IPsec connection = 2 redundant tunnels (for high availability)
```

**Components:**

```
┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Component               │  Description                             │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  CPE (Customer Premises  │  Physical or software VPN router located │
│  Equipment)              │  in the on-premises environment          │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  CPE Object              │  Virtual representation of the CPE       │
│                          │  created on the OCI side; requires a     │
│                          │  name and public IP address              │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  IPsec Connection        │  Connects the CPE object to the DRG;    │
│                          │  represents the site-to-site VPN         │
│                          │  connection; creates 2 tunnels           │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  DRG                     │  VPN headend on the OCI side;            │
│                          │  attached to the VCN                     │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Tunnels (x2)            │  Each has a public IP and a pre-shared   │
│                          │  key (shared secret); provides redundancy│
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CPE public IP limit: one CPE public IP can have up to 8 IPsec connections
```

**Routing Options (configured per tunnel):**

```
  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Routing Type        │  Description                             │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Static Routing      │  Routes defined manually                 │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Dynamic Routing     │  Uses BGP for automatic route exchange   │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Policy-Based        │  Routes based on defined policies        │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Oracle recommendation: use the SAME routing type on both tunnels
```

**IKE (Internet Key Exchange):**

```
  - Oracle supports IKE version 1 and IKE version 2

  CPE behind a NAT device:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  On-Premises                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌────────────┐                              │
  │  │   CPE    │────►│  NAT device│──── (public IP of NAT) ────► │
  │  │(private  │     │            │                              │
  │  │  IP)     │     └────────────┘                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Default IKE identifier = CPE public IP address

  If CPE is behind NAT:
  → Change IKE identifier to the CPE's PRIVATE IP address
    (Oracle uses the CPE public IP by default, which won't work
     if the CPE is behind a NAT device)
```

**Advantages / Use Cases:**

```
  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Advantage              │  Detail                              │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Cost-effective         │  Uses public internet — no dedicated │
  │                         │  leased lines required               │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Quick to set up        │  Ideal for POC — no contracts or     │
  │                         │  commitments needed                  │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Encrypted              │  Secure IPsec tunnel over internet   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  High availability      │  Two redundant tunnels per           │
  │                         │  IPsec connection                    │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  FastConnect redundancy │  Can back up a FastConnect           │
  │                         │  connection                          │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Multi-site             │  Connect HQ, branches, and private   │
  │                         │  data centers to OCI                 │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Verified CPE Vendors (examples):** Cisco, Fortinet, Check Point, Juniper, Libreswan

**Key Points:**

- Site-to-Site VPN (also called VPN Connect / IPsec VPN) creates an **encrypted IPsec connection** between on-premises and OCI — traffic travels over the public internet but is secured

- Oracle supports **tunnel mode** (encrypts entire packet including header) — transport mode (payload only) is NOT supported by Oracle

- Each **IPsec connection** creates **2 redundant tunnels**, each with its own public IP and pre-shared key

- A **CPE object** is the OCI-side virtual representation of the physical/software CPE device on-premises; requires name and public IP

- One CPE public IP can support up to **8 IPsec connections**

- Routing options: **static**, **dynamic (BGP)**, or **policy-based** — configured per tunnel; Oracle recommends same type on both tunnels

- Oracle supports **IKE v1 and IKE v2**; if CPE is behind a NAT device, change the IKE identifier to the CPE's **private IP**

================================================================================

**FastConnect:**

**Overview:**

- **FastConnect** is a **dedicated and private physical connection** between an on-premises network and Oracle Cloud Infrastructure

- Unlike Site-to-Site VPN, traffic does **not** traverse the public internet

- Provides **higher bandwidth**, greater **reliability**, and more **consistent network performance**

- **No egress data transfer charges** when using FastConnect

**Key Concepts:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Concept                     │  Description                             │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  FastConnect Location        │  Oracle data center where you connect    │
│                              │  to OCI                                  │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Metro Area                  │  Geographical area with MULTIPLE          │
│                              │  FastConnect locations                   │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Oracle Partner              │  Network service provider that has       │
│                              │  already integrated with Oracle in a     │
│                              │  FastConnect location                    │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Third-Party Provider        │  Network service provider NOT on the     │
│                              │  Oracle partner list                     │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Co-location                 │  Customer's own equipment (cage +        │
│                              │  edge servers) deployed IN a             │
│                              │  FastConnect location                    │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Cross-Connect               │  PHYSICAL connection — the actual cable  │
│                              │  connecting your network to Oracle in a  │
│                              │  FastConnect location                    │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Cross-Connect Group         │  Collection of cross-connects; used when │
│                              │  bandwidth needs increase                │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Virtual Circuit             │  LOGICAL connection — isolated network   │
│                              │  path running over the physical network  │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  BGP Session                 │  Exchange of routing information between │
│                              │  two autonomous systems                  │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  BFD (Bi-directional         │  Verifies connectivity between devices;  │
│  Forwarding Detection)       │  used for failure detection between      │
│                              │  adjacent networks (does not exchange    │
│                              │  routing info)                           │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Oracle Edge                 │  Oracle's physical + logical device      │
│                              │  inside the Oracle cage at a FastConnect │
│                              │  location                                │
│                              │  • Physical device = terminates the      │
│                              │    cross-connect                         │
│                              │  • Logical device = terminates the       │
│                              │    virtual circuit                       │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  LOA (Letter of              │  Authorization issued by Oracle to       │
│  Authorization)              │  enable a third party to establish the   │
│                              │  physical connection in a FastConnect    │
│                              │  location                                │
│                              │  • NOT required for Oracle partners      │
│                              │    (connection already pre-established)  │
│                              │  • Required for third-party providers    │
│                              │    (forwarded to the carrier)            │
│                              │  • Required for co-location              │
│                              │    (given to data center technician)     │
└──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Physical vs Logical in FastConnect:**

```
  FastConnect has TWO layers:

  Physical layer → Cross-Connect
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Actual fiber cable connecting your network to Oracle   │
  │  in the FastConnect location                            │
  │  Terminated by: Oracle Edge PHYSICAL device             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Logical layer → Virtual Circuit
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Isolated network path running over the physical        │
  │  connection                                             │
  │  Terminated by: Oracle Edge LOGICAL device              │
  │  Two types: Private Virtual Circuit                     │
  │             Public Virtual Circuit                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Virtual Circuit Types:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Type                │  Description                               │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Private Virtual     │  Private communication between on-premises │
  │  Circuit             │  and a VCN (private peering)               │
  │                      │  → Requires a DRG attached to the VCN      │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Public Virtual      │  Connection from on-premises to OCI PUBLIC │
  │  Circuit             │  services — without using the internet     │
  │                      │  (public peering)                          │
  │                      │  → DRG NOT required                        │
  └──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Connectivity Models:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Model 1: FastConnect with an Oracle Partner                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Customer DC ──── (your network) ──── Partner Edge ──── Oracle Edge
                                       (already in        (FastConnect
                                        FastConnect         location)
                                        location)

  • Partner already has physical connection to Oracle FastConnect edge
  • You work with the partner to extend connection from your DC to
    the partner edge
  • NO Letter of Authorization required
  • Least expensive and most flexible model
  • OCI Console option: "Oracle Partner"

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Model 2: FastConnect Direct — Third-Party Provider                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Customer DC ──── (carrier circuit) ──── Oracle FastConnect Edge

  • You choose any network carrier (not an Oracle partner)
  • Carrier establishes the physical cross-connect to Oracle edge
  • Oracle issues you an LOA → you forward to the carrier
  • Good if data center is NOT served by any Oracle partner
  • OCI Console option: "FastConnect Direct"

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Model 3: FastConnect Direct — Co-location with Oracle              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [Your cage in FastConnect location] ──── [Oracle cage]
           (your edge servers)               (Oracle edge)

  • Your equipment is ALREADY in the FastConnect location
  • Oracle issues LOA → you give it to the data center technician
  • Technician establishes direct fiber cross-connect between your
    cage and Oracle cage
  • OCI Console option: "FastConnect Direct"
```

**Connectivity Model Comparison:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Model               │  LOA     │  Notes                           │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Oracle Partner      │  No      │  Least expensive, most flexible  │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Third-Party Provider│  Yes     │  Use when no Oracle partner      │
  │                      │          │  serves your DC                  │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Co-location         │  Yes     │  Use when you already have       │
  │                      │          │  presence at a FastConnect       │
  │                      │          │  location                        │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Site-to-Site VPN vs FastConnect Comparison:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │  Attribute           │  Site-to-Site VPN    │  FastConnect         │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │  Connection type     │  Encrypted over      │  Dedicated private   │
  │                      │  public internet     │  physical connection │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │  Bandwidth           │  Limited by internet │  Higher bandwidth    │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │  Reliability         │  Internet-dependent  │  More reliable &     │
  │                      │                      │  consistent          │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │  Routing protocol    │  BGP, Static, or     │  BGP only (always)   │
  │                      │  Policy-based        │                      │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │  Setup speed         │  Fast (no contracts) │  Slower (physical    │
  │                      │                      │  provisioning)       │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │  Cost                │  Lower               │  Higher              │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │  Egress charges      │  Yes                 │  No                  │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- FastConnect is a **dedicated, private physical connection** between on-premises and OCI — traffic does NOT traverse the internet

- Has two layers: **physical (cross-connect)** and **logical (virtual circuit)**

- Two virtual circuit types: **private** (extends on-prem into a VCN, requires DRG) and **public** (accesses OCI public services without internet, no DRG needed)

- **FastConnect always uses BGP** for route advertisements

- Three connectivity models: **Oracle Partner** (pre-established link, no LOA, cheapest), **Third-Party Provider** (LOA required, forwarded to carrier), **Co-location** (LOA required, given to DC technician)

- **LOA (Letter of Authorization)** is issued by Oracle only for third-party provider and co-location models — not needed for Oracle partner

- **BFD** detects failures between adjacent network devices; **BGP session** handles routing information exchange

- **No egress data transfer charges** with FastConnect

================================================================================

**FastConnect — Oracle Partner Connectivity Model:**

**Overview:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              FastConnect with Oracle Partner — Architecture         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Customer DC           FastConnect Location         OCI Region
  ┌──────────┐          ┌──────────────────┐        ┌────────────────┐
  │  CPE /   │──────────│  Partner Edge    │        │  VCN           │
  │  Edge    │  physical│  ┌────────────┐  │        │  ┌──────────┐  │
  │  Device  │  circuit │  │(redundant  │  │────────│  │  DRG     │  │
  └──────────┘          │  │ cross-     │  │        │  └──────────┘  │
                        │  │ connects   │  │        │                │
                        │  │ to Oracle) │  │        └────────────────┘
                        │  └────────────┘  │
                        │  Oracle Edge     │
                        └──────────────────┘

  Key facts:
  • Partner has ALREADY established redundant cross-connects to Oracle
  • NO Letter of Authorization required
  • You provision connectivity from your DC to the partner edge only
```

**BGP Session Options:**

```
  Option A: BGP session from your edge directly to Oracle
  ┌──────────┐          ┌─────────────┐          ┌───────────┐
  │ Your Edge│──────────│ Partner Edge│──────────│  Oracle   │
  └──────────┘  physical└─────────────┘   BGP   └───────────┘
                circuit       (passthrough)    ◄── BGP session here

  Option B: BGP session from your edge to Oracle partner edge
  ┌──────────┐   BGP    ┌─────────────┐          ┌───────────┐
  │ Your Edge│─────────►│ Partner Edge│──────────│  Oracle   │
  └──────────┘          └─────────────┘          └───────────┘
              ◄── BGP session here
```

**Setup Steps:**

```
  Step 1: Order the connection from the Oracle partner
          (timing varies by partner)

  Step 2: If using PRIVATE peering (private virtual circuit),
          create a Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG) and attach to VCN

  Step 3: Create one or more virtual circuits for the FastConnect
          connection — each virtual circuit gets an OCID on creation

  Step 4: Contact the partner and provide the OCID for each
          virtual circuit → partner configures their end to complete
          the logical connectivity

  Step 5: Configure your edge / CPE with BGP peering information
          (depends on which BGP session option you are using —
           to Oracle directly, or to the partner edge)

  Step 6: Verify physical layer
          • Confirm light levels are good on each physical connection
          • Confirm interfaces are up

  Step 7: Verify logical layer (BGP session)
          If BGP goes to Oracle directly:
            → Ping Oracle BGP IP address
            → Confirm BGP session is established

          If BGP goes to Oracle partner edge:
            → Ping Oracle partner's edge
            → Confirm BGP session to partner is established
            → Also ping Oracle BGP IP address

  Step 8: Test the connection end-to-end
```

**Key Points:**

- The Oracle partner has **pre-established redundant cross-connects** to Oracle in the FastConnect location — no LOA needed

- For **private peering** (private virtual circuit), a **DRG** must be created and attached to the VCN

- Each virtual circuit created generates an **OCID** — this is given to the partner to configure their side

- BGP session can terminate at **Oracle directly** or at the **partner edge** — configure your CPE accordingly

- Verification covers both **physical** (light levels, interface up) and **logical** (BGP session established) layers

- Oracle Partner model is the **least expensive and most flexible** of the three FastConnect connectivity models

================================================================================

**FastConnect — Third-Party Provider Connectivity Model:**

**Overview:**

- You work with a **network carrier of your choice** that is NOT an Oracle partner

- The third-party provider is responsible for the **physical connection** between your on-premises network and the Oracle FastConnect edge devices

- An **LOA (Letter of Authorization)** is required — Oracle issues it to you, and you forward it to the carrier

- Good choice when: you already have an existing relationship with a carrier, or Oracle partners do not serve your data center location

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│        FastConnect — Third-Party Provider Architecture              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Customer DC           Third-Party         FastConnect        OCI
                        Provider            Location
  ┌──────────┐          ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────────┐  ┌─────┐
  │  CPE /   │──────────│  Carrier │────────│  Oracle Edge │──│ DRG │
  │  Edge    │ circuit 1│          │cross-  │  (Oracle cage│  │ VCN │
  └──────────┘          └──────────┘connect │   devices)   │  └─────┘
                                    circuit2└──────────────┘

  Two physical connections:
    1. Customer DC → Third-party provider
    2. Third-party provider → Oracle FastConnect edge (cross-connect,
       provisioned by carrier using the LOA)
```

**Setup Steps:**

```
  Step 1: If using PRIVATE peering (private virtual circuit),
          create a Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG) and attach to VCN

  Step 2: Set up cross-connect GROUP and cross-connect in OCI Console
          → Oracle issues a Letter of Authorization (LOA)

  Step 3: Forward the LOA to the third-party provider / carrier

  Step 4: Third-party provider requests cabling at the FastConnect
          location (provisions the physical cross-connect on your behalf)

  Step 5: Verify physical layer after provider completes setup:
          • Confirm light levels are good for each physical connection
          • Confirm interfaces are up for each cross-connect

  Step 6: ACTIVATE each cross-connect in OCI Console
          (this informs Oracle that the physical fiber cable is ready)

  Step 7: Create one or more virtual circuits for your connection

  Step 8: Configure your edge routers with BGP peering information

  Step 9: Ping the Oracle BGP IP address
          → Confirm BGP session to Oracle is established

  Step 10: Test the connection end-to-end
```

**Key Points:**

- Third-party provider model uses a **carrier of your choice** — not an Oracle partner

- **LOA is required**: Oracle issues it → you forward it to the carrier → carrier provisions the cross-connect in the FastConnect location

- Two physical segments: (1) your DC to the carrier, and (2) carrier's cross-connect to Oracle FastConnect edge

- Cross-connects must be **activated** in OCI after physical setup to signal Oracle the fiber is ready

- **DRG** is required only for private peering (private virtual circuit)

- BGP session terminates directly at **Oracle** in this model (unlike Oracle Partner model where it can go to the partner edge)

================================================================================

**FastConnect — Co-location with Oracle:**

**Overview:**

- **Co-location** means the customer already has their **own equipment (cage + CPE/edge devices) deployed inside a FastConnect data center location** alongside Oracle's cage

- Only a **direct cross-connect** needs to be established between the customer cage and the Oracle cage — no external carrier circuit required

- Oracle issues an **LOA** → handed to the **data center technician** (not a third-party carrier) → technician configures the direct fiber cross-connect

- Best suited when you **already have a presence** at an Oracle FastConnect location

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│        FastConnect Co-location Architecture                         │
│        (everything inside the same FastConnect data center)         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FastConnect Data Center Location                              │
  │                                                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐   cross-connect   ┌────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Customer Cage     │ ◄────────────────► │  Oracle Cage   │  │
  │  │  ┌─────────────┐   │   (direct fiber)   │ ┌────────────┐ │  │
  │  │  │  Customer   │   │                   │ │  Oracle    │ │  │
  │  │  │  CPE /      │   │                   │ │  Edge      │ │  │
  │  │  │  Edge device│   │                   │ │  Devices   │ │  │
  │  │  └─────────────┘   │                   │ └────────────┘ │  │
  │  └────────────────────┘                   └────────────────┘  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                                        │
                                                   OCI Region
                                                   ┌──────────┐
                                                   │ DRG → VCN│
                                                   └──────────┘
  Redundant cross-connects recommended between customer and Oracle cage
```

**Setup Steps:**

```
  Step 1: If using PRIVATE peering (private virtual circuit),
          create a Dynamic Routing Gateway (DRG) and attach to VCN

  Step 2: Set up cross-connect GROUP and cross-connect in OCI Console
          (connection can consist of a cross-connect group or a
           single cross-connect)

  Step 3: Submit the LOA (issued by Oracle) and request cabling
          for each cross-connect at the FastConnect location
          → LOA is given to the DATA CENTER TECHNICIAN
          → Technician provisions the direct cross-connect between
            your cage and Oracle cage

  Step 4: Verify physical layer:
          • Check light levels for each cross-connect — confirm they are good
          • Confirm interfaces are up for each cross-connect

  Step 5: ACTIVATE each cross-connect in OCI Console
          (signals Oracle that the physical fiber is ready to use)

  Step 6: Create one or more virtual circuits for your connection

  Step 7: Configure your edge routers with BGP peering information
          for each virtual circuit

  Step 8: Ping the Oracle BGP IP address
          → Confirm BGP session to Oracle is established

  Step 9: Test the connection end-to-end
```

**Comparison — Three FastConnect Connectivity Models:**

```
  ┌──────────────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │  Model           │  Physical Path     │  LOA / Who receives it    │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │  Oracle Partner  │ DC → Partner Edge  │ Not required              │
  │                  │ → Oracle Edge      │                           │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │  Third-Party     │ DC → Carrier       │ Required → forwarded to   │
  │  Provider        │ → Oracle Edge      │ the carrier               │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │  Co-location     │ Customer cage      │ Required → given to DC    │
  │                  │ → Oracle cage      │ technician at FC location │
  │                  │ (direct fiber)     │                           │
  └──────────────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Co-location is suitable only when you **already have equipment deployed** in an Oracle FastConnect location

- The connection is a **direct fiber cross-connect** between your cage and Oracle cage — no external provider circuit

- **LOA** is issued by Oracle and handed to the **data center technician** (not to a carrier) to provision the cross-connect

- Steps mirror the third-party provider model but without a carrier: set up cross-connect → receive LOA → submit to DC technician → verify physical → activate → create virtual circuits → configure BGP → test

- **DRG** is required only for private peering (private virtual circuit)

- BGP session terminates directly at **Oracle** (same as third-party provider model)

================================================================================

**FastConnect — Redundancy Best Practices:**

**Oracle-Side Redundancy (built-in):**

```
  Oracle provides these redundancy guarantees out of the box:
  • Multiple Oracle partners available per region
  • Multiple physical connections between each Oracle partner and Oracle
  • At least ONE FastConnect location per region (some regions have more)
  • MINIMUM of TWO FastConnect routers per FastConnect location
```

**Physical Layout — Multiple FastConnect Locations:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              FastConnect Redundancy — Physical Overview             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

           FastConnect Location 1        FastConnect Location 2
           ┌──────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────┐
           │  Edge Device 1a      │      │  Edge Device 2a      │
           │  Edge Device 1b      │      │  Edge Device 2b      │
           └──────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────┘
                    │                              │
                    └──────────────┬───────────────┘
                                   │
                              OCI Region

  Cross-connect strategy:
    Blue  (1st): Connect to Edge Device 1a (FastConnect Location 1)
    Red   (2nd): Connect to Edge Device 2a (FastConnect Location 2)
                 — protects against entire FC Location 1 failure
    Green (3rd): Connect to Edge Device 1b / 2b within a location
                 — protects against single router failure within a location
```

**Redundancy Responsibility — Oracle Partner:**

```
  Oracle handles:
  • Redundancy of physical connections between partner and Oracle
  • Redundancy of routers in FastConnect locations (2+ routers per location)

  Customer handles:
  • Making the physical connection from their network to the Oracle
    Partner network redundant

  Partner flavors and BGP session behavior:

  ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Layer 2        │  BGP session: Customer Edge → Oracle (directly)  │
  │  Connection     │  • Each partner has ≥2 separate physical         │
  │                 │    connections to Oracle                         │
  │                 │  • Each virtual circuit goes to a DIFFERENT      │
  │                 │    physical router in the FastConnect location    │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Layer 3        │  BGP session: Customer Edge → Oracle Partner     │
  │  Connection     │  • Oracle ↔ Partner already has redundant BGP   │
  │                 │    sessions established                           │
  │                 │  • Single virtual circuit is automatically        │
  │                 │    redundant and diverse                          │
  │                 │  • Customer only needs to ensure their link to   │
  │                 │    the provider is redundant                      │
  └─────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Redundancy Responsibility — Third-Party Provider / Co-location:**

```
  Oracle handles:
  • Redundancy of routers in FastConnect locations

  Customer handles:
  • Making the physical connection between their network and Oracle redundant

  Best practice for FastConnect Direct (co-location or third-party):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FastConnect Location                                            │
  │  ┌──────────────┐   Virtual Circuit 1   ┌──────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Customer /  │ ─────────────────────►│  Edge Device 1   │   │
  │  │  Carrier     │                        ├──────────────────┤   │
  │  │  Equipment   │ ─────────────────────►│  Edge Device 2   │   │
  │  └──────────────┘   Virtual Circuit 2   └──────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  → Two virtual circuits, each on a DIFFERENT physical connection /
    edge device for redundancy
```

**Site-to-Site VPN as FastConnect Backup:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              FastConnect + VPN Backup Architecture                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  On-Premises ══════════════════════════════► OCI VCN (via DRG)
               FastConnect (private peering)     ▲
               [preferred path — blue]           │
                                                 │
  On-Premises - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - ► OCI VCN (via DRG)
               Site-to-Site VPN IPsec tunnel
               [backup path — red dotted]

  Rules:
  • FastConnect is PREFERRED over VPN when the same route and same
    routing attributes are advertised over both connections
  • Configure at least one available VPN tunnel as backup
  • Can use ECMP across multiple VPN tunnels for additional VPN bandwidth
    when FastConnect is unavailable
```

**Key Points:**

- Oracle guarantees at least **2 FastConnect routers per location** and **multiple physical connections per partner** — built-in redundancy on Oracle's side

- For **Oracle Partner Layer 2**: each virtual circuit should target a **different physical router**; BGP goes directly to Oracle

- For **Oracle Partner Layer 3**: the single virtual circuit is **automatically redundant** — Oracle-partner BGP sessions are already diverse

- For **Third-Party Provider / Co-location**: use **2 virtual circuits on 2 separate physical connections** to different edge devices

- Use **Site-to-Site VPN as a backup** to FastConnect — FastConnect is automatically preferred when the same routes are advertised over both

- **ECMP** can be enabled across multiple VPN tunnels to increase backup bandwidth when FastConnect is down

================================================================================

**Transit Routing (Hub and Spoke Topology):**

**Overview:**

- **Transit routing** enables an on-premises network to communicate with **multiple VCNs** through a single VPN/FastConnect connection — avoiding the cost and overhead of separate connections per VCN

- Implemented using a **hub-and-spoke topology**:
  - One **hub VCN** connects to the on-premises network via Site-to-Site VPN or FastConnect
  - Multiple **spoke VCNs** are locally peered to the hub VCN
  - On-premises traffic to any spoke VCN **transits through the hub VCN**

- All hub and spoke VCNs must be in the **same region** (but can be in different tenancies)

**Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Hub and Spoke Transit Routing Architecture             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

                         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
                         │  Hub VCN  (10.0.0.0/16)                 │
  On-Premises            │  ┌─────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐   │
  ┌──────────┐  VPN or   │  │ DRG │  │ LPG Hub1 │  │ LPG Hub2 │   │
  │  CPE     │ FastConnect│  └──┬──┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘   │
  │ 172.16   │◄──────────►│    │           │              │         │
  │  .0.0/12 │           │  Subnet H      │              │         │
  └──────────┘           └───────────────────────────────────────── ┘
                                           │              │
                              ┌────────────┘              └────────────┐
                              ▼                                        ▼
              ┌──────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────┐
              │  Spoke VCN 1             │      │  Spoke VCN 2             │
              │  192.168.0.0/24          │      │  192.168.1.0/24          │
              │  LPG1 — Subnet 1         │      │  LPG2 — Subnet 2         │
              └──────────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────────┘

  Traffic path: On-prem → DRG → Hub VCN → LPG Hub1/2 → Spoke VCN
```

**Route Table Configuration (6 tables required):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Route Table for Subnet 1 (in Spoke VCN 1)                      │
├──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Destination             │  Next Hop                                │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  172.16.0.0/12 (on-prem) │  LPG1 (local peering gateway)           │
│  10.0.0.0/16  (hub VCN)  │  LPG1                                   │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  2. Route Table for Subnet 2 (in Spoke VCN 2)                      │
├──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Destination             │  Next Hop                                │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  172.16.0.0/12 (on-prem) │  LPG2                                   │
│  10.0.0.0/16  (hub VCN)  │  LPG2                                   │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  3. Route Table attached to DRG (VCN route table on hub VCN's DRG) │
├──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Destination             │  Next Hop                                │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  192.168.0.0/24          │  LPG Hub 1                              │
│  192.168.1.0/24          │  LPG Hub 2                              │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Note: This is a VCN route table associated with the DRG attachment
        in the hub VCN — NOT the DRG's own internal route table.
        This is an advanced configuration; attach via VCN attachment
        → Edit → Show Advanced Options → VCN Route Table

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  4. Route Table for Subnet H (in Hub VCN)                          │
├──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Destination             │  Next Hop                                │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  172.16.0.0/12 (on-prem) │  DRG                                    │
│  192.168.0.0/24          │  LPG Hub 1                              │
│  192.168.1.0/24          │  LPG Hub 2                              │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  5. Route Table for LPG Hub 1 (attached to hub VCN LPG facing VC1) │
├──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Destination             │  Next Hop                                │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  172.16.0.0/12 (on-prem) │  DRG                                    │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  6. Route Table for LPG Hub 2 (attached to hub VCN LPG facing VC2) │
├──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Destination             │  Next Hop                                │
├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  172.16.0.0/12 (on-prem) │  DRG                                    │
└──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Constraint: Route tables attached to LPGs must use DRG or private IP
              as the route target (enforced by OCI)
```

**How to Associate Route Tables (OCI Console):**

```
  Attach VCN route table to DRG:
    DRG → VCN Attachments → [attachment] → Edit
    → Show Advanced Options → VCN Route Table → select table

  Attach route table to Local Peering Gateway:
    VCN → Local Peering Gateways → Create LPG
    → Show Advanced Options → Route Table → select table
    (rules in this table must use DRG or private IP as target)
```

**Key Points:**

- Transit routing solves the problem of connecting **multiple VCNs to on-premises** via a single VPN/FastConnect — no need for a separate connection per VCN

- Uses a **hub VCN** (connected to on-premises via DRG) and **spoke VCNs** (connected to hub via LPGs) — traffic transits through the hub

- All VCNs must be in the **same region** but can be in **different tenancies**

- Requires **6 route tables** configured across hub subnet, spoke subnets, DRG attachment, and both hub-side LPGs

- The **DRG attachment VCN route table** (not the DRG's internal table) routes incoming on-premises traffic toward the appropriate spoke LPG

- **LPG route tables** must use **DRG or private IP** as the route target — enforced by OCI

================================================================================

**OCI Load Balancer:**

**Overview:**

- A **load balancer** sits between clients and backend servers, distributing incoming traffic across multiple backends through a single entry point

- Benefits: **Scaling** (add/remove backends anytime), **Resource utilization** (distribute based on policy), **High availability** (traffic continues to healthy backends if one fails)

**Types of Load Balancers:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Type                │  Description                             │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Public              │  Has a public IP; reachable from the     │
  │                      │  internet; deployed in a public subnet   │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Private             │  Has a private IP from the hosting       │
  │                      │  subnet; visible only within the VCN     │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Supported protocols: HTTP, HTTPS, HTTP/2, TCP
  Layer support:       Layer 4 (TCP) and Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS)
```

**Core Concepts:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Backend Server                                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  The servers that generate content and receive distributed traffic
  from the load balancer (TCP or HTTP traffic)

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  2. Backend Set                                                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  A logical entity defining:
    • List of backend servers
    • Health check policy
    • Load balancing policy

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  3. Health Check Policy                                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  A test to confirm whether backend servers are available and healthy
    • TCP level  — connection attempt
    • HTTP level — request sent to a specific URI; response validated
  If a backend fails the health check → removed from rotation

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  4. Listener                                                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Checks for incoming traffic on the load balancer's IP address
  Configured with: Protocol (HTTP, HTTPS, HTTP/2, TCP) + Port number
  Rule: configure at least ONE listener per traffic type

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  5. Load Balancing Policy                                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Tells the load balancer HOW to distribute traffic among backends:

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy          │  Behavior                                    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Round Robin     │  Distributes traffic SEQUENTIALLY to each    │
  │  (default)       │  backend server in order                     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Least           │  Sends traffic to the backend with the       │
  │  Connection      │  FEWEST active connections                   │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  IP Hash         │  Uses SOURCE IP as hashing key; non-sticky   │
  │                  │  traffic from the same IP always routes to   │
  │                  │  the SAME backend server                     │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Note: Policy behavior differs for TCP, sticky sessions,
        sticky HTTP, and non-sticky HTTP

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  6. Shape (Bandwidth)                                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  OCI uses FLEXIBLE shape only (dynamic shape no longer supported)
  Specify:
    • Minimum bandwidth — provides instant readiness for load
    • Maximum bandwidth — controls cost
  Range: 10 Mbps to 8,000 Mbps

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  7. SSL Options                                                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Option             │  Behavior                                │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  SSL Termination    │  Client ──[encrypted]── LB ──[plain]──   │
  │                     │  Backend                                 │
  │                     │  LB decrypts traffic; backend receives   │
  │                     │  unencrypted traffic                     │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Point-to-Point SSL │  Client ──[encrypted]── LB ──[encrypted]─│
  │                     │  Backend                                 │
  │                     │  LB terminates SSL then re-initiates     │
  │                     │  a new SSL connection to backend         │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  SSL Tunneling      │  LB tunnels the incoming SSL directly to │
  │                     │  the backend — no termination at LB      │
  └─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  8. Session Persistence (Sticky Sessions)                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  All requests from a single client are directed to the SAME backend
  Use cases: shopping carts, login sessions
  Methods:
    • Application cookie stickiness
    • Load balancer cookie stickiness

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  9. Certificates                                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  When using HTTPS listener → associate an SSL server certificate
  with the load balancer so it can terminate the connection and
  decrypt incoming requests
```

**Advanced Routing Features (Routing Policies):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Path-Based Routing                                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Routes traffic to different backend sets based on the URL PATH

  Example:
    www.abc.com/app     → Backend Set 1
    www.abc.com/videos  → Backend Set 2
    www.abc.com/images  → Backend Set 3

  Benefit: optimizes resource utilization — different server pools
           handle different types of content

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Host-Based Routing (Content-Based Routing)                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Routes traffic to different backend sets based on the HOST header

  Example:
    host1.domain1.com → Backend Set 1
    host2.domain2.com → Backend Set 2

  Benefit: host multiple websites behind a SINGLE load balancer
```

**Architecture Summary:**

```
  Internet / Clients
          │
          ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Load Balancer (public subnet)│
  │   Listener (protocol + port)   │
  │   Routing Policy               │
  └─────────────┬──────────────────┘
                │
       ┌────────┴────────┐
       ▼                 ▼
  Backend Set 1     Backend Set 2
  (LB policy +      (LB policy +
   health check)     health check)
  [Server 1a]       [Server 2a]
  [Server 1b]       [Server 2b]
```

**Key Points:**

- Load balancer distributes traffic to multiple backends via a single entry point — provides scaling, resource utilization, and high availability

- **Public** load balancer has a public IP; **private** load balancer uses a private IP and is VCN-internal only

- A **backend set** = list of backend servers + health check policy + load balancing policy

- **Health checks** can be TCP (connection attempt) or HTTP (URI request + response validation) — unhealthy backends are removed from rotation

- **Listener** = configured with protocol + port; one listener required per traffic type

- Three **load balancing policies**: Round Robin (default), Least Connection, IP Hash

- OCI uses **flexible shape** only (10–8,000 Mbps); set minimum (readiness) and maximum (cost control)

- Three **SSL options**: SSL Termination (backend traffic unencrypted), Point-to-Point SSL (re-encrypted to backend), SSL Tunneling (pass-through)

- **Session persistence** keeps all requests from a client on the same backend — via app cookie or LB cookie stickiness

- **Path-based routing** directs by URL path; **host-based routing** directs by hostname — both configured via routing policies

================================================================================

**OCI Load Balancer — Policies:**

**Three Load Balancing Policies:**

```
┌──────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Policy          │  Description                                   │
├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Round Robin     │  DEFAULT. Distributes incoming traffic         │
│                  │  SEQUENTIALLY to each backend server.          │
│                  │  Best when:                                    │
│                  │  • Backends have similar capacity              │
│                  │  • Processing load per request does not        │
│                  │    vary significantly                          │
│                  │  Not ideal when backends have different        │
│                  │  hardware configs or varying task durations    │
├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Least           │  Sends traffic to the backend with the        │
│  Connections     │  FEWEST active connections. Balances active    │
│                  │  connection distribution. Supports weighted    │
│                  │  assignment per backend server.                │
├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  IP Hash         │  Uses INCOMING SOURCE IP as hashing key.      │
│                  │  Non-sticky traffic from the same source IP    │
│                  │  always routes to the SAME backend server.    │
│                  │  (Only non-sticky traffic is affected)         │
└──────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Weighted Backend Servers:**

- You can assign a **weight** to each backend server to influence how much traffic it receives relative to others

- Weight applies in conjunction with the selected load balancing policy (e.g., weighted Least Connections)

**How Policy Applies Differently by Traffic Type:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Policy Behavior by Traffic Type                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Traffic Type        │  How Policy is Applied                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  TCP Load Balancer   │  Policy + weight used for the INITIAL    │
  │                      │  incoming request only.                  │
  │                      │  All subsequent packets on the same      │
  │                      │  connection go to the SAME backend.      │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  HTTP — Sticky       │  Request forwarded to backend specified  │
  │  (session           │  by the COOKIE'S session information.    │
  │   persistence)       │  Policy/weight NOT re-evaluated.         │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  HTTP — Non-sticky   │  Policy + weight criteria applied to     │
  │                      │  EVERY incoming request independently.   │
  │                      │  Same client's requests can go to        │
  │                      │  different backends on different calls.  │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Visual Summary:**

```
  TCP:
    Request 1 ──[policy+weight]──► Backend 1
    Packets 2,3,4... ─────────────► Backend 1 (same connection)

  HTTP Sticky:
    Request 1 ──[cookie info]────► Backend 2
    Request 2 ──[cookie info]────► Backend 2 (same backend)

  HTTP Non-Sticky:
    Request 1 ──[policy+weight]──► Backend 1
    Request 2 ──[policy+weight]──► Backend 2  (may differ)
    Request 3 ──[policy+weight]──► Backend 3  (may differ)
```

**Key Points:**

- Three policies: **Round Robin** (default, sequential), **Least Connections** (fewest active connections, supports weights), **IP Hash** (source IP → same backend for non-sticky traffic)

- **Round Robin** works best when backends have similar capacity and request processing loads do not vary significantly

- **Backend weights** can be assigned to any server and influence traffic distribution alongside the chosen policy

- Policy is applied **differently** depending on traffic type:
  - **TCP**: policy used once for first packet; subsequent packets follow same connection
  - **HTTP Sticky**: backend determined by cookie session info, not the policy
  - **HTTP Non-sticky**: policy evaluated on every individual request

================================================================================

**OCI Load Balancer — Health Checks:**

**Overview:**

- A **health check** continuously monitors backend servers to confirm whether they are available and healthy

- If a backend server fails health checks, the load balancer **temporarily removes it from rotation** — no traffic is sent to it until it recovers

- Health check is configured and activated for the **backend**, the **backend set**, and the **overall load balancer**

**Health Check Types:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Type                │  Method                                  │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  TCP level           │  Connection attempt                      │
  │                      │  → tries to establish a TCP connection   │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  HTTP level          │  HTTP request to a specific URI          │
  │                      │  → validates the response                │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Best practice: match health check protocol to the application/service
```

**Health Check Configuration Parameters:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Parameter               │  Description                         │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Protocol                │  HTTP or TCP                         │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Interval (ms)           │  How frequently to run the health    │
  │                          │  check. Default: 10,000 ms (10 sec)  │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Timeout (ms)            │  Maximum time to wait for a reply.   │
  │                          │  If reply received within timeout    │
  │                          │  → health check is successful.       │
  │                          │  Default: 3,000 ms (3 sec)           │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Number of Retries       │  Number of failed attempts before    │
  │                          │  marking backend as UNHEALTHY.       │
  │                          │  Also applies when RECOVERING a      │
  │                          │  server to healthy state.            │
  │                          │  Default: 3                          │
  └──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Health Check Flow:**

```
  Load Balancer ──── health check request ────► Backend Server
                ◄─── response (within timeout)──

  If response received within timeout → SUCCESS (attempt counts toward
                                         recovery threshold)
  If no response within timeout       → FAILURE (attempt counts toward
                                         unhealthy threshold)

  After 3 consecutive failures (default):
    Backend Server → marked UNHEALTHY → removed from rotation

  After 3 consecutive successes (default) during recovery:
    Backend Server → marked HEALTHY → returned to rotation
```

**Key Points:**

- Health checks run **continuously** at a configurable interval (default 10 seconds)

- Two protocols: **TCP** (connection attempt) and **HTTP** (request to URI + response validation) — choose the one matching your application

- **Timeout** = max wait time for a response; if exceeded, the attempt counts as a failure (default 3 seconds)

- **Number of Retries** (default 3) controls both the threshold to mark a server **unhealthy** AND the threshold to return it to **healthy** state

- Unhealthy backends are taken **out of rotation** temporarily — traffic continues to healthy servers; recovered servers are automatically returned

================================================================================

**OCI Load Balancer — Public Load Balancer:**

**Overview:**

- A **public load balancer** is deployed in a **public subnet** and is assigned a **public IP address**, making it reachable directly from the internet

- **Scope: Regional** — a public load balancer spans the region, not a single availability domain

**Subnet Requirements:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Subnet Type             │  Requirement                         │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Regional subnet         │  One regional public subnet          │
  │  (preferred)             │  → OCI places primary and standby    │
  │                          │    LBs in different ADs              │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  AD-specific subnets     │  Two AD-specific public subnets      │
  │  (if no regional subnet) │  → Primary in one AD, standby in    │
  │                          │    the other AD                      │
  └──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**High Availability — Primary and Standby Load Balancers:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Public Load Balancer HA Architecture                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Multi-AD Region
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VCN (spans all ADs)                                            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Public Subnet (regional or 2x AD-specific)                     │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────┐             │
  │  │  AD 1               │   │  AD 2               │             │
  │  │  ┌───────────────┐  │   │  ┌───────────────┐  │             │
  │  │  │ Primary LB    │  │   │  │ Standby LB    │  │             │
  │  │  │ (active)      │  │   │  │ (passive)     │  │             │
  │  │  │ private IP    │  │   │  │ private IP    │  │             │
  │  │  └───────────────┘  │   │  └───────────────┘  │             │
  │  └─────────────────────┘   └─────────────────────┘             │
  │            ▲                                                    │
  │       Floating Public IP (attached to active LB)                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                    │
          Internet traffic
```

**Failover Behavior:**

```
  Normal operation:
    Internet → Floating Public IP → Primary (active) LB → Backends

  AD 1 outage:
    Internet → Floating Public IP switches to Standby LB → Backends
    (standby LB takes over; public IP floats to it)
```

**IP Address Requirements:**

```
  Each load balancer (primary + standby) requires:
    • 1 private IP address from its host subnet
    • 1 shared floating public IP address (switches on failover)

  Total: 2 private IPs (one per LB instance) + 1 floating public IP
```

**Important Notes:**

```
  • Both load balancers are technically EQUIVALENT — you cannot
    designate which is "primary" and which is "standby"
  • The terms "primary/active" and "standby/secondary" are
    conceptual only; OCI manages failover automatically
  • Public load balancer CANNOT be deployed in a private subnet
```

**Key Points:**

- Public load balancer requires a **public subnet** and gets a **public IP** — accepts traffic directly from the internet

- **Regional scope**: requires either a **regional subnet** (preferred) or **two AD-specific subnets** in a multi-AD region

- OCI automatically provisions a **primary and standby** load balancer in different ADs — both are equivalent; you cannot designate which is primary

- A **floating public IP** is attached to the active load balancer; on AD failure, the IP **switches to the standby** automatically

- Each LB instance needs **one private IP** from its host subnet (2 total for the pair) plus the shared floating public IP

================================================================================

**OCI Load Balancer — Private Load Balancer:**

**Overview:**

- A **private load balancer** is deployed in a **private subnet** and is assigned a **private IP address** as its entry point for incoming traffic

- It is **isolated from the internet** — accessible only from within the VCN that contains the host subnet

- Use case: improve security posture by keeping the load balancer off the public internet (e.g., internal microservices, backend tier load balancing)

**Subnet Requirement:**

```
  Unlike public load balancer (which requires 1 regional OR 2 AD-specific
  subnets), a private load balancer requires only ONE subnet to host
  both the primary and standby load balancers.
```

**IP Address Requirements:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Private Load Balancer IP Allocation                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Private load balancer consumes 3 private IP addresses from host subnet:

    1. Private IP for the PRIMARY load balancer instance
    2. Private IP for the STANDBY load balancer instance
    3. Floating private IP (switches between primary and standby
       on failover — this is the entry point IP used by clients)

  Total: 3 private IP addresses from the host subnet
         (no public IP address)
```

**Public vs Private Load Balancer Comparison:**

```
  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │  Attribute              │  Public LB           │  Private LB      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Subnet type            │  Public subnet       │  Private subnet  │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  IP address             │  Public IP           │  Private IP      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Internet accessible    │  Yes                 │  No (VCN only)   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Subnets required       │  1 regional OR       │  1 subnet        │
  │                         │  2 AD-specific       │  (any)           │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Floating IP type       │  Floating public IP  │  Floating        │
  │                         │                      │  private IP      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  IPs consumed from      │  2 private IPs +     │  3 private IPs   │
  │  host subnet            │  1 floating public   │  (no public IP)  │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Private load balancer is deployed in a **private subnet** — not reachable from the internet, only from within the VCN

- Requires only **one subnet** (unlike public LB which needs regional or two AD-specific subnets)

- Consumes **3 private IP addresses** from the host subnet: one for primary LB, one for standby LB, one floating private IP (the client entry point)

- The **floating private IP** switches to the standby LB on failover — same behavior as the floating public IP in a public LB

================================================================================

**OCI Network Load Balancer:**

**Overview:**

- **Network Load Balancer (NLB)** operates at the **connection level (Layer 3 / Layer 4)**

- Key characteristic: **preserves source and destination IP addresses and port** — unlike the standard load balancer which may modify these

- Provides **high throughput with ultra-low latency** — designed to handle volatile traffic patterns

- **Elastically scalable** — automatically scales up and down based on client traffic; no bandwidth configuration required

- Ideal for **latency-sensitive workloads**

**Load Balancing Policies:**

```
  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │  Policy              │  Hash Key            │  Description       │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │  5-tuple hash        │  Source IP           │  DEFAULT policy.   │
  │  (default)           │  Source Port         │  Routes based on   │
  │                      │  Destination IP      │  all 5 fields.     │
  │                      │  Destination Port    │                    │
  │                      │  Protocol            │                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │  3-tuple hash        │  Source IP           │  Routes based on   │
  │                      │  Destination IP      │  3 fields only     │
  │                      │  Protocol            │                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │  2-tuple hash        │  Source IP           │  Routes based on   │
  │                      │  Destination IP      │  source and dest   │
  │                      │                      │  IP only           │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────────┘
```

**Network Load Balancer vs Load Balancer Comparison:**

```
  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │  Attribute              │  Load Balancer       │  Network LB      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  OSI Layer              │  Layer 4 + Layer 7   │  Layer 3 + 4     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Source IP preserved    │  No                  │  Yes             │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Bandwidth config       │  Required (flexible  │  Not required    │
  │                         │  shape, 10–8000 Mbps)│  (elastic)       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Latency                │  Standard            │  Ultra-low       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  Protocols              │  HTTP, HTTPS,        │  TCP, UDP        │
  │                         │  HTTP/2, TCP         │                  │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │  LB policies            │  Round Robin,        │  5-tuple hash,   │
  │                         │  Least Connection,   │  3-tuple hash,   │
  │                         │  IP Hash             │  2-tuple hash    │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Network Load Balancer operates at **Layer 3/4 (connection level)** and **preserves source/destination IP and port**

- Provides **high throughput and ultra-low latency** — best for latency-sensitive and volatile traffic workloads

- **Elastically scalable** — no need to configure minimum/maximum bandwidth (unlike the standard flexible-shape LB)

- Three load balancing policies: **5-tuple hash** (default — source IP, source port, dest IP, dest port, protocol), **3-tuple hash** (source IP, dest IP, protocol), **2-tuple hash** (source IP + dest IP only)

================================================================================

**Web Application Acceleration (WAA):**

**Overview:**

- **Web Application Acceleration** is a service that speeds up traffic on a **Layer 7 (HTTP) load balancer** using **caching** and **compression**

- Goals: reduce application latency, reduce load on backend servers, decrease network bandwidth usage, improve end-user experience

- Works **only** with Layer 7 load balancers (not Network Load Balancer)

**How It Works — Setup:**

```
  Step 1: Create a Web Application Acceleration POLICY
