```
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Example PAR URL:                                          │
   │  https://objectstorage.<region>.oraclecloud.com/p/        │
   │  <unique-token>/n/<namespace>/b/<bucket>/o/<object>       │
   │                                                            │
   │  Components:                                               │
   │  • /p/<token> - Pre-authenticated request token           │
   │  • Unique, non-guessable token                            │
   │  • Embedded permissions and expiration                     │
   │  • Cryptographically secure                                │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Use Cases:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Pre-Authenticated Request Use Cases                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Business Partner Collaboration
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Share quarterly financial reports with business partner│
   │  • Partner doesn't have OCI account                       │
   │  • Need secure, temporary access                          │
   │                                                           │
   │  Solution:                                                │
   │  • Create PAR with Read access to reports folder          │
   │  • Set 7-day expiration                                   │
   │  • Share URL via secure email                             │
   │  • Partner downloads reports without credentials          │
   │  • Access automatically expires after 7 days              │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Customer File Uploads
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Customers need to upload documents (forms, contracts)  │
   │  • Don't want customers to have OCI accounts              │
   │  • Need simple upload mechanism                           │
   │                                                           │
   │  Solution:                                                │
   │  • Create PAR with Write access to submissions bucket     │
   │  • Embed PAR URL in web application form                  │
   │  • Customers upload directly to Object Storage            │
   │  • No backend server processing required                  │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Public Content Distribution
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Distribute software downloads, documentation, media    │
   │  • Need public access without making bucket public        │
   │  • Want ability to revoke access if needed                │
   │                                                           │
   │  Solution:                                                │
   │  • Create PAR with Read access to distribution files      │
   │  • Publish PAR URL on website or documentation            │
   │  • Users download without authentication                  │
   │  • Can revoke by deleting PAR if needed                   │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. Third-Party Integration
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Integrate with external systems or services            │
   │  • External system needs to fetch/upload data             │
   │  • Don't want to manage API keys in external system       │
   │                                                           │
   │  Solution:                                                │
   │  • Create PAR with appropriate access type                │
   │  • Configure external system with PAR URL                 │
   │  • External system accesses via standard HTTP             │
   │  • Rotate PAR periodically for security                   │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5. Temporary Data Sharing
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Share large files temporarily (too big for email)      │
   │  • Need secure sharing without file sharing services      │
   │  • Want automatic access expiration                       │
   │                                                           │
   │  Solution:                                                │
   │  • Upload file to Object Storage                          │
   │  • Create PAR with Read access and 24-hour expiration     │
   │  • Share URL with recipient                               │
   │  • File automatically becomes inaccessible after 24 hours │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

6. Static Website Hosting
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Host static website content (HTML, CSS, JS, images)    │
   │  • Need public read access to website files               │
   │  • Want control over access permissions                   │
   │                                                           │
   │  Solution:                                                │
   │  • Create PAR with Read access to website content         │
   │  • Configure long expiration (e.g., 1 year)               │
   │  • Use PAR URL as base for website access                 │
   │  • Rotate PAR annually or as needed                       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Security Considerations:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              PAR Security Best Practices                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Principle of Least Privilege
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ✓ Grant minimum necessary access type                    │
   │  ✓ Use Read-only when write not needed                    │
   │  ✓ Limit to specific object vs entire bucket when possible│
   │  ✓ Use prefix-based access for directory-level control    │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Short Expiration Periods
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ✓ Set shortest expiration that meets requirements        │
   │  ✓ Shorter durations reduce exposure window               │
   │  ✓ Can always create new PAR if needed                    │
   │  ✓ Avoid indefinite or very long expiration periods       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. URL Protection
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ⚠️  PAR URL grants access to anyone who has it            │
   │                                                            │
   │  ✓ Share via secure channels (encrypted email, etc.)      │
   │  ✓ Don't publish in public forums or logs                 │
   │  ✓ Treat PAR URLs like passwords                          │
   │  ✓ Consider using HTTPS-only applications                 │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. PAR Lifecycle Management
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ✓ Delete PARs when no longer needed (don't wait for      │
   │    expiration)                                             │
   │  ✓ Maintain inventory of active PARs                      │
   │  ✓ Regular audit of PAR usage                             │
   │  ✓ Document purpose and intended users for each PAR       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5. Creator Account Management
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ✓ Use service accounts (not personal accounts) to        │
   │    create PARs                                             │
   │  ✓ Ensure creator accounts have stable permissions        │
   │  ✓ Monitor creator account status                         │
   │  ✓ Plan for creator account changes                       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

6. Monitoring and Auditing
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ✓ Enable Object Storage access logging                   │
   │  ✓ Monitor PAR usage patterns                             │
   │  ✓ Alert on unusual access volumes                        │
   │  ✓ Review audit logs for PAR-based access                 │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Managing Pre-Authenticated Requests:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              PAR Management Operations                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Creating PAR:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Via OCI Console:                                        │
│  1. Navigate to bucket or object                         │
│  2. Click "Pre-Authenticated Requests"                   │
│  3. Click "Create Pre-Authenticated Request"             │
│  4. Configure target, access type, expiration            │
│  5. Copy generated URL (only shown once!)                │
│                                                          │
│  Via CLI/API:                                            │
│  • Use OCI CLI or REST API                               │
│  • Programmatically create PARs                          │
│  • Integrate PAR creation into workflows                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Viewing Active PARs:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • List all PARs for bucket or object                    │
│  • View PAR name, expiration, access type                │
│  • Check which PARs are active vs expired                │
│  • Note: Cannot view URL after creation                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Revoking PAR:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Delete PAR to immediately revoke access               │
│  • Access revoked even if expiration not reached         │
│  • Cannot un-delete PAR (must create new one)            │
│  • Users with URL will get access denied                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

⚠️  IMPORTANT: PAR URL shown only once during creation
    • Copy and save URL immediately
    • Cannot retrieve URL later
    • Must create new PAR if URL lost
```

**PAR vs Other Access Methods:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
│  Feature                            │   PAR    │  Public  │   IAM    │
│                                     │          │  Bucket  │  Policy  │
├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
│  Requires OCI Credentials           │    ✗     │    ✗     │    ✓     │
│  Time-Limited Access                │    ✓     │    ✗     │    ✗     │
│  Granular Object Control            │    ✓     │    ✗     │    ✓     │
│  Can Be Revoked Early               │    ✓     │    N/A   │    ✓     │
│  External User Access               │    ✓     │    ✓     │    ✗     │
│  Supports Write Operations          │    ✓     │    ✗*    │    ✓     │
│  Best Practice for Sharing          │    ✓     │    ✗     │    ✓**   │
│  Audit Trail                        │    ✓     │    ✓     │    ✓     │
└─────────────────────────────────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘

* Public buckets typically read-only for security
** For internal OCI users with accounts
```

**Key Benefits:**

- **No Credentials Required:** External users access without OCI accounts or API keys

- **Temporary Access:** Automatic expiration reduces long-term exposure

- **Granular Control:** Specify exact bucket, object, or prefix to share

- **Flexible Access Types:** Support read, write, or both operations

- **Standard Tools:** Works with any HTTP-capable tool or application

- **Security:** No need to make entire bucket public for selective sharing

- **Revocable:** Can delete PAR to immediately revoke access

- **Audit Support:** Access via PAR logged in Object Storage audit logs

**Important Limitations:**

- **Cannot Edit:** Once created, PAR cannot be modified (must create new one)

- **Creator Dependency:** PAR stops working if creator loses permissions or is deleted

- **No Deletion:** PARs cannot be used to delete buckets or objects

- **URL Security:** Anyone with URL has access (treat URLs as sensitive)

- **One-Time Display:** URL shown only during creation (cannot be retrieved later)

**Summary:**

- Pre-Authenticated Requests enable secure, temporary, credential-free access to Object Storage

- Alternative to making buckets public while maintaining security control

- Generates unique URL with embedded permissions and expiration

- Three access types: Read, Write, or Read+Write (deletion not supported)

- Expiration date required but can be set as far in future as needed

- Cannot edit PAR after creation - must create new one for changes

- PAR validity depends on creator maintaining required permissions

- Works with standard HTTP tools - no special OCI tools needed

- Ideal for business partner sharing, customer uploads, and temporary file sharing

- Security best practices: least privilege, short expirations, secure URL handling

================================================================================

**Object Lifecycle Management:**

**Overview:**

- Lets you manage lifecycle of object storage data through automated archiving and deletion

- Reduces storage costs and saves time through automation

- Works by taking automated actions based on rules you define

- Rule-based system for managing object transitions and deletions

**Supported Lifecycle Actions:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Three Lifecycle Actions                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Action 1: Move to Infrequent Access Tier
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Transitions objects from Standard to Infrequent Access│
│  • Reduces storage costs for less-accessed data          │
│  • Immediate availability maintained                     │
│  • Subject to 31-day minimum retention in IA tier        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Action 2: Move to Archive Tier
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Transitions objects to Archive tier                   │
│  • Lowest storage cost option                            │
│  • Restore required before access (~1 hour)              │
│  • Subject to 90-day minimum retention in Archive tier   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Action 3: Delete
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Permanently deletes objects                           │
│  • Frees up storage space                                │
│  • Irreversible action                                   │
│  • Highest priority action (overrides move actions)      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Note: Delete rules always take priority over move rules
      for the same object
```

**Supported Resources:**

- **Objects:** Regular objects in bucket

- **Object Versions:** Previous versions of objects (when versioning enabled or suspended)

- **Uncommitted or Failed Multipart Uploads:** Incomplete multipart upload operations

**Object Name Filtering:**

- Object-related rules can include object name prefix and pattern matching conditions

- Narrows scope of individual lifecycle policy rules

- Allows precise targeting of specific objects or object groups

**Rule Configuration and Limits:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Lifecycle Rule Configuration                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Maximum Rules per Bucket:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Up to 1,000 lifecycle rules per bucket                  │
│  Allows complex, fine-grained lifecycle management       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Rule Priority:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Delete rules ALWAYS take priority over move rules       │
│  If same object matches both delete and move rules:      │
│  → Delete action executes (move action ignored)          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Auto-Tiering Restriction:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  If auto-tiering is enabled on bucket:                   │
│  → CANNOT create rules that move objects to Infrequent   │
│    Access tier                                           │
│  Reason: Auto-tiering already manages Standard ↔ IA      │
│          transitions automatically                       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Archive Storage Bucket Restriction:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Archive storage buckets:                                │
│  → Only DELETE rules are supported                       │
│  → Cannot create move rules (all objects already Archive)│
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Retroactive Application:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│         Lifecycle Policies Apply to Existing Data                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Question: What happens to data uploaded before policy creation?

Answer: Lifecycle policies apply RETROACTIVELY
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Timeline Example:                                          │
│                                                             │
│  Day 1: Upload objects to bucket                           │
│  Day 40: Objects are now 40 days old                       │
│  Day 40: Create lifecycle policy:                          │
│          "Archive objects older than 30 days"              │
│                                                             │
│  Result: Objects immediately identified as candidates       │
│          for archiving (already 40 days old, exceeds        │
│          30-day threshold)                                  │
│          Archiving process begins immediately              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key Point: Policies evaluate object age from creation/last update
           regardless of when policy was created
```

**Object Name Filters:**

**Two Types of Filters:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Object Name Filter Types                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Filter Type 1: Prefix Matching
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Definition:                                             │
│  • Exact matching of left-most characters of object name │
│  • Does NOT support wildcard characters                  │
│                                                          │
│  Example:                                                │
│  Prefix: "training/oci"                                  │
│                                                          │
│  Matches:                                                │
│  ✓ training/oci/os.jpg                                   │
│  ✓ training/oci/compute.pdf                              │
│  ✓ training/oci/networking/vpc.doc                       │
│                                                          │
│  Does NOT Match:                                         │
│  ✗ training/aws/ec2.jpg (different prefix)               │
│  ✗ oci/training/test.jpg (wrong order)                   │
│  ✗ mytraining/oci/test.jpg (extra chars before)          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Filter Type 2: Pattern Matching
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Definition:                                             │
│  • Matching on entire object name                        │
│  • Supports wildcard characters (*)                      │
│                                                          │
│  Example:                                                │
│  Pattern: "*.jpg"                                        │
│                                                          │
│  Matches:                                                │
│  ✓ oci.jpg                                               │
│  ✓ training/oci/test.jpg                                 │
│  ✓ images/photos/vacation.jpg                            │
│  ✓ backup/2026/file123.jpg                               │
│                                                          │
│  Does NOT Match:                                         │
│  ✗ oci.png (different extension)                         │
│  ✗ test.jpeg (different extension)                       │
│  ✗ document.pdf (different extension)                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key Differences:
- Prefix: Left-most chars only, no wildcards
- Pattern: Entire name, supports wildcards
```

**Filter Precedence Rules:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│         Rule Precedence During Object Name Filter Evaluation       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Three Filter Types (in order of evaluation):
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Priority 1: EXCLUDE by Pattern (Highest Priority)                │
│  ├─ Pattern exclusions evaluated FIRST                             │
│  ├─ If object matches exclusion pattern → STOP, exclude object     │
│  └─ Object will NOT be affected by lifecycle rule                  │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓ (If not excluded)
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Priority 2: INCLUDE by Pattern (Medium Priority)                 │
│  ├─ Pattern inclusions evaluated SECOND                            │
│  ├─ If object matches inclusion pattern → Include for rule         │
│  └─ Object will be affected by lifecycle rule action               │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓ (If not matched)
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Priority 3: INCLUDE by Prefix (Lowest Priority)                  │
│  ├─ Prefix inclusions evaluated LAST                               │
│  ├─ If object matches prefix → Include for rule                    │
│  └─ Object will be affected by lifecycle rule action               │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Evaluation Order Summary:
1. Pattern Exclusions (excludes override everything)
2. Pattern Inclusions (specific pattern matches)
3. Prefix Inclusions (general prefix matches)

Example Scenario:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Filters configured:                                     │
│  • Exclude by pattern: "*.temp"                          │
│  • Include by pattern: "*.jpg"                           │
│  • Include by prefix: "images/"                          │
│                                                          │
│  Object: "images/photo.temp"                             │
│  Evaluation:                                             │
│  1. Matches "*.temp" exclusion → EXCLUDED                │
│  Result: Not affected by rule (exclusion wins)           │
│                                                          │
│  Object: "images/photo.jpg"                              │
│  Evaluation:                                             │
│  1. No exclusion match                                   │
│  2. Matches "*.jpg" inclusion → INCLUDED                 │
│  Result: Affected by rule (pattern inclusion)            │
│                                                          │
│  Object: "images/document.pdf"                           │
│  Evaluation:                                             │
│  1. No exclusion match                                   │
│  2. No pattern inclusion match                           │
│  3. Matches "images/" prefix → INCLUDED                  │
│  Result: Affected by rule (prefix inclusion)             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Supported Lifecycle Policy Options:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Lifecycle Policy Rule Options                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Option 1: Move or Delete ALL Objects in Bucket
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Applies to every object in the bucket                 │
│  • No filtering - blanket application                    │
│  • Simplest option for bucket-wide policies              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Option 2: Move or Delete Objects Matching Filters
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Apply to objects matching specified name filters      │
│  • Can use object name prefixes                          │
│  • Can use pattern matching                              │
│  • Targeted, granular control                            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Option 3: Delete Uncommitted or Failed Multipart Uploads
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Clean up incomplete multipart uploads                 │
│  • Delete after specified number of days                 │
│  • Prevents storage waste from abandoned uploads         │
│  • Storage cost optimization                             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Option 4: With Object Versioning (Enabled or Suspended)
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Additional options when versioning enabled/suspended:   │
│                                                          │
│  • Move or delete PREVIOUS versions of all objects       │
│  • Move or delete LATEST version of objects              │
│  • Can also specify object name filters for versions     │
│  • Fine-grained version lifecycle management             │
│                                                          │
│  Example use cases:                                      │
│  - Delete previous versions after 90 days                │
│  - Archive previous versions after 30 days               │
│  - Keep only latest 5 versions                           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Lifecycle Policy Example:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│         Example: Multi-Rule Lifecycle Policy                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Bucket: Production Data Bucket

Rule 1: Archive Standard Tier Objects
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Action: Move to Archive Tier                            │
│  Condition: 30 days after creation or last update        │
│  Target: All objects in Standard tier                    │
│  Result: Reduces storage costs for aging data            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓ 30 days later
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Object State: Now in Archive tier                       │
│  Cost: Lowest storage tier pricing                       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓ 150 days later (180 days total)
Rule 2: Delete Archived Objects
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Action: Delete                                          │
│  Condition: 180 days after creation or last update       │
│  Target: All archived objects                            │
│  Result: Frees storage space, eliminates costs           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Rule 3: Clean Up Failed Multipart Uploads
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Action: Delete                                          │
│  Condition: 5 days after initiation                      │
│  Target: Uncommitted or failed multipart uploads         │
│  Result: Prevents wasted storage on incomplete uploads   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Combined Workflow Visualization:
Day 0: Object created in Standard tier
Day 30: → Moved to Archive tier (Rule 1)
Day 180: → Deleted permanently (Rule 2)

Multipart Upload Cleanup:
Upload initiated → Not completed after 5 days → Deleted (Rule 3)

Benefits:
• Automated cost optimization
• No manual intervention required
• Predictable data lifecycle
• Storage waste prevention
```

**Key Features Summary:**

- **Automated Management:** Set rules once, automated execution ongoing

- **Cost Optimization:** Move data to cheaper tiers or delete to save costs

- **Time Savings:** No manual archiving or deletion required

- **Flexible Filtering:** Prefix and pattern matching for precise targeting

- **Retroactive Application:** Policies apply to existing data immediately

- **Multi-part Upload Cleanup:** Automatically delete failed uploads to prevent waste

- **Version Support:** Manage lifecycle of versioned objects separately

- **Rule Priority:** Delete actions override move actions for safety

**Use Cases:**

- **Compliance Retention:** Automatically delete data after regulatory retention period expires

- **Cost Optimization:** Move aging data to cheaper tiers, delete when no longer needed

- **Storage Management:** Clean up temporary files, logs, or test data automatically

- **Version Control:** Delete old object versions to reduce storage costs

- **Multipart Upload Maintenance:** Prevent storage waste from incomplete uploads

- **Data Archival:** Automatically archive historical data for long-term retention

**Best Practices:**

- Start with broader rules, add specific filters as needed

- Use pattern exclusions to protect critical data from deletion

- Set up multipart upload cleanup to prevent storage waste (recommend 5-7 days)

- Consider combining with auto-tiering for optimal cost management

- Test rules on small dataset before applying bucket-wide

- Monitor lifecycle rule execution through OCI Console or API

- Use versioning lifecycle rules to manage storage costs of previous versions

- Set delete rules after sufficient archive period to ensure compliance

**Key Takeaways:**

- Object lifecycle management automates archiving and deletion based on defined rules

- Three actions supported: Move to Infrequent Access, Move to Archive, Delete

- Applies to objects, object versions, and uncommitted/failed multipart uploads

- Up to 1,000 rules per bucket for fine-grained control

- Delete rules always take priority over move rules

- Cannot create Infrequent Access move rules if auto-tiering enabled

- Archive storage buckets only support delete rules

- Policies apply retroactively to existing data in bucket

- Two filter types: Prefix matching (exact left chars, no wildcards) and Pattern matching (entire name, wildcards supported)

- Filter precedence: Pattern exclusions > Pattern inclusions > Prefix inclusions

- Supports versioning with separate rules for previous and latest versions

- Multipart upload cleanup prevents storage waste

- Reduces costs, saves time, ensures compliance through automation

================================================================================

**Object Versioning:**

**Overview:**

- Directs object storage to automatically create an object version each time a new object is uploaded, an existing object is overwritten, or when an object is deleted

- Enabled at the bucket level

- Can enable object versioning at bucket creation time or later

- Cannot disable object versioning once enabled (can only suspend it)

- Provides data protection against accidental or malicious object update, overwrite, or deletion

**Key Characteristics:**

- There is always one latest version of the object and zero or more previous versions

- Charged for all the latest object versions and previous object versions

- Previous object versions are retained until you explicitly delete them

- Each uploaded object is assigned a unique version identifier (when versioning enabled)

**Three Versioning Statuses:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Object Versioning Status Options                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Status 1: Disabled (Default)                            │
│                                                          │
│  Upload Behavior:                                        │
│  • Upload object with same name as existing object       │
│    → Object is overwritten                               │
│    → Overwritten object NOT retained or recoverable      │
│                                                          │
│  Delete Behavior:                                        │
│  • Delete object → Deletion is permanent                 │
│    → Objects cannot be recovered                         │
│                                                          │
│  ⚠ Warning: No protection, no recovery possible          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Status 2: Enabled                                       │
│                                                          │
│  Upload Behavior:                                        │
│  • Upload object with same name as existing object       │
│    → Existing object becomes previous version            │
│    → Newly uploaded object becomes latest version        │
│  • Each object assigned unique version identifier        │
│                                                          │
│  Delete Behavior:                                        │
│  • Delete object → Object storage retains version        │
│  • Delete marker created (latest version becomes marker) │
│  • Previous versions retained                            │
│                                                          │
│  ✓ Full protection with version history                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Status 3: Suspended                                     │
│                                                          │
│  Upload Behavior:                                        │
│  • Same as Disabled status                               │
│  • Upload overwrites without creating versions           │
│                                                          │
│  Delete Behavior:                                        │
│  • Same as Disabled status                               │
│  • Deletion is permanent                                 │
│                                                          │
│  Previous Versions:                                      │
│  • Object versions created BEFORE suspension retained    │
│  • Retained versions persist unless explicitly deleted   │
│  • Can re-enable versioning at any time                  │
│                                                          │
│  ℹ Preserves history, pauses new version creation        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Versioning Interaction with Other Object Storage Features:**

**1. Bucket Re-encryption:**

- When you perform bucket re-encryption, existing versions are re-encrypted

- All object versions (both latest and previous) updated with new encryption

**2. Lifecycle Policies:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│         Lifecycle Policies with Versioning Behavior                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Archive Latest Version:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Lifecycle policy can archive latest version           │
│  • Lifecycle policy can archive previous versions        │
│  • Moves versions to Archive tier based on age rules     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Delete Latest Version:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  When lifecycle policy deletes latest version:           │
│  • Object becomes a previous version                     │
│  • Delete marker is created                              │
│  • Object NOT permanently deleted                        │
│  • Can be recovered by removing delete marker            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Delete Previous Version:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  When lifecycle policy deletes previous version:         │
│  • Deletion is PERMANENT                                 │
│  • Previous version cannot be recovered                  │
│  • Use to clean up old versions and reduce costs         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**3. Copy Object Functionality:**

- Only the selected version will be copied

- Cannot copy all versions at once

- Must specify which version to copy when using copy object feature

**4. Replication:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│         Versioning Limitations with Replication                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Replication Cannot Replicate Previous Object Versions:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Replication only replicates latest version            │
│  • Previous versions NOT replicated to destination       │
│  • Only current/latest object version synced             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Cannot Enable Versioning on Replication Destination Bucket:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Destination bucket is read-only                       │
│  • Cannot enable versioning on destination bucket        │
│  • Destination controlled entirely by source bucket      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**5. Retention Rules:**

- Cannot enable versioning on a bucket with active retention rules

- Retention rules and versioning are mutually exclusive features

- Must remove retention rules before enabling versioning

**Versioning Benefits:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Key Benefits of Object Versioning                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Protection Against Accidental Deletion:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Objects not permanently deleted                       │
│  • Delete marker created instead                         │
│  • Can recover by removing delete marker                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Protection Against Accidental Overwrites:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Previous versions automatically preserved             │
│  • Can retrieve earlier version if needed                │
│  • Complete version history maintained                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Protection Against Malicious Actions:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Malicious updates or deletions don't destroy data     │
│  • Version history provides audit trail                  │
│  • Can rollback to known good version                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Rollback Capability:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Easily revert to any previous version                 │
│  • Useful for undoing mistakes or changes                │
│  • No data loss from bad updates                         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Cost Considerations:**

- Charged for storage of ALL versions (latest + all previous versions)

- Previous versions consume storage space until explicitly deleted

- Use lifecycle policies to automatically delete old versions after retention period

- Balance data protection needs with storage costs

**Example Versioning + Lifecycle Strategy:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│         Recommended: Versioning + Lifecycle Combination             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Setup:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Enable versioning on bucket                          │
│     → Protects against accidental deletion/overwrite     │
│                                                          │
│  2. Create lifecycle policy for previous versions        │
│     → Delete previous versions after 30/60/90 days       │
│     → Keeps recovery window while controlling costs      │
│                                                          │
│  3. Result: Data protection + Cost optimization          │
│     → Short-term recovery available                      │
│     → Long-term storage costs minimized                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Example Policy:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Rule 1: Delete previous versions older than 30 days     │
│  Rule 2: Archive previous versions older than 7 days     │
│          (then delete after 30 days total)               │
│                                                          │
│  This provides:                                          │
│  • 7-day recovery window (Standard tier)                 │
│  • 23-day extended recovery (Archive tier)               │
│  • Automatic cleanup after 30 days                       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Versioning Management:**

**Cannot Disable Versioning:**

- Once enabled, versioning cannot be fully disabled

- Can only suspend versioning (pauses new version creation)

- Existing versions remain even when suspended

**Suspending Versioning:**

- Changes status from Enabled to Suspended

- Stops creating new versions for uploads/overwrites

- Preserves all existing versions created before suspension

- Can re-enable at any time

**Re-enabling Versioning:**

- Changes status from Suspended back to Enabled

- Resumes creating new versions for all operations

- Previously retained versions remain intact

**Use Cases:**

**1. User-Generated Content Applications:**

- Users upload profile pictures, documents, media files

- Users might accidentally delete or overwrite files

- Versioning allows recovery without backup infrastructure

**2. Compliance and Audit:**

- Maintain complete history of document changes

- Audit trail for who changed what and when (using version identifiers)

- Meet regulatory requirements for data retention

**3. Application Development and Testing:**

- Developers can experiment with changes

- Easy rollback to previous working version

- No fear of permanent data loss during testing

**4. Configuration File Management:**

- Store application configuration files with version history

- Quickly revert to previous configuration if issues arise

- Track changes over time

**Key Takeaways:**

- Object versioning automatically creates versions for uploads, overwrites, and deletions

- Three statuses: Disabled (default, no protection), Enabled (full protection), Suspended (preserves old versions, pauses new)

- Cannot disable once enabled (only suspend)

- Always one latest version + zero or more previous versions

- Charged for storage of all versions

- Delete latest version creates delete marker (recoverable)

- Delete previous version is permanent

- Lifecycle policies can archive/delete latest and previous versions separately

- Replication does NOT replicate previous versions

- Cannot enable versioning on destination bucket or bucket with active retention rules

- Combine with lifecycle policies to balance protection and cost

- Ideal for protecting against accidental deletion, overwrites, and malicious actions

================================================================================

**Block Volume Overview:**

**OCI Storage Services — Block Storage Summary:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              OCI Block Storage Options                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────────┐
│   Local NVMe                 │        │   Block Volume               │
│                              │        │                              │
│  • Non-persistent storage    │        │  • Persistent, durable       │
│  • Survives reboots          │        │  • 99.99% (4 nines) annual   │
│  • Does NOT survive          │        │    durability                │
│    termination               │        │  • Independent of compute    │
│  • Extremely low latency     │        │    instance lifecycle        │
│  • High performance          │        │  • SAN-like features         │
│  • Locally attached to       │        │  • Attach/detach as needed   │
│    instance                  │        │  • 50 GB - 32 TB per volume  │
│  • Customer responsible      │        │  • Up to 32 volumes per      │
│    for data protection       │        │    instance                  │
└──────────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────────────┘

Local NVMe: Best for high-performance, low-latency workloads (Big Data, OLTP)
Block Volume: Best for persistent, durable storage independent of instances
```

**Block Volume Service Overview:**

- Provides persistent, durable, and high-performance block storage for OCI compute instances

- Data stored on Block Volumes exists independently of and beyond the lifespan of compute instances

- Use volumes like a regular hard drive once attached and connected to an instance

- Enables migration of data between instances without data loss

**Block Volume Durability:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Block Volume Durability Architecture                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Durability: 99.99% (4 nines) annual durability

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    Block Volume Data                     │
│                          │                               │
│         ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐              │
│         │                │                │              │
│    ┌────▼──────┐   ┌─────▼─────┐   ┌─────▼─────┐       │
│    │ Storage   │   │  Storage  │   │  Storage  │       │
│    │ Server 1  │   │ Server 2  │   │ Server 3  │       │
│    │  Copy 1   │   │  Copy 2   │   │  Copy 3   │       │
│    └───────────┘   └───────────┘   └───────────┘       │
│                                                          │
│   Automatically replicated across multiple storage       │
│   servers with built-in repair mechanisms                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Block Volume Specifications:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Block Volume Configuration Parameters                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Parameter                 │  Value                                  │
├────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Minimum Volume Size       │  50 GB                                  │
│  Maximum Volume Size       │  32 TB (32,768 GB)                      │
│  Size Increment            │  1 GB increments                        │
│  Default Volume Size       │  1 TB (1,024 GB)                        │
│  Max Volumes per Instance  │  32 volumes                             │
│  Durability                │  99.99% (4 nines) annual                │
│  Encryption at Rest        │  AES-256 (always enabled)               │
│  In-Transit Encryption     │  Optional (paravirtualized VM only)     │
└────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Block Volume Key Features:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Block Volume Features                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Persistence and Independence
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Data persists beyond instance lifecycle                 │
   │  • Survives instance termination                           │
   │  • Can attach to new instance after termination            │
   │  • Data safe even when not connected to any instance       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Flexibility
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Create, attach, connect, and move volumes as needed     │
   │  • Attach up to 32 volumes per instance                    │
   │  • Detach from one instance and attach to another          │
   │  • Resize volumes to meet changing storage requirements    │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Automatic Replication
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Automatically replicated across multiple storage servers│
   │  • Built-in repair mechanisms detect and fix corruption    │
   │  • No manual configuration required for redundancy         │
   │  • Protects against data loss from hardware failure        │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. Always-On Encryption at Rest
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • ALL Block Volumes encrypted at rest by default          │
   │  • ALL Boot Volumes encrypted at rest by default           │
   │  • ALL Volume Backups encrypted at rest by default         │
   │  • Uses AES (Advanced Encryption Standard) 256-bit         │
   │  • Cannot disable encryption at rest                       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5. Optional In-Transit Encryption
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Available for paravirtualized volume attachments        │
   │  • Encrypts data in transit between instance and volume    │
   │  • Virtual machine instances only                          │
   │  • Provides additional layer of security                   │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Block Volume Encryption Details:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Block Volume Encryption Architecture                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

At-Rest Encryption (Always Enabled):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Block Volumes  ──[AES-256 Encryption]──▶ Storage        │
│  Boot Volumes   ──[AES-256 Encryption]──▶ Storage        │
│  Volume Backups ──[AES-256 Encryption]──▶ Storage        │
│                                                          │
│  • Automatic, always-on                                  │
│  • No performance impact                                 │
│  • Cannot disable                                        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

In-Transit Encryption (Optional - VM Only):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  VM Instance ──[Encrypted Channel]──▶ Block Volume       │
│                                                          │
│  • Paravirtualized attachments only                      │
│  • Must explicitly enable                                │
│  • Protects data moving between instance and volume      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Block Volume Use Cases:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Block Volume Use Cases                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Persistent Storage
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Need data to survive instance termination               │
   │  • Migrate data between instances                          │
   │  • Store data that is not always connected to instance     │
   │                                                            │
   │  Solution:                                                 │
   │  • Attach Block Volume to instance for working storage     │
   │  • Detach when instance terminated                         │
   │  • Reattach to new instance - data intact                  │
   │                                                            │
   │  Key Benefit: Data safely stored even without connection   │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Expanding Compute Instance Storage
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Running out of storage on compute instance              │
   │  • Need more capacity without terminating instance         │
   │  • Add storage for specific application data               │
   │                                                            │
   │  Solution:                                                 │
   │  • Create new Block Volume                                 │
   │  • Attach to existing running instance                     │
   │  • Immediately available as additional storage             │
   │  • Can attach up to 32 volumes per instance                │
   │                                                            │
   │  Key Benefit: Expand storage dynamically without downtime  │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Extend Scaling (Boot Volume Use)
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Scenario:                                                 │
   │  • Need to change instance shape/type (scale up/down)      │
   │  • Keep data and OS configuration from old instance        │
   │  • Change number of OCPUs for different workload           │
   │                                                            │
   │  Solution:                                                 │
   │  • Terminate current instance                              │
   │  • Preserve boot volume during termination                 │
   │  • Launch new instance with different shape                │
   │  • Attach preserved boot volume                            │
   │                                                            │
   │  Key Benefit: Scale up or down by reusing boot volume      │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Block Volume vs Local NVMe Comparison:**

```
┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
│  Attribute                     │   Block Volume     │   Local NVMe       │
├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
│  Persistence                   │  Yes               │  No (non-persistent│
│                                │                    │  but survives reboot│
│  Durability                    │  99.99% (4 nines)  │  Not guaranteed    │
│  Performance                   │  High              │  Extremely high    │
│  Latency                       │  Low               │  Extremely low     │
│  Survives Reboots              │  Yes               │  Yes               │
│  Survives Termination          │  Yes               │  No                │
│  Location                      │  Network-attached  │  Locally attached  │
│  Data Protection               │  Oracle managed    │  Customer managed  │
│  OCI Backup/RAID               │  Yes (backups)     │  No                │
│  Attach/Detach Flexibility     │  Yes               │  No                │
│  Encryption at Rest            │  Always (AES-256)  │  N/A               │
│  Typical Use Case              │  Persistent data   │  Big Data, OLTP    │
└────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────┘
```

**Local NVMe SSD Details:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Local NVMe SSD Storage                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Overview:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Included with specific compute shapes (DenseIO)        │
│  • Example: BM.DenseIO2.52 includes locally attached NVMe│
│  • Extremely low latency and high-performance             │
│  • Ideal for: Big Data, OLTP, high-performance workloads  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Data Protection Responsibility:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ⚠️  Oracle does NOT provide:                              │
│  • Images or backups of NVMe data                        │
│  • RAID protection for NVMe devices                      │
│  • Any other data protection mechanisms                  │
│                                                          │
│  Customer is SOLELY responsible for:                     │
│  • Protecting NVMe device data                           │
│  • Managing durability of data on NVMe devices           │
│  • Implementing own backup/RAID solutions if needed      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Identifying NVMe Devices (Linux Command):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Command: lsblk                                          │
│                                                          │
│  Sample output (BM.DenseIO2.52 shape):                   │
│  NAME        MAJ:MIN RM   SIZE RO TYPE MOUNTPOINT        │
│  nvme0n1     259:0    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│  nvme1n1     259:1    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│  nvme2n1     259:2    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│  nvme3n1     259:3    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│  nvme4n1     259:4    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│  nvme5n1     259:5    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│  nvme6n1     259:6    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│  nvme7n1     259:7    0   6.4T  0 disk                   │
│                                                          │
│  BM.DenseIO2.52 = 8 NVMe drives (nvme0 to nvme7)        │
│  Number of drives depends on instance shape              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Summary:**

- Block Volume provides persistent, durable (99.99%), high-performance network-attached block storage

- Stores data independently of compute instance lifecycle (persists after termination)

- Create, attach, connect, and move volumes as needed like a regular hard drive

- Size range: 50 GB to 32 TB in 1 GB increments; default size 1 TB

- Up to 32 Block Volumes can be attached per compute instance

- Automatically replicated across multiple storage servers with built-in repair

- ALL Block Volumes, Boot Volumes, and Volume Backups always encrypted with AES-256 at rest

- Optional in-transit encryption available for paravirtualized VM attachments

- Three main use cases: persistent storage, expanding instance storage, extend scaling via boot volume

- Local NVMe: non-persistent (survives reboot, not termination), extremely low latency, customer manages data protection

- Local NVMe comes with DenseIO shapes (e.g., BM.DenseIO2.52 has 8 NVMe drives nvme0–nvme7)

- Use `lsblk` command to view block devices including locally attached NVMe drives

================================================================================

**Block Volume Attachment Types:**

**Overview:**

- When attaching a Block Volume to a VM instance, two attachment types are available: iSCSI and Paravirtualized

- Choice of attachment type affects how the guest OS accesses the block volume and the maximum IOPS performance

**Attachment Type Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Block Volume Attachment Type Comparison                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

iSCSI Attachment:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                          │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐              │
│  │           VM Instance                  │              │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐   │              │
│  │  │   Guest Operating System        │   │              │
│  │  │   (Internal Storage Stack)      │   │              │
│  │  └──────────────┬──────────────────┘   │              │
│  │                 │                      │              │
│  │  ┌──────────────▼──────────────────┐   │              │
│  │  │   Network Hardware              │   │              │
│  │  │   Virtualization                │   │              │
│  │  └──────────────┬──────────────────┘   │              │
│  └─────────────────┼────────────────────── ┘             │
│                    │  (Hypervisor NOT involved)           │
│                    ▼                                      │
│             Block Volume (iSCSI)                         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Paravirtualized Attachment:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                          │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐              │
│  │           VM Instance                  │              │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐   │              │
│  │  │   Guest Operating System        │   │              │
│  │  │   (calls Hypervisor APIs)       │   │              │
│  │  └──────────────┬──────────────────┘   │              │
│  └─────────────────┼────────────────────── ┘             │
│                    │                                      │
│  ┌─────────────────▼──────────────────────┐              │
│  │         Hypervisor                     │              │
│  │  (Light virtualization layer)          │              │
│  └─────────────────┬──────────────────────┘              │
│                    │                                      │
│                    ▼                                      │
│             Block Volume                                 │
│          (Accessed as local device)                      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**iSCSI Attachment:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              iSCSI Attachment Details                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

How It Works:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Utilizes internal storage stack in guest OS             │
│  • Uses network hardware virtualization to access volumes  │
│  • Hypervisor is NOT involved in the attachment process    │
│  • Requires additional commands to configure access        │
│    before volume can be used                               │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Performance:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✓ Maximum IOPS performance for larger block volumes       │
│  ✓ Best choice when storage IOPS is critical               │
│  ✓ No virtualization overhead on I/O path                 │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Configuration:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Requires extra commands before volume is accessible     │
│  • More configuration steps than paravirtualized           │
│  • Must run iSCSI connect commands inside guest OS         │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Paravirtualized Attachment:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Paravirtualized Attachment Details                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

How It Works:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Light virtualization technique                          │
│  • Guest OS uses hypervisor APIs to access remote storage  │
│  • Volume accessed directly as if it were a local device   │
│  • More efficient than full virtualization                 │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Performance:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ⚠️  Reduced maximum IOPS for larger block volumes          │
│     compared to iSCSI attachments                          │
│  • Virtualization overhead reduces peak I/O performance    │
│  • Acceptable performance for most general workloads       │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Configuration:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✓ Simplified configuration process                        │
│  ✓ No extra commands needed before accessing volume        │
│  ✓ Volume immediately accessible after attach              │
│  ✓ Supports in-transit encryption (iSCSI does not)         │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Side-by-Side Comparison:**

```
┌────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
│  Attribute                     │      iSCSI           │  Paravirtualized     │
├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
│  Hypervisor Involvement        │  No                  │  Yes (APIs)          │
│  Virtualization Type           │  Network hardware    │  Light virtualization│
│  IOPS Performance              │  Higher (max IOPS)   │  Lower for large vols│
│  Configuration Complexity      │  More complex        │  Simplified          │
│  Extra Setup Commands          │  Yes (required)      │  No                  │
│  Volume Access Type            │  iSCSI protocol      │  Local device-like   │
│  In-Transit Encryption         │  No                  │  Yes (optional)      │
│  Best For                      │  IOPS-critical       │  General purpose     │
│                                │  workloads           │  easy setup          │
└────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘
```

**When to Use Each Attachment Type:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Attachment Type Decision Guide                         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Use iSCSI When:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✓ Storage IOPS performance is of paramount importance     │
│  ✓ Running high-performance databases                      │
│  ✓ Running OLTP workloads needing maximum throughput       │
│  ✓ Working with large block volumes requiring max IOPS     │
│  ✓ Willing to perform extra configuration steps            │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Use Paravirtualized When:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✓ Simplicity and ease of setup is preferred               │
│  ✓ Workload does not require maximum IOPS                  │
│  ✓ In-transit encryption is required                       │
│  ✓ General-purpose storage access is sufficient            │
│  ✓ Want volume to appear as local device                   │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Two attachment types for Block Volumes on VM instances: iSCSI and Paravirtualized

- iSCSI uses internal OS storage stack and network hardware virtualization; hypervisor NOT involved

- Paravirtualized uses hypervisor APIs for light virtualization; volume appears as local device

- Paravirtualized is simpler (no extra commands) but has lower max IOPS on large volumes due to virtualization overhead

- iSCSI delivers maximum IOPS performance — choose for IOPS-critical workloads

- Paravirtualized supports optional in-transit encryption; iSCSI does not

- Both types provide full AES-256 encryption at rest

================================================================================

**Block Volume Access Types:**

**Overview:**

- OCI Block Volume service allows multiple instances to connect to the same volume

- Three access types control how a volume can be shared and accessed

- Access type is configured at attachment time

**Three Access Types:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Block Volume Access Types                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Read/Write (Default)                                             │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  Instance A ──[Read/Write]──▶ Block Volume                  │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  • Volume attached as read/write                            │   │
│  │  • NOT shareable with other instances                       │   │
│  │  • Single instance attachment only                          │   │
│  │  • Default configuration                                    │   │
│  │  • Exclusive access - one instance at a time                │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  2. Read/Write Shareable                                             │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  Instance A ──[Read/Write]──┐                               │   │
│  │                             ├──▶ Block Volume               │   │
│  │  Instance B ──[Read/Write]──┤                               │   │
│  │                             │                               │   │
│  │  Instance C ──[Read/Write]──┘  (up to 8 instances)         │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  • Volume attached as read/write                            │   │
│  │  • Shareable with other instances                           │   │
│  │  • Multiple instances can read AND write simultaneously     │   │
│  │  • Maximum: 8 instances                                     │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  ⚠️  REQUIRED: Must configure clustered file system          │   │
│  │     (e.g., OCFS2) to prevent data corruption                │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  3. Read-Only Shareable                                              │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  Instance A ──[Read-Only]──┐                                │   │
│  │                            ├──▶ Block Volume                │   │
│  │  Instance B ──[Read-Only]──┤                                │   │
│  │                            │                                │   │
│  │  Instance C ──[Read-Only]──┘  (up to 8 instances)          │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  • Volume attached as read-only                             │   │
│  │  • Shareable with other instances                           │   │
│  │  • Multiple instances can READ simultaneously               │   │
│  │  • No writes allowed from any attached instance             │   │
│  │  • Maximum: 8 instances                                     │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  │  ✓ No clustered file system required (no writes)            │   │
│  │                                                              │   │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Access Types Comparison Table:**

```
┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────┐
│  Attribute              │   Read/Write     │  Read/Write       │  Read-Only       │
│                         │   (Default)      │  Shareable        │  Shareable       │
├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┤
│  Read Access            │  Yes             │  Yes              │  Yes             │
│  Write Access           │  Yes             │  Yes              │  No              │
│  Shareable              │  No              │  Yes              │  Yes             │
│  Max Instances          │  1               │  Up to 8          │  Up to 8         │
│  Default                │  Yes             │  No               │  No              │
│  Clustered FS Required  │  No              │  Yes (OCFS2)      │  No              │
│  Data Corruption Risk   │  None            │  Yes (if no FS)   │  None            │
│  Use Case               │  Standard        │  Shared storage   │  Shared read-    │
│                         │  single-instance │  cluster apps     │  only content    │
└─────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────┘
```

**Clustered File System Requirement:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Why Clustered File System Is Required                  │
│              for Read/Write Shareable Volumes                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Problem Without Clustered File System:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ⚠️  Block Volume service does NOT provide coordination   │
│     for concurrent write operations to shared volumes    │
│                                                          │
│  Without coordination:                                   │
│  Instance A writes data ──▶ Block Volume                 │
│  Instance B writes data ──▶ Block Volume (same time)     │
│                                                          │
│  Result: DATA CORRUPTION                                 │
│  • Writes can overwrite each other                       │
│  • File system metadata becomes inconsistent             │
│  • Data loss or corruption                               │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Solution — Clustered File System (e.g., OCFS2):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✓ Coordinates concurrent access across instances        │
│  ✓ Manages locks and write ordering                      │
│  ✓ Prevents data corruption from simultaneous writes     │
│  ✓ Must be installed and configured before use           │
│                                                          │
│  Steps Required:                                         │
│  1. Attach volume as Read/Write Shareable                │
│  2. Install clustered file system (e.g., OCFS2)          │
│     on all instances that will use the volume            │
│  3. Configure cluster membership between instances       │
│  4. Format volume with clustered file system             │
│  5. Mount volume on all instances                        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Use Cases:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Access Type Use Cases                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Read/Write (Default):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Standard single-instance workloads                    │
│  • Databases on a single VM                              │
│  • Application servers with dedicated storage            │
│  • Boot volumes (OS disk)                                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Read/Write Shareable:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Clustered database applications (Oracle RAC)          │
│  • High-availability clustered applications              │
│  • Shared storage between cluster nodes                  │
│  • Applications that require simultaneous writes from    │
│    multiple instances                                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Read-Only Shareable:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Distributing read-only reference data to many VMs     │
│  • Sharing configuration files or static content         │
│  • Distributing software packages to multiple instances  │
│  • Providing common read-only datasets for analytics     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Three access types: Read/Write (default), Read/Write Shareable, Read-Only Shareable

- Default Read/Write — single instance only, not shareable, exclusive access

- Read/Write Shareable and Read-Only Shareable — both support up to a maximum of 8 instances

- Read/Write Shareable requires a clustered file system (e.g., OCFS2) to prevent data corruption

- Block Volume service does NOT coordinate concurrent writes — clustered file system must handle this
