```
│  • Must first REMOVE the volume from the group           │
│  • Then delete the volume                                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Rule 3: Volume Group Membership
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Each volume can only belong to ONE volume group       │
│  • Cannot add same volume to multiple groups             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Benefits of Volume Groups:**

- **Crash Consistency:** All volumes in group backed up or cloned at exact same point in time — ensures application data is consistent across tiers

- **Simplified Management:** Manage backups for multiple related volumes as a single unit instead of individually

- **Scheduled Backups:** Apply backup policies to the entire group for automated, scheduled backups with configurable retention

- **Complete Restore:** Restore entire group of volumes from a single volume group backup

- **No Additional Cost:** Volume group feature provided at no extra charge

**Key Points:**

- Volume group can contain up to 32 volumes with a maximum combined size of 128 TB

- Supports both boot volumes and block volumes in the same group

- Creates point-in-time, crash-consistent backups and clones across all volumes simultaneously

- Each volume can only belong to one volume group at a time

- Deleting a volume group does NOT delete the individual volumes inside it

- To delete a volume that is in a group, must first remove it from the group

- Automated scheduled backups with retention policies supported for volume groups

- Can restore an entire group of volumes from a volume group backup

- Volume groups are available at no additional charge

================================================================================

**Cross Availability Domain (AD) Replication:**

**Overview:**

- Replication of block volumes, boot volumes, and volume groups to another Availability Domain within the same region

- Similar to cross-region replication but scoped within a single region across ADs

- Does not cause any downtime or performance impact on source volumes

- Replication is asynchronous

**Cross-AD vs Cross-Region Replication — Key Differences:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Cross-AD vs Cross-Region Replication                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
│  Attribute                   │  Cross-AD            │  Cross-Region        │
│                              │  Replication         │  Replication         │
├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
│  Scope                       │  Same region,        │  Different regions   │
│                              │  different ADs        │                      │
│  Supports Block Volumes      │  Yes                 │  Yes                 │
│  Supports Boot Volumes       │  Yes                 │  Yes                 │
│  Supports Volume Groups      │  Yes                 │  Yes (varies)        │
│  Network Costs               │  NO network costs    │  Yes (applicable     │
│                              │                      │  network costs)      │
│  Replica Storage Cost        │  Lower Cost pricing  │  Lower Cost pricing  │
│  Resize Restriction          │  Must disable first  │  Must disable first  │
│  Customer-Managed Keys       │  Not supported       │  Not supported       │
│  Activate to Create Volume   │  Yes (clones replica)│  Yes (clones replica)│
│  Downtime on Source          │  None                │  None                │
└──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

Key Differentiator: Cross-AD replication has NO network costs;
                    Cross-Region replication incurs network costs
```

**How Cross-AD Replication Works:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Cross-AD Replication Architecture                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Same Region (e.g., Ashburn):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                                      │
│  Availability Domain 1          Availability Domain 2               │
│  ┌───────────────────┐                ┌────────────────────┐        │
│  │   Block Volume    │──[Async Rep]──▶│  Volume Replica    │        │
│  │   (Source)        │                │  (Destination AD)  │        │
│  └───────────────────┘                └────────────────────┘        │
│                                                                      │
│  • Same region name (e.g., Ashburn → Ashburn)                       │
│  • Different Availability Domain selected as destination             │
│  • No cross-region network charges                                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Configuration Comparison:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Cross-Region:   Region A   ──▶  Region B                │
│                  (e.g., Phoenix → Ashburn)               │
│                                                          │
│  Cross-AD:       Region A, AD 1 ──▶ Region A, AD 2       │
│                  (e.g., Ashburn AD1 → Ashburn AD2)        │
│                  Same region, different AD               │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Enabling Cross-AD Replication:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Steps to Enable Cross-AD Replication                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Edit volume properties (or during creation)
Step 2: Enable Replication
Step 3: Select destination region → choose SAME region
         (e.g., Ashburn → Ashburn)
Step 4: Select a DIFFERENT Availability Domain as destination
         (e.g., AD 1 → AD 2)
Step 5: Provide a name for the volume replica
Step 6: Save — replication begins automatically
```

**Restrictions and Limitations:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Cross-AD Replication Restrictions                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Cannot Resize While Enabled
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Disable replication → replica deleted                     │
   │  Resize the volume                                         │
   │  Re-enable replication → starts from scratch               │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Customer-Managed Vault Keys Not Supported
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  ✗ Not supported for volumes encrypted with               │
   │    customer-managed Vault encryption keys                  │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Asynchronous Replication
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Not real-time — some lag between source and replica     │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Pricing:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Cross-AD Replication Costs                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Storage Cost:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Billed for replica storage in destination AD          │
│  • Billed at Lower Cost block storage pricing            │
│    (regardless of source volume's performance tier)      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Network Cost:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✓ NO network costs for cross-AD replication             │
│    (intra-region traffic, no egress charges)             │
│                                                          │
│  Compare: Cross-Region replication DOES incur            │
│           network costs for inter-region traffic         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Activating a Replica:**

- Same process as cross-region replication

- Activate the replica → activation clones the replica → new volume created in the destination AD

- Use for disaster recovery when source AD is unavailable

**Key Points:**

- Cross-AD replication replicates block volumes, boot volumes, and volume groups to a different AD in the same region

- Same region is selected but a different Availability Domain is chosen as the destination

- No network costs (unlike cross-region replication which does incur network costs)

- Replica billed at Lower Cost block storage pricing regardless of source volume's performance tier

- Cannot resize a volume with cross-AD replication enabled — must disable first (deletes replica), resize, then re-enable

- Not supported for volumes encrypted with customer-managed Vault keys

- No downtime or performance impact on source volumes

- To use replica data: activate the replica → creates new volume by cloning the replica

================================================================================

**File Storage Service Overview:**

**Overview:**

- Fully managed, elastic, enterprise-grade Network File System (NFS) service

- Supports NFS version 3.0 protocol

- Data replicated for durability within each Availability Domain

- Scales automatically from a single byte to up to 8 exabytes — no upfront provisioning required

- Well-suited for applications requiring a shared file system across multiple instances

**Key Characteristics:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage Service Key Features                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Protocol Support                                                    │
│  • NFS version 3.0 (Network File System)                             │
│  • NLM (Network Lock Manager) for file locking functionality         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Scalability                                                         │
│  • Scales automatically — no manual provisioning needed              │
│  • Storage provisioning fully managed and automatic                  │
│  • Grows from 1 byte to 8 exabytes without interruption              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Durability                                                          │
│  • Data replicated within each Availability Domain                   │
│  • Snapshot support for data protection                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Encryption                                                          │
│  • All stored data and snapshot data encrypted at rest by default    │
│  • AES-256 encryption algorithm                                      │
│  • Default: Oracle-managed encryption keys                           │
│  • Option: Customer-managed keys via OCI Vault                       │
│  • In-transit encryption available (TLS between instances and FS)   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Access Methods:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage Access Options                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

From Within VCN:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Bare Metal instances                                  │
│  • Virtual Machine instances                             │
│  • Container instances                                   │
│  → All can connect directly from within the VCN          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

From Outside VCN:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • VCN Peering (local or remote peering)                 │
│  • OCI FastConnect (private dedicated connection)        │
│  • IPsec VPN connection                                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Access Architecture:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                           │
│  │  Bare    │  │   VM     │  │Container │  ← Within VCN             │
│  │  Metal   │  │ Instance │  │ Instance │                           │
│  └─────┬────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘                           │
│        └────────────┴─────────────┘                                  │
│                          │                                           │
│                          ▼                                           │
│                 ┌─────────────────┐                                  │
│                 │  File Storage   │                                  │
│                 │  Service (NFS)  │                                  │
│                 └─────────────────┘                                  │
│                          ▲                                           │
│        ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐                        │
│        │                 │                 │                        │
│  ┌─────┴──────┐  ┌───────┴────┐  ┌────────┴───┐                    │
│  │  VCN       │  │FastConnect │  │  IPsec VPN │ ← Outside VCN      │
│  │  Peering   │  │            │  │            │                    │
│  └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────┘                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Encryption Details:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage Encryption                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

At-Rest Encryption (Default, Always On):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • All file system data encrypted at rest                │
│  • All snapshot data encrypted at rest                   │
│  • AES-256 encryption algorithm                          │
│  • Cannot disable encryption at rest                     │
│                                                          │
│  Key Options:                                            │
│  Default: Oracle-managed encryption keys (automatic)    │
│  Custom:  Customer-managed keys via OCI Vault            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

In-Transit Encryption (Optional):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Secures data between instances and mounted file system│
│  • Uses TLS (Transport Layer Security) encryption        │
│  • Configured separately when mounting                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Use Cases:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage Use Cases                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Lift and Shift of Enterprise Applications
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Migrate Oracle applications requiring NFS storage       │
   │  • Example: Oracle E-Business Suite                        │
   │  • No code changes needed — same NFS interface             │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. General Purpose File Storage
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Unlimited pool of file systems                          │
   │  • Manage growth of structured and unstructured data       │
   │  • Shared storage across multiple compute instances        │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Analytics Workloads
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Run analytics workloads using shared file systems       │
   │  • Store persistent data accessible across instances       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. Scale-Out Applications
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Large compute clusters of thousands of instances        │
   │  • High-performance shared storage                         │
   │  • Horizontal scaling supported                            │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5. Test and Development
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Share common code, configuration, and data              │
   │  • Dev/test environments with shared file systems          │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

6. Containers (Stateful Persistent Storage)
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Deliver stateful persistent storage for containers      │
   │  • Scale as container-based environments grow              │
   │  • Persistent storage that survives container restarts     │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**File Storage vs Block Volume vs Object Storage:**

```
┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────┐
│  Attribute               │  File Storage     │  Block Volume     │  Object Storage  │
├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┤
│  Protocol                │  NFS v3           │  iSCSI / Paravirt │  HTTP/REST       │
│  Access Type             │  Shared (multiple │  Exclusive or     │  Any (URL)       │
│                          │  instances)       │  limited sharing  │                  │
│  Use Case                │  Shared file      │  OS/data disks    │  Unstructured    │
│                          │  system           │  per instance     │  data, archival  │
│  Max Scale               │  8 exabytes       │  32 TB per volume │  Unlimited       │
│  File Locking            │  Yes (NLM)        │  N/A              │  N/A             │
│  Encryption at Rest      │  Yes (AES-256)    │  Yes (AES-256)    │  Yes             │
│  In-Transit Encryption   │  Optional (TLS)   │  Optional (Parav) │  TLS default     │
└──────────────────────────┴───────────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- File Storage is a fully managed elastic NFS service supporting NFS version 3.0

- Scales automatically from 1 byte to 8 exabytes — no upfront provisioning

- Data replicated within each Availability Domain for durability

- Supports NLM (Network Lock Manager) for file locking functionality

- Accessible from bare metal, VM, and container instances within VCN

- Access from outside VCN via VCN peering, FastConnect, or IPsec VPN

- All data and snapshots encrypted at rest with AES-256 by default

- Default encryption uses Oracle-managed keys; option to use customer-managed keys via OCI Vault

- Optional in-transit encryption using TLS between instances and mounted file system

- Supports snapshots for data protection

- Use cases: enterprise app lift-and-shift, analytics, scale-out apps, dev/test, containers

================================================================================

**File Storage Concepts:**

**Three Core Constructs:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage Three Core Constructs                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. File System                                                      │
│     • The actual storage where files exist                           │
│     • Holds all file data                                            │
│     • Made accessible to NFS clients through exports                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  2. Mount Target                                                     │
│     • NFS endpoint that lives in a subnet                            │
│     • Highly available                                               │
│     • Provides IP address or DNS name used in mount commands         │
│     • NFS clients connect to file systems via the mount target       │
│     • One mount target can serve multiple file systems               │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  3. Export                                                           │
│     • The NFS control layer                                          │
│     • Controls how NFS clients access file systems                   │
│       when connecting to a mount target                              │
│     • Links a file system to a mount target                          │
│     • A file system must have at least one export in one             │
│       mount target for instances to be able to mount it              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Relationship Diagram:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage Component Relationships                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  NFS Client (Instance)
         │
         │  mount command using IP/DNS of Mount Target
         ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Mount Target (NFS endpoint in subnet, highly available)    │
  │                                                             │
  │  Export Set (collection of exports)                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────┐          │
  │  │  Export 1           │  │  Export 2           │          │
  │  │  Path: /finance     │  │  Path: /hr          │          │
  │  │  Options: (access)  │  │  Options: (access)  │          │
  │  └──────────┬──────────┘  └──────────┬──────────┘          │
  └─────────────┼──────────────────────── ┼───────────────────── ┘
                │                         │
                ▼                         ▼
  ┌─────────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────┐
  │  File System 1      │   │  File System 2      │
  │  (Finance data)     │   │  (HR data)          │
  └─────────────────────┘   └─────────────────────┘

One Mount Target → Multiple Exports → Multiple File Systems
```

**Mount Target Details:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Mount Target                                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • NFS endpoint residing in a VCN subnet                 │
│  • Highly available                                      │
│  • Provides IP address OR DNS name for mount commands    │
│  • Single mount target can serve MULTIPLE file systems   │
│    (via separate exports for each file system)           │
│  • File systems are exported/made available through      │
│    mount targets                                         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Export Concepts:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Export Components                                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Export Set:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Collection of one or more exports                     │
│  • Each mount target has one export set                  │
│  • Each file system associated with the mount target     │
│    has at least one export in the export set             │
│  • Can create as many exports as needed for different    │
│    file systems                                          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Export Path:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Path specified when an export is created              │
│  • Uniquely identifies the file system within the        │
│    mount target                                          │
│  • Allows many file systems to share a single mount      │
│    target (distinguished by path)                        │
│  • Example: /finance, /hr, /engineering                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Export Options:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Set of parameters specifying access level granted     │
│    to NFS clients when connecting to a mount target      │
│  • Controls read/write permissions, client restrictions  │
│  • Up to 100 export options per export                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Role Summary:**

```
┌───────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Construct        │  Role                                            │
├───────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  File System      │  Storage layer — where files actually live       │
│  Export           │  NFS control layer — controls client access      │
│  Mount Target     │  NFS endpoint — how clients connect (IP/DNS)     │
└───────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Steps to Mount a File System:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File System Mount Steps                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Open Required Ports
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Open required ports in Security List or NSG           │
│  • NFS uses ports: 111 (portmapper), 2048-2050 (NFS)     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Step 2: Launch OCI Instance
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Create the compute instance that will mount the FS    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Step 3: SSH Into Instance
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Connect to instance via SSH                           │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Step 4: Install NFS Client
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Linux (Oracle Linux/CentOS):                            │
│  sudo yum install nfs-utils                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Step 5: Create Mount Point Directory
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  sudo mkdir -p /mnt/myfilesystem                         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Step 6: Get Mount Command from OCI Console
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Check Mount Target details in OCI Console             │
│  • Copy the mount command provided                       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ↓
Step 7: Run Mount Command
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Example:                                                │
│  sudo mount -t nfs <mount-target-IP>:/<export-path>      │
│       /mnt/myfilesystem                                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Three constructs: File System (storage), Export (NFS control layer), Mount Target (NFS endpoint)

- Mount Target is an NFS endpoint in a subnet — highly available — provides IP/DNS for mount commands

- One mount target can serve multiple file systems via separate exports

- A file system must have at least one export in one mount target before instances can mount it

- Export Set is the collection of exports associated with a mount target

- Export Path uniquely identifies a file system within a mount target (allows multiple FSes per mount target)

- Export Options control the level of access granted to NFS clients — up to 100 options per export

- Mount steps: open ports → launch instance → SSH in → install NFS client → create mount point → get mount command from console → run mount command

================================================================================

**NFS Export Options:**

**Overview:**

- NFS Export Options enable granular access control to file systems

- Specify access levels for specific IP addresses or CIDR blocks connecting through a mount target

- Can restrict access so each client's file system is inaccessible and invisible to other clients

- Applied per export — controls access at the export level

**Four Export Option Parameters:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              NFS Export Option Parameters                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Source
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • IP address OR CIDR block of the connecting NFS client   │
   │  • Defines which clients this option entry applies to      │
   │  • Example: 10.0.0.8 (single IP) or 10.0.0.0/24 (subnet) │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Ports
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Privileged: Only root user can open privileged ports    │
   │  • Any: Connections from any port allowed                  │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Access
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Read-Only: Client can only read files                   │
   │  • Read-Write: Client can read and write files             │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. Squash
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  Controls whether client user ID (UID) and group ID (GID)  │
   │  are remapped to anonymous UID/GID                         │
   │                                                            │
   │  • All:  ALL users and groups are remapped to anonymous    │
   │  • Root: Only root user UID/GID remapped to anonymous      │
   │  • None: No users are remapped (original UID/GID retained) │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Access Control Example:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              NFS Export Options — Access Control Scenario           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Setup:
  File System A     File System B
  Client X (subnet: 10.0.0.0/24)   → Read-Write to FS-A, NO access to FS-B
  Client Y (subnet: 10.0.1.0/24)   → Read-Only to FS-B, NO access to FS-A

Solution:

Export Options for File System A:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Source: 10.0.0.0/24    (Client X subnet)               │
│  Access: Read-Write                                      │
│                                                          │
│  Note: Client Y's CIDR (10.0.1.0/24) NOT listed         │
│        → Client Y has NO access to File System A         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Export Options for File System B:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Source: 10.0.1.0/24    (Client Y subnet)               │
│  Access: Read-Only                                       │
│                                                          │
│  Note: Client X's CIDR (10.0.0.0/24) NOT listed         │
│        → Client X has NO access to File System B         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Result:
  Client X ──[Read-Write]──▶ File System A
  Client X ──[NO ACCESS] ──✗ File System B
  Client Y ──[Read-Only] ──▶ File System B
  Client Y ──[NO ACCESS] ──✗ File System A
```

**Denying Access Completely:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              How to Completely Deny Client Access                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

To completely deny a client access to a file system:

  ✓ Ensure the client's IP address or CIDR block is NOT included
    in ANY export for ANY mount target associated with that file system

  If a client's IP is not listed in any export → client has NO access
  If a client's IP matches any export → client gets that export's access

Example:
  File System A has exports for 10.0.0.0/24 only
  → Client at 10.0.1.5 has NO access (IP not in any export)
  → Client at 10.0.0.50 has access per the matching export entry
```

**First-Match Rule (CRITICAL):**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Export Options First-Match Evaluation                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Rule:
  File Storage service applies the FIRST set of options that
  matches the client source IP address.
  Once the first match is applied, ALL remaining entries are IGNORED.

Example — Order Matters:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Entry 1: Source = 10.0.0.0/16   Access = Read-Only      │
│  Entry 2: Source = 10.0.0.8      Access = Read-Write     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Client connecting from IP 10.0.0.8:
  • IP 10.0.0.8 IS contained in 10.0.0.0/16 → Entry 1 MATCHES first
  • Entry 1 applied → Read-Only access granted
  • Entry 2 NEVER evaluated (ignored after first match)
  • Result: Client gets Read-Only (NOT Read-Write as Entry 2 would give)

⚠️  More specific rules must be placed BEFORE more general rules
    to take effect — order of export option entries is critical
```

**Export Options Configuration Table:**

```
┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Parameter       │  Options / Values                                 │
├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Source          │  IP address (e.g., 10.0.0.8)                      │
│                  │  CIDR block (e.g., 10.0.0.0/24)                   │
├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Ports           │  Privileged (root only)                           │
│                  │  Any                                              │
├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Access          │  Read-Only                                        │
│                  │  Read-Write                                       │
├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Squash          │  All  (all users/groups remapped to anonymous)    │
│                  │  Root (only root UID/GID remapped)                │
│                  │  None (no remapping)                              │
└──────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Maximum: Up to 100 export options per export
```

**Key Points:**

- NFS Export Options provide granular access control per IP address or CIDR block

- Four parameters: Source (IP/CIDR), Ports (privileged/any), Access (read-only/read-write), Squash (all/root/none)

- Squash controls whether client UIDs/GIDs are remapped to anonymous UID/GID

- To completely deny access: ensure client IP/CIDR is not included in any export for the file system

- First-match rule: service applies the first matching export option entry and ignores the rest

- More specific rules (single IP) must be placed before more general rules (CIDR blocks) to take effect

- Order of export option entries is critical — determines which rule applies

- Up to 100 export options per export

================================================================================

**File System Paths:**

**Overview:**

- File Storage service uses three kinds of paths: Export Paths, Mount Point Paths, and File System Paths

- Each path type serves a different purpose and exists at a different layer

**Three Path Types:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage — Three Path Types                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Layer:           Mount Target         Client Instance       File System
                     │                      │                    │
Path Type:     Export Path          Mount Point Path      File System Path
                     │                      │                    │
Purpose:     Identifies FS         Local directory        Paths within
             within mount          where FS is            the file system
             target                mounted                itself
```

**1. Export Path:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Export Path                                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Uniquely identifies a file system within a mount      │
│    target                                                │
│  • Used by an instance to mount/logically attach to the  │
│    file system                                           │
│  • Unrelated to any path within the file system itself   │
│    or within the client instance                         │
│  • Up to 100 file systems can be associated behind a     │
│    single mount target (each with its own export path)   │
│  • Cannot be edited after the export is created          │
│                                                          │
│  Example:                                                │
│  /FileSystem-CIFSexport                                  │
│  → uniquely identifies this file system within           │
│    the mount target                                      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**2. Mount Point Path:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Mount Point Path                                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Path within the CLIENT INSTANCE                       │
│  • Points to a locally accessible directory on the       │
│    instance where the remote file system is mounted      │
│  • Created manually on the client before mounting        │
│                                                          │
│  Example mount command:                                  │
│  sudo mount <IP>:/export-path /mnt/mountpointA           │
│                                                          │
│  Here: /mnt/mountpointA is the mount point path          │
│        (directory on client instance)                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**3. File System Path:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File System Path                                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Paths to directories WITHIN the file system           │
│  • Contain the contents of the file system               │
│  • Created inside the mounted file system                │
│  • Any directory structure can be created after mounting │
│                                                          │
│  Special Use — Snapshot Access:                          │
│  Snapshots accessible via file system path:              │
│  <mount-point>/.snapshot/<snapshot-name>                 │
│                                                          │
│  Example:                                                │
│  Snapshot name: April-22                                 │
│  Access path:   /mnt/mountpointA/.snapshot/April-22      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Three Path Types Summary:**

```
┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
│  Path Type           │  Location                │  Purpose / Example               │
├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
│  Export Path         │  Mount Target level      │  Uniquely identifies FS within   │
│                      │                          │  mount target                    │
│                      │                          │  e.g. /FileSystem-CIFSexport     │
│                      │                          │  Cannot be edited after creation │
├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
│  Mount Point Path    │  Client Instance level   │  Local directory on client where │
│                      │                          │  remote FS is mounted            │
│                      │                          │  e.g. /mnt/mountpointA           │
├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
│  File System Path    │  Inside the File System  │  Directories and files within    │
│                      │                          │  the mounted file system         │
│                      │                          │  e.g. /mnt/mountpointA/data/     │
│                      │                          │  Snapshot: /.snapshot/April-22   │
└──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Snapshot Access via File System Path:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Accessing Snapshots Using File System Paths            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Snapshot stored under file system root at:
  <mount-point>/.snapshot/<snapshot-name>

Example:
  Mount Point:    /mnt/mountpointA
  Snapshot Name:  April-22
  Access Path:    /mnt/mountpointA/.snapshot/April-22

  → Browse snapshot contents like any directory
  → Useful for file-level recovery from snapshots
```

**Key Points:**

- Three path types: Export Path (mount target level), Mount Point Path (client instance level), File System Path (inside the file system)

- Export Path uniquely identifies a file system within a mount target — up to 100 file systems per mount target

- Export Path cannot be edited after the export is created

- Export Path is unrelated to any path within the file system or on the client instance

- Mount Point Path is a local directory on the client instance where the remote file system is mounted

- File System Path refers to directories and files inside the mounted file system

- Snapshots are accessible via file system path at: `<mount-point>/.snapshot/<snapshot-name>`

================================================================================

**File System Snapshots:**

**Overview:**

- Snapshots provide data protection for file systems

- Consistent, point-in-time view of a file system's data

- Accessible directly from within the mounted file system via the `.snapshot` directory

- Created and managed via OCI Console, CLI, or API

**Snapshot Access Path:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Accessing Snapshots                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Access path:
  <mount-point>/.snapshot/<snapshot-name>

Example:
  Mount Point:    /mnt/mountpointA
  Snapshot Name:  22AprilSnapshot
  Access Path:    /mnt/mountpointA/.snapshot/22AprilSnapshot

  → Navigate into .snapshot directory from within the mount point
  → Each snapshot appears as a subdirectory named after the snapshot
  → Browse and recover files directly from the snapshot directory
```

**Snapshot Storage Behavior:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Snapshot Storage — How It Works                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Initial Snapshot State:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Snapshot initially consumes NO additional storage     │
│  • References the original data instead of duplicating   │
│  • No copy of data made at snapshot creation time        │
│  • Limits usage cost at time of creation                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Storage Grows Only with Changes:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Data usage metered against DIFFERENTIATED snapshot    │
│    data (only the changed/new blocks)                    │
│  • If nothing has changed in the file system since the   │
│    last snapshot, a new snapshot consumes NO storage     │
│  • Storage consumed = only the data that differs from    │
│    the previous state                                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Example:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  File System: 100 GB of data                             │
│  Snapshot 1 created → 0 GB additional storage used      │
│                                                          │
│  10 GB of files changed/added after Snapshot 1          │
│  Snapshot 2 created → ~10 GB additional storage used    │
│  (only the differentiated/changed data is stored)       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Snapshots are consistent, point-in-time views of the file system

- Accessible under the root directory of the file system at `.snapshot/<snapshot-name>`

- Initially consume no additional storage — reference original data rather than duplicating it

- Storage is metered only against differentiated (changed) snapshot data

- If no data has changed since the last snapshot, a new snapshot uses no additional storage

- Useful for file-level recovery — browse and restore individual files from within the snapshot directory

================================================================================

**File System Cloning:**

**Overview:**

- A clone is a new file system created based on a snapshot of an existing file system

- At the point of creation, the clone's data is identical to the snapshot it was created from

- Clone does NOT replicate or move data — it references the parent file system for shared data

- After creation, changes in the clone are independent from the original file system, and vice versa

**How Cloning Works:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File System Clone Creation                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Take snapshot of original file system
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Original File System (e.g., ociarcassfilesystem)        │
│  Data: [A] [B] [C] [D]                                   │
│           │                                              │
│           ▼ (take snapshot)                              │
│  Snapshot: 22AprilSnapshot                               │
│  (point-in-time reference to A, B, C, D)                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 2: Create clone from snapshot
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Clone (e.g., clonedfilesystem)                          │
│  Created from: 22AprilSnapshot                           │
│  Data: references [A] [B] [C] [D] in parent             │
│        (no data physically copied yet)                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 3: After creation — independent changes
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Original File System changes [A] → [A']                 │
│  Clone still sees [A] (not affected by parent change)    │
│                                                          │
│  Clone adds new file [E]                                 │
│  Original File System does NOT see [E]                   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Result: Completely independent after creation point
```

**Key Cloning Concepts:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File System Cloning Terminology                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Source Snapshot
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • The snapshot used as blueprint to create the clone      │
   │  • Point-in-time reference of the file system              │
   │  • Clone's initial state matches this snapshot exactly     │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Parent File System
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • The file system that contains data referenced by        │
   │    one or many clones                                      │
   │  • Clone continues to reference parent for any data        │
   │    they share in common                                    │
   │  • Does NOT need to remain unchanged after cloning         │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Hydration
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Indicates whether the clone is currently copying        │
   │    metadata from the source                                │
   │  • Clone is USABLE while hydration is in progress          │
   │  • Background process — no need to wait for completion     │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. Clone Tree
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • A group of clones that descend from the same root       │
   │    file system                                             │
   │  • Multiple clones from the same parent form a tree        │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5. Clone Root
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Indicates whether a file system is the root of a        │
   │    cloned tree                                             │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

6. Descendants
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Indicates whether a file system has been cloned         │
   │  • True if the file system has clones derived from it      │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Clone vs Original — Data Independence:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Clone Independence After Creation                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Changes in CLONE → NOT reflected in original file system            │
│  Changes in ORIGINAL → NOT reflected in clone                        │
│                                                                      │
│  Both file systems are fully independent after clone creation        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Shared Data (via reference to parent):
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  • Clone references parent for data shared at creation   │
│  • No physical data copy at creation time                │
│  • Efficient storage — shared blocks not duplicated      │
│  • Similar to snapshot copy-on-write behavior            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Multiple Clones from Snapshots:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Cloning at Multiple Points in Time                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Original File System
       │
       ├── Snapshot (Jan 1)  →  Clone A  (state as of Jan 1)
       │
       ├── Snapshot (Feb 1)  →  Clone B  (state as of Feb 1)
       │
       └── Snapshot (Mar 1)  →  Clone C  (state as of Mar 1)

All three clones descend from the same parent → form a Clone Tree
Each clone reflects the file system at its respective snapshot point
```

**Use Cases:**

- **Testing and Development:** Clone production file system to a test environment without affecting production data

- **Point-in-Time Recovery:** Create clones from historical snapshots to access data as it existed at a past point in time

- **Parallel Processing:** Create multiple independent clones for parallel workloads using identical starting data

- **Environment Duplication:** Rapidly duplicate a file system environment for staging or QA

**Key Points:**

- Clone is created from a snapshot (source snapshot) of an existing file system

- At creation, clone data is identical to the snapshot it was based on

- Clone does NOT physically copy data — it references the parent file system for shared data

- After creation, clone and original are fully independent (changes in one do not affect the other)

- Hydration is the background process of copying metadata from source — clone is usable during hydration

- Clone Tree = group of clones descended from the same root file system

- Clone Root and Descendants fields indicate a file system's role within a clone tree

- Taking regular snapshots allows creating clones that represent the file system at multiple points in its lifetime

================================================================================

**File Storage In-Transit Encryption:**

**Overview:**

- In-transit encryption secures data between instances and mounted file systems using Transport Layer Security (TLS)

- Provides end-to-end security for data flowing between the NFS client and the mount target

- Requires opening a security list / firewall rule for TCP port 2051 (encrypted NFS access port)

- To enforce encrypted access only, disable the standard NFS access ports (TCP 111, 2048, 2049, 2050; UDP 111, 2048)

**How In-Transit Encryption Works:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              In-Transit Encryption Architecture                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Compute Instance        │
  │                                 │
  │  ┌───────────┐  ┌────────────┐  │
  │  │ NFS Client│→ │ OCI FSS    │  │
  │  │           │  │ Forwarder  │  │
  │  └───────────┘  │ (process)  │  │
  │                 └─────┬──────┘  │
  │  oci-fss-utils creates:         │
  │  • Network namespace            │
  │  • Virtual network interface    │
  │  • Local NFS endpoint           │
  └───────────────────┬─────────────┘
                      │
                      │  TLS Tunnel (TCP port 2051)
                      │  (encrypted traffic)
                      ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Mount Target            │
  │  (NFS endpoint in VCN subnet)   │
  └─────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Component — oci-fss-utils Package:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              oci-fss-utils: Oracle Cloud Infrastructure             │
│              File Storage Service Client Utility                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Install this package on your compute instance to enable in-transit encryption

What oci-fss-utils creates upon installation:
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Network namespace — isolated network environment      │
│  2. Virtual network interface — for local NFS endpoint   │
│  3. Local NFS endpoint — NFS client connects here        │
│  4. OCI FSS Forwarder — background process that:         │
│     • Receives requests from the NFS client              │
│     • Encrypts them using TLS                            │
│     • Forwards them to the mount target via TLS tunnel   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Setup Steps:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Steps to Enable In-Transit Encryption                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Open network rule for TCP port 2051
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Add ingress/egress rule for TCP port 2051 in    │
  │  security list or NSG associated with subnet     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
Step 2: Download and install oci-fss-utils package
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Install Oracle Cloud Infrastructure File        │
  │  Storage Service Client Utility on instance      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
Step 3: Mount file system using in-transit encryption command
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Use special mount command that initiates        │
  │  encryption (different from standard NFS mount)  │
  │  OCI FSS Forwarder handles encrypted forwarding  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
Step 4 (optional): Enforce encryption only
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Disable standard NFS ports to prevent           │
  │  unencrypted access (TCP 111, 2048, 2049, 2050;  │
  │  UDP 111, 2048)                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Request Flow After Mount:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Encrypted Request Flow                                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

NFS Client
    │
    │ (1) Sends NFS request to local NFS endpoint
    ▼
OCI FSS Forwarder (background process)
    │
    │ (2) Receives request from NFS client
    │ (3) Encrypts request using TLS
    ▼
TLS Tunnel → TCP port 2051
    │
    │ (4) Encrypted traffic sent to mount target
    ▼
Mount Target
    │
    │ (5) Decrypts and processes NFS request
    ▼
File System (data returned encrypted via same tunnel)
```

**Port Reference:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Port     │  Protocol  │  Purpose                                    │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2051     │  TCP       │  Encrypted NFS access (in-transit enc.)     │
│  111      │  TCP/UDP   │  NFS portmapper (standard, unencrypted)     │
│  2048     │  TCP/UDP   │  NFS mount daemon (standard, unencrypted)   │
│  2049     │  TCP       │  NFS server (standard, unencrypted)         │
│  2050     │  TCP       │  NFS lock manager (standard, unencrypted)   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- In-transit encryption uses TLS to secure data between compute instances and mounted file systems

- Prerequisite: open TCP port 2051 in security rules for encrypted access

- Install the `oci-fss-utils` package (Oracle Cloud Infrastructure File Storage Service Client Utility) on the instance

- The package creates a network namespace, virtual network interface, and local NFS endpoint upon installation

- The `OCI FSS Forwarder` is a background process that receives NFS requests, encrypts them, and sends them to the mount target via a TLS tunnel

- Mount the file system using a special in-transit encryption mount command (not the standard NFS mount command)

- To enforce encryption-only access, disable the standard NFS ports (TCP 111, 2048, 2049, 2050; UDP 111, 2048)

================================================================================

**File System Replication:**

**Overview:**

- File system replication asynchronously copies data updates from a source file system to a target file system

- Replication can be configured across multiple Availability Domains within a region or across different regions

- Source file system has an active role — it can be used normally during replication

- Target file system has a passive role — it receives replicated data

- Provides consistent file system replicas — applications can use the target file system with full confidence of consistency

- Replication does NOT rely on block-level replication — it uses file-level snapshot-based replication

- A source file system can be replicated to multiple target regions simultaneously

**Replication Architecture:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File System Replication Architecture                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Primary AD / Region                     Secondary AD / Region
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │  Compute Instances           │         │                            │
  │  (active workloads)          │         │                            │
  │         │                   │         │                            │
  │         ▼ (data updates)     │         │                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐   │         │  ┌─────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Source File System  │   │         │  │  Target File System │   │
  │  │  (exported, active)  │   │         │  │  (unexported,       │   │
  │  └──────────┬───────────┘   │         │  │   passive)          │   │
  │             │               │         │  └──────────▲──────────┘   │
  │             ▼               │         │             │              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐   │  TLS    │  ┌──────────┴──────────┐   │
  │  │  Replication         │───┼────────▶│  │  Replication Target │   │
  │  │  Resource            │   │  tunnel │  │  Resource           │   │
  │  │  (attached to source)│   │         │  │  (auto-created)     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┘   │         │  └─────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Replication Concepts:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Replication Terminology                                │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1. Source File System
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • File system whose data is replicated to another region  │
   │    or availability domain at regular intervals             │
   │  • Actively used — applications can read/write during rep. │
   │  • Can be exported and mounted by instances                │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

2. Target File System
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Destination for replicated data from source             │
   │  • MUST be a file system that has NEVER been exported      │
   │  • Passive role — receives replicated snapshots only       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3. Replication Resource
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • The control component of the replication process        │
   │  • Attached to the SOURCE file system                      │
   │  • Contains all configuration information for replication  │
   │  • Captures data updates by creating replication snapshots │
   │  • Transmits snapshots to the replication target resource  │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

4. Replication Target Resource
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Resides in the DESTINATION region or AD                 │
   │  • Receives replication snapshots from replication res.    │
   │  • Applies received data to the target file system         │
   │  • Automatically created when replication resource created │
   │  • Only manual operation allowed: DELETE (during failover) │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5. Replication Snapshot
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Captures INCREMENTAL changes in source file system      │
   │    since the last snapshot                                 │
   │  • Managed automatically by the service (not user)        │
   │  • Retained in both source and target until next interval  │
   │  • Old snapshot auto-deleted when new one is created       │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

6. Replication Interval
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  • Interval in minutes between replication cycles          │
   │  • Must be >= 60 minutes                                   │
   │  • Choose based on business RPO requirements               │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Base Copy vs Delta Copy:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Replication Data Transfer Types                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Base Copy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • The INITIAL full data transfer from source to target  │
  │  • Occurs once at the start of replication               │
  │  • Happens automatically — no user intervention needed   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼ (base copy complete)
  Delta Copy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Periodic incremental transfers after the base copy    │
  │  • System-driven snapshots taken on source at each       │
  │    replication interval                                  │
  │  • Only changed data transferred (incremental)           │
  │  • Frequency controlled by the replication interval      │
  │  • Happens automatically — no user intervention needed   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Timeline:
  T=0        T=60min    T=120min   T=180min
  │          │          │          │
  [Base Copy][Delta  1 ][Delta  2 ][Delta  3 ] ...
```

**Delta Cycle States:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Four States of a Delta Cycle                           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────┐     ┌────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
  │  IDLE    │────▶│ CAPTURING  │────▶│ TRANSFERRING │────▶│ APPLYING │
  └──────────┘     └────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────┘
       │                                                         │
       └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                         (cycle repeats at next interval)

  IDLE         Replication is not capturing or applying data
               (waiting for next interval)

  CAPTURING    Replication is capturing differentiated data in
               the source snapshot (reading changed blocks)

  TRANSFERRING Replication is both capturing and committing
               snapshot data (transfer in progress)

  APPLYING     Replication is committing snapshot data to the
               target file system (writing to target)
```

**Replication Process Step-by-Step:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Replication Process Flow                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Replication resource created on source site
         → Attached to the source file system

Step 2: Replication resource creates a replication snapshot
         → Captures state/incremental changes of source FS

Step 3: Replication snapshot transferred to replication target resource
         → Securely transferred over TLS to destination region/AD

Step 4: Replication target resource writes data to target file system
         → Target FS updated with snapshot data

Step 5: Snapshot retained in both source and target until next interval
         → Acts as reference for next incremental (delta) snapshot

Step 6: At the next interval:
         → Old replication snapshot auto-deleted
         → New replication snapshot created (delta of new changes)
         → Steps 3–5 repeat

Step 7: Process continues at every replication interval indefinitely
```

**Use Cases:**

- **Disaster Recovery:** Cross-region replication protects against regional outages; e.g., primary operations in Phoenix AD with recovery site in Ashburn AD

- **Data Redundancy Compliance:** Meet regulatory requirements for data copies in geographically separate locations

- **Cross-AD Resilience:** Replicate across availability domains within the same region for intra-region redundancy

- **Business Continuity:** Maintain an up-to-date copy of critical file data ready for failover at any time

**Key Points:**

- Replication is asynchronous — source FS remains fully usable during replication

- Source can be exported (active); target must have NEVER been exported (passive)

- The replication resource is attached to the source and manages the entire replication process

- The replication target resource is auto-created; only manual action allowed is DELETE (during failover)

- Replication snapshots are incremental — only changed data since the last snapshot is transferred

- Replication snapshots are service-managed (not user-managed)

- Replication interval must be >= 60 minutes; choose based on RPO requirements

- Initial transfer = Base Copy (full); subsequent transfers = Delta Copies (incremental)

- Delta cycle has four states: Idle → Capturing → Transferring → Applying

- A source file system can replicate to multiple target regions simultaneously

================================================================================

**File Storage Security:**

**Overview:**

- The File Storage Service uses four distinct layers of access control, each operating at a different level

- Layers work together to provide defense-in-depth for file system access

**The Four Layers of Access Control:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              File Storage — Four Security Layers                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Layer 1: IAM (Identity and Access Management)                   │
  │  Controls what OCI users/groups can DO within OCI                │
  │  → Create instances, VCNs, security rules, mount targets,        │
  │    file systems                                                  │
  │  → Enforced via IAM policies                                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Layer 2: Network Security (VCN Security Lists / NSGs)           │
  │  Controls which IP addresses / CIDR blocks can connect to FS     │
  │  → Uses VCN security list rules to allow/deny traffic to         │
  │    the mount target                                              │
  │  → Blocks/allows specific ports from specific IPs/CIDRs          │
  │  → ALL-OR-NOTHING basis: client can access ALL or NO file        │
  │    systems on the mount target                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Layer 3: NFS Export Options                                     │
  │  Access control at the file system export level                  │
  │  → Filters access based on source IP address                     │
  │  → Bridges between network security layer and NFS/Unix layer     │
  │  → More granular than network security (per-export control)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Layer 4: Unix Security                                          │
  │  Controls what users can do ON the instance                      │
  │  → Install applications, mount external file systems             │
  │  → Create directories, read and write files                      │
  │  → Enforced by OS-level permissions (users, groups, modes)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Layer Comparison:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Layer  │  Mechanism          │  Scope / Granularity                        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1 IAM  │  OCI IAM policies   │  OCI resource operations (create/delete)    │
│  2 Net  │  VCN security lists │  Mount target level — all-or-nothing        │
│         │  / NSGs             │  (IP/CIDR based)                            │
│  3 Exp  │  NFS export options │  Per-export / per-file system (IP-based)    │
│  4 Unix │  OS permissions     │  Per-user/group on the instance             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Layer Details:**

**Layer 1 — IAM:**
- Controls OCI-level operations using policies (e.g., `Allow group Admins to manage file-family in compartment X`)
- Governs: creating/deleting instances, VCNs, security rules, mount targets, file systems

**Layer 2 — Network Security:**
- Uses VCN security list rules or Network Security Groups (NSGs) to allow/deny traffic at the mount target
- Can block specific ports from specific IP addresses and CIDR blocks
- Important limitation: access is all-or-nothing at the mount target level — a client either can or cannot access the mount target, and therefore ALL file systems associated with it

**Layer 3 — NFS Export Options:**
- Applied per file system export, not per mount target
- Controls access based on source IP address of the connecting client
- Bridges the network security layer and the Unix security layer
- Provides more granular control than network security (individual export-level access)

**Layer 4 — Unix Security:**
- Standard operating system-level permissions on the instance
- Controls user actions such as: installing applications, mounting external file systems, creating directories, reading and writing files
- Enforced by file/directory permissions, user IDs (UID), and group IDs (GID)

**Key Points:**

- File Storage uses four security layers: IAM → Network Security → NFS Export Options → Unix Security

- Layer 1 (IAM) governs OCI resource-level operations via IAM policies

- Layer 2 (Network Security) uses VCN security lists/NSGs and is all-or-nothing at the mount target level — a client either accesses ALL or NO file systems on that mount target

- Layer 3 (NFS Export Options) provides per-export IP-based access control, bridging network and Unix security

- Layer 4 (Unix Security) governs what OS users can do on the instance itself

- Together, the four layers provide defense-in-depth for file system security

================================================================================

**File System Usage and Metering:**

**Overview:**

- The File Storage Service reports metered file system utilization and updates it hourly

- Metered utilization comes from the `meteredBytes` value in the API

- Adding or removing files can take up to one hour to reflect in the reported metered size

**Viewing Usage from the Instance:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              CLI Commands for File System Usage                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  df  (disk free)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Reports the amount of storage metered for the FS      │
  │  • Reports the SAME value as meteredBytes                │
  │  • Use df to accurately view metered file system size    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  du  (disk usage)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Reports storage used by a directory hierarchy         │
  │  • Can report a MUCH LARGER value than meteredBytes      │
  │  • Not equivalent to meteredBytes                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Rule: Use df to check metered size; du for directory-level usage
```

**Snapshot Metered Utilization:**

- Snapshots are point-in-time views of a file system

- Snapshots initially consume NO additional storage — they reference original data (no duplication)

- The metered size of a snapshot is INCLUDED in the `meteredBytes` value of the file system it belongs to

- Additional metered storage only accrues when data in the live file system diverges from the snapshot

**Snapshot Metering Example:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Snapshot Metering Walkthrough                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Create Testfilesystem + add file1 (1 GB including metadata)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Testfilesystem: file1 = 1 GB        │
  │  Metered bytes: 1 GB                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 2: Create a snapshot
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Snapshot references same data       │
  │  No differentiated data yet          │
  │  Metered bytes: still 1 GB           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 3: Overwrite first 0.5 GB of file1
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Live FS has new version of 0.5 GB   │
  │  Snapshot retains original 0.5 GB    │
  │  → Differentiated data = 0.5 GB      │
  │                                      │
  │  Metered bytes calculation:          │
  │  1 GB (snapshot) + 0.5 GB (delta)    │
  │  = 1.5 GB total metered bytes        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

Key insight: Differentiated (changed) data between the live file system
and the snapshot adds to the total metered bytes
```

**Clone Metered Utilization:**

- The initial cost of a file system clone is based on its **metadata only** (clones reference parent data, not duplicate it)

- **Parent file system** is metered for all data shared with descendant clones

- **Clone** is metered for: its metadata + all incremental changes made to its own data

**Clone Metering Example:**

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Clone Metering Walkthrough                             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Create testclone from Testfilesystem
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Testfilesystem (parent):                    │
  │    → Metered for its data + metadata         │
  │  testclone:                                  │
  │    → Metered for metadata ONLY               │
  │    (references parent data — no duplication) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 2: Create a 2 GB file inside testclone
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Testfilesystem (parent):                    │
  │    → Metered for data SHARED with testclone  │
  │  testclone:                                  │
  │    → Metered for metadata                    │
  │    + 2 GB of new/changed data                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key insight: Shared data is charged to the parent; only incremental
changes on the clone are charged to the clone itself
```

**Summary — What Gets Metered:**

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Entity            │  What is metered                                │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  File System       │  All data + metadata + snapshot differentiated  │
│                    │  data included in meteredBytes                  │
│  Snapshot          │  No additional cost initially; differentiated   │
│                    │  data adds to parent FS meteredBytes            │
│  Clone (initial)   │  Metadata only                                  │
│  Clone (after chg) │  Metadata + incremental changes on clone        │
│  Parent FS (clone) │  All data shared with descendant clones         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key Points:**

- Metered utilization comes from the `meteredBytes` API value and updates hourly (up to 1 hour lag)

- Use `df` to view metered file system size — it matches `meteredBytes` exactly

- Use `du` for directory-level usage, but it can report values much larger than `meteredBytes`

- Snapshots initially consume no additional storage (reference original data); differentiated data adds to metered bytes

- Snapshot metered size is included in the parent file system's `meteredBytes` value

- Clone initial cost = metadata only (references parent data, no duplication)

- Parent file system is metered for all data shared with its descendant clones

- Clone is metered for its own metadata plus any incremental data changes it introduces

================================================================================
